The home view of my MacOS machine would be a Space that has my normal communication tools open (Signal, GMail, etc) as well as a window for this “Portal” tool (that’s a placeholder name) that lets me quickly select existing projects or create a new one. There would also be a system-wide hotkey that opens a small Portal window in which I could jot notes or issue commands with a “/command” format. Notes go into the global lab notebook 

To “open” a project on MacOS desktop would mean to jump to a Space dedicated to that project, and ensure that all the active files are open in their respective apps — most commonly a browser window for various URLs and VSCode for the directory as a whole, but sometimes other things like Clarity, Preview, Terminal, Illustrator, or whatever.

Branching (“let’s consider this fork of the project idea”) is always explicit but also very helpful.

This does imply a need for both 2-way diffs and 3-way merges of files, which is easy for things like .md and .tex and code, but obviously less so for PDFs and so on… but I could think of ways to be flexible with that. 

On a mobile device, my needs are more variable, and I could easily imagine wanting to be able to access this from an untrusted device via a web service as well.

I’ve got all sorts of other thoughts, but this is sort of a snapshot of where my thinking is starting from.

**1. Pressure-testing the goal itself.** You've described "Portal" pretty concretely already — dashboard, Spaces, hotkey capture, git-backed directories, mobile access. Before I treat that as the destination: if it already existed exactly as described, what would you actually be able to *do* that you can't today? I ask because there seem to be two candidate "why"s bundled together, and they'd pull the design in different directions:

   - **(a) Reduce the tax of context-switching** — get from "sitting down" to "in flow on project X" faster.
   - **(b) Prevent silent decay** — stop back-burner and archived projects from falling off your radar entirely (the ISO push and the legal side thing sound like exactly the kind of thing that could quietly die from neglect).

   Is it both, is one primary, or is there a sharper "actual problem" underneath both? What's the worst version of Portal that would still count as a real win for you?

**2. What's actually broken today.** You already have Spaces and custom keyboards, and you clearly like them. What's the specific pain that makes "just use Spaces + keyboards better" insufficient? (E.g., is it that Spaces don't persist a *list* of what's active vs. back-burner vs. archived? That there's no single place to see everything at a glance? That opening a project still means manually recreating window/app state each time?)

**3. Scope vs. the team-tool idea.** You mentioned a separate directory with ideas for a multi-user team project management tool, and floated that Portal might grow into that "if it's useful to other people." For now, should I treat Portal as strictly single-user/personal, and treat multi-tenancy/sharing as explicitly out of scope (just a noted future aspiration, not something the architecture needs to accommodate yet)? Or is there a real near-term reason to keep the door open architecturally?

**4. The untrusted-device mobile case.** When you say you might need this "from an untrusted device via a web service," what's the actual scenario — borrowed/public computer (implying: leave no residue, maybe read-only, ephemeral session) vs. your own phone's browser (implying: full access, just not the native app)? That distinction matters a lot for what the security/auth model has to guarantee.

**5. How hard is the diff/merge requirement, really?** You flagged that 2-way diff and 3-way merge are easy for .md/.tex/code but not for PDFs, .docx/.xlsx, or things that live entirely in web services like LucidChart. Is "solve versioning for non-text files" a hard requirement for v1, or is "git tracks binary file history, no real diff/merge, and you handle rare conflicts by hand" good enough for now?

- *Glance-and-jot*: borrowed computer or public terminal, want to see what's active and drop a quick note. Should be read-mostly and leave no residue.
- *Own-phone-browser*: not the native setup, but your own device — probably fine to allow fuller access, sandboxed to that session.
- *Real-work-from-borrowed-hardware*: need to actually edit a .tex file or review a contract from a machine that isn't yours. This is the highest-risk, highest-value case, and probably the one that most determines the security model.

I'll write this into `goal/open-questions.md` as an open question ("what does 'access from an untrusted device' actually need to guarantee?") with strategy "thinking," flagged for a dedicated pass before we lock architecture. Reasonable, or do you want to hash it out now instead?

I've written the four goal documents (`problem.md`, `stakeholders.md`, `requirements.md`, `open-questions.md`) reflecting everything so far. Quick recap before we lock it in:

- **Problem**: overhead of manually managing many concurrent/back-burner/archived projects, showing up as context-switching cost, silent decay of neglected projects, and lack of location/connectivity independence.
- **Scope**: single-user, git-backed, macOS-Spaces-centric; explicitly *not* multi-user (separate future project) and *not* a comms/task-tracker replacement.
- **Stakeholders**: just you, directly; collaborators on shared content indirectly (their work could be hurt by a bad auto-merge, which is why reconciliation has to fail safe); a not-yet-characterized adversarial party tied to Q1.
- **Requirements**: dashboard, one-action open/checkpoint, standard project scaffolding, explicit branching, frictionless global capture, offline-with-auto-reconciliation (using your branch-on-disconnect / debounced-merge / fallback-to-3WM mechanism — noted in `notes.md` as the leading candidate for solution design, not locked yet), and provisional mobile/secondary-device access pending Q1.
- **Open question Q1**: what "untrusted device access" needs to guarantee — parked with three seed scenarios, flagged for a dedicated pass before architecture.

One more thing per the process: the summary that would introduce this project to someone else. Here's a draft —

> Right now there's a graveyard problem hiding in plain sight: a pile of real, valuable projects — two house deals, two research threads, a course, a book about to launch, an ISO standards push, a legal side project, plus Portal itself — competing for one person's attention, with the connective tissue between them (which Space, which files, which git commit) all held together by hand. Nothing is actually lost, but the overhead of remembering and rebuilding context every time you switch is a real tax, and the projects that don't get attention can quietly die of neglect rather than by choice.
>
> Portal is the answer: a home base that shows every project — live, back-burner, or archived — at a glance, and lets "opening" one mean actually landing in it: the right Space, the right files, the right tools, instantly. Git does the heavy lifting underneath — every project is a versioned repo with a consistent structure, checkpoints are as easy as hitting save, and forks are explicit branches you can walk away from or merge back. And it doesn't stop at the desk: work continues on a plane with no wifi and reconciles automatically when you're back online, and a stray idea at a café has somewhere real to land instead of getting stuck in a notes app you'll never revisit.
>
> It starts as a tool built for one very busy person to stop paying the context-switching tax — but the bones of it are general enough that it's easy to imagine it growing into something other people use too, someday, once it's proven itself here first.

**Assistant:** Let's dig in. Grounding this in the requirements: dashboard, one-action open/checkpoint, standard git scaffolding, explicit branching, frictionless capture, offline-with-reconciliation, and full access from your own phone.

**The real fork, before anything else gets decided:**

**Option A — A dedicated native app owns everything.** A macOS app (menu bar + dashboard window) that itself handles Space-switching, app/file launching, git operations, and the global-hotkey capture window. A companion native iOS app mirrors the dashboard/capture/edit experience on mobile.

- *Pros:* one integrated, polished experience; no dependency on other tools.
- *Cons:* macOS has no public Spaces API — this means scripting private/accessibility-layer behavior yourself, which is exactly the kind of automation that Hammerspoon/skhd/yabai/Keyboard Maestro already do well. Building and maintaining that from scratch, plus a full native iOS app, is a lot of surface for a solo builder to own.

**Option B — Portal is an orchestrator, not an owner.** You already build custom Spaces/keyboard setups — Portal leans on whatever you're already using for window management (Hammerspoon, skhd+yabai, Keyboard Maestro, whatever it is) by driving it through its scripting interface, rather than reinventing Space-switching. The dashboard and capture UI are a single small web app: run locally for full trusted desktop use, and the *same* app hosted somewhere lightweight for mobile/other-device access. Git operations happen through a small local helper. Your phone hits the hosted instance; your desktop hits the local one; it's the same UI either way, just a different trust/capability level depending on context.

- *Pros:* doesn't duplicate tooling you already like and have invested in; one dashboard codebase instead of two (web + native mobile); the local/hosted split maps naturally onto the "own device vs. untrusted device" question we already parked — when we resolve Q1, it's a matter of what the hosted instance is allowed to do, not a separate build.
- *Cons:* still need *some* small always-on-ish local helper and a lightweight hosted component; slightly more moving parts than a single app bundle.

**Option C — Fully cloud-native product**, with a real backend/database as the source of truth and a thin local helper just for OS actions. This is the shape you'd build if optimizing for an eventual multi-user product. I'd set this aside: it inverts your stated preference for git as the durable source of truth (making the web app's DB primary and git a mirror), and it's more infrastructure than a single-user tool warrants right now. Worth naming so we don't accidentally drift into it later.

I'd lean **B** — it fits "orchestrate what already works" better than "replace it," and it quietly sets up the mobile/untrusted-device story instead of creating a second one later. But this is a real fork with a real cost difference, so: does that match your instinct? And — what do you actually use today for the Spaces/hotkey side (Hammerspoon, skhd, Keyboard Maestro, something else)? That determines whether B is "drive the thing I already have" or "first go build that thing."

**Two smaller things worth your steer on while we're here:**

1. **The global lab notebook is its own repo**, separate from per-project repos — captures land there by default and get triaged/moved into the right project later (rather than trying to guess the right project at capture time, which would add friction right when you're trying to jot something down fast). Seems obviously right given what you described — flag if not.

2. **Automatic checkpoint commits vs. clean history.** If "checkpoint" fires automatically (e.g., on leaving a project's Space, or idle timeout) *and* writes real commits on the main line, history gets noisy fast — lots of "auto-checkpoint" commits mixed with your deliberate ones. Two ways to go: (a) don't worry about it, noisy history is fine, it's a personal tool; or (b) auto-checkpoints go somewhere lighter-weight (e.g., a rolling WIP branch/stash-like mechanism) and only get folded into real history when you explicitly commit. Which matches how you actually want to think about your own git history?

**Filed for later, not blocking:** OS-level window-manager automation is inherently a bit fragile across macOS updates (architecture risk); a shared local/hosted dashboard codebase means the trust boundary between them needs to be a first-class design element, not bolted on after Q1 is resolved; and GitHub (or whichever host) becomes a real single point of failure for cross-device access, worth a line in failure analysis later.

Here's where things landed:

**Solution locked in:** Portal is a native Mac app that owns Space assignment directly (not scripted through Hammerspoon/yabai/etc. — you don't have existing tooling worth preserving, and current-project inference needs that tight integration anyway). Key decisions written into `solution/solution.md`:

- Space↔project mapping is local, per-machine state — never synced, since a Space number means nothing on another device.
- A "Portal home" repo holds the project registry (active/back-burner/archived/pinned) and the root lab notebook; it's the same reusable lab-notebook/TODO/file-list template used inside every project repo, just applied once more at the top level.
- Capture is context-aware: current Space known → note goes straight into that project's notebook; no Space context → goes to the global notebook.
- Implicit checkpoints (idle-timeout auto-commits and disconnect-triggered auto-branching) are treated as *one mechanism with two triggers*, tagged distinctly from your explicit commits — clean history and full rewind granularity from the same log.
- Mobile client is deliberately lightweight, not a port of the Mac app — no Spaces to manage, just dashboard/capture/edit against the same repos.

**Two alternatives considered and set aside** (orchestrating existing window-manager tooling; a fully cloud-native product with a DB as source of truth) — reasons recorded in `solution.md`.

**New open question surfaced and logged (Q2, `goal/open-questions.md`):** there's no public macOS API for enumerating/creating/switching Spaces — this is now the biggest feasibility risk to the whole approach, flagged for an early prototyping spike before the rest of the app gets built on top of that assumption. Q1 (non-owned-device access) is still open and retagged in `notes.md` for architecture-design, since it now specifically bears on the mobile client's trust boundary.

Also updated: `requirements.md` (capture is now explicitly context-aware), `summary.md` (full problem+solution story), and wrote `solution/solution-summary.md` (a two-page concrete walkthrough of what using Portal feels like).

**Assistant:** Real question, and worth digging into properly rather than waving it off. Let me break "Space handling" into its three actual sub-problems, because they don't have the same answer.

**1. Switching to a known Space — genuinely solvable with public APIs.** macOS has real, documented "Switch to Desktop N" keyboard shortcuts (System Settings → Keyboard → Shortcuts → Mission Control). Posting those keystrokes is a fully public, documented mechanism — `CGEventPost` (Core Graphics Event Services) can synthesize them directly from Swift; you don't even need to shell out to `osascript`/AppleScript, which is slower and less clean anyway. Caveat: it's index-based (Space 3, not "the Clarity space"), so if you ever manually reorder Spaces outside Portal, the mapping can drift — Portal would need to periodically re-verify, not just trust its own memory forever.

**2. Detecting the current Space — no public Space-ID API exists, but we may not need one.** There's no documented way to ask "what space number am I on." But `CGWindowListCopyWindowInfo` *is* fully public and documented, and it returns which windows are currently on-screen. Since Portal already knows which windows it launched for which project, it can cross-check "are any of Project X's windows currently visible?" — a real, observed signal rather than an assumed one. Combine that with Portal's own record of "the last thing I switched you to," and you get a decent-confidence guess without ever touching Spaces internals directly. The nice part: this fails safe into behavior we already designed — when it's ambiguous, capture just falls back to the global notebook, exactly like the "no project context" case already does. We don't need certainty, we need a good guess with a safe fallback, and this gets there.

**3. Creating a new Space — this is the genuinely hard one.** There's no headless way to create a desktop; the only affordance is the "+" button inside the Mission Control overview itself. But here's the useful fact: Mission Control has real VoiceOver support, which means Apple exposes *some* Accessibility-API structure for it — the same public `AXUIElement`/System Events framework VoiceOver itself runs on, not a private framework. So the candidate approach is: trigger Mission Control via the public shortcut, walk its accessibility tree to find the "+" element, activate it, close Mission Control. That's automating a real, visible UI action through Apple's documented accessibility surface — a fundamentally different (and IMO much more defensible) risk than calling `CGSGetActiveSpace` via `dlsym`. It's undocumented *structure*, not an undocumented *API*. I can't tell you with certainty it'll work reliably without actually inspecting that tree — that's a real unknown, checkable in an afternoon with Xcode's Accessibility Inspector, not something I should assert confidently from memory.

One honest caveat: even fully avoiding private frameworks probably doesn't reopen Mac App Store distribution on its own — sandboxed App Store apps face separate restrictions on driving other processes via Accessibility/Apple Events (Apple rarely grants the entitlement for it). So this is worth doing for the reason you actually gave — not wanting to depend on reverse-engineered internals — rather than as a path back to the Store.

Net recommendation: **make this the new leading hypothesis for Q2** — public window-list cross-checking + self-tracking for detection, public keystroke simulation for switching, and accessibility-tree UI automation (not private symbols) only for the rare "create a new Space" case. Still needs the empirical spike either way, but now it's spiking a fully-public-framework approach first, with SkyLight/CGS as the fallback only if the accessibility tree turns out too thin to find the "+" reliably. Want me to write this into `architecture.md` and Q2 as the primary path?

Good — this tightened things up considerably. Here's the shape now:

**`SpaceController`** is a single protocol with two operations, matching your breakdown almost exactly but merging the first two since they turn out to need the same underlying observation:

- **`observeCurrentContext()`** — checks currently-visible windows (`CGWindowListCopyWindowInfo`, public) against what Portal knows it launched per project, infers the current project (or safely returns "none," which just falls through to the existing global-notebook capture behavior), and *as a side effect* refreshes the Space↔project mapping for wherever this turns out to be. Your "refresh the mapping" and "figure out current project" aren't two operations — they're one observation used two ways.
- **`activateSpace(for:)`** — reuse the assigned Space (verified by content, not trusted blindly), else an unassigned one, else create a new one and assign it.

**The self-healing bit worth flagging:** Spaces are addressed by ordinal position (same index the "Switch to Desktop N" shortcuts use), which can drift if you manually reorder Spaces. Rather than trusting that index, every activation gets verified against actual window content — a mismatch corrects the mapping on the spot instead of propagating a wrong guess.

**Your parenthetical caught a real wrinkle:** creating a new Space means the shortcut for switching to it needs to exist too, and macOS doesn't enable "Switch to Desktop N" by default for arbitrary N. That's now an explicit sub-question in Q2 — may need to programmatically edit the symbolic-hotkeys plist (common utility-app convention, not blessed but far more benign than `dlsym`-ing CGS) or fall back to slower Mission-Control-visible switching past whatever's pre-enabled.

Two implementations sit behind the protocol: the public-API hybrid we discussed as primary, and private SkyLight/CGS symbols as a fallback — meaning if the public approach doesn't hold up, it's a contained swap, not a rewrite. I've updated `architecture.md`, `system-design.json`, `threat-model.md`, `solution-summary.md`, and Q2 in `open-questions.md` to reflect this, and logged the general pattern ("isolate genuinely uncertain mechanisms behind a narrow, swappable interface") in `notes.md` since it's a reusable design instinct, not a one-off.

Both real unknowns (shortcut auto-enablement, Accessibility Inspector check on the "+" button) are cheap to check empirically. Want to actually go do that spike next, or keep going on architecture (command grammar semantics, Project manifest format) first?

**The spikes are now T1–T5 in `todo.md`**, written as falsification tests rather than "look into it" — each names what result would change the design. Two additions beyond what we'd discussed:

- **T3**: whether `CGWindowListCopyWindowInfo` needs a Screen Recording grant. Window *titles* require it on modern macOS; PIDs and bounds don't. Since our inference keys on PIDs of processes Portal launched itself, we should be fine — but if we're wrong, Portal triggers a recurring scary permission prompt, which would undercut the entire low-friction premise. Cheap to check, bad to discover late.
- **T5**: cross-compiling the git library + UniFFI to `aarch64-linux-android` with a working authenticated HTTPS round trip. This tests the *shared-core bet itself*, not just the library choice — and TLS is the likely sharp edge. It's the most load-bearing decision in the architecture and among the cheapest to falsify today.

**Then the architecture work — and the thread I pulled turned out to be the important one.**

Git is the reconciliation engine for everything, and git's merge is line-based. Which means: **the conflict rate is a property of our file layout, not a fact of nature.** Two devices diverged and both editing is the *normal* case here. That makes repo layout load-bearing architecture, not a formatting detail. The rule that falls out — make a file's line structure match its semantic unit of change, and never let position in a file carry meaning:

- **Registry**: one small file per project, not one `registry.json`. A single registry file would be the most-contended file in the system, and a botched three-way merge on JSON produces a *syntactically invalid* file rather than a marked-up one.
- **Notebook**: one file per entry, `<ISO-timestamp>-<device>.md`. Capture is the highest-frequency operation in the system, and appending to a shared file conflicts *every single time* two diverged devices both do it. Per-entry files make collision impossible by construction.
- **TODO/collection**: one item per line, stable IDs, ordering as a rendered property. Reordering on the Mac while checking one item off on the phone is semantically trivial but would be a huge textual conflict if position meant anything.

**One real gap surfaced.** "One merge flow" quietly assumed mergeable content — but delegated files are largely binary, and git can't three-way merge them at all. So a disconnect-branch can produce a conflict that's not just unresolved but *unresolvable on the device holding it*: a binary conflict on a phone that can't open the file. I've written a keep-both policy (retain both, rename the incoming one by device+timestamp, file a TODO) and added it to the threat model as a new medium-high risk. Never blocks, never picks a winner, never loses data.

I also made "auto-merge must fail safe" concrete: **automatic reconciliation performs only clean merges.** Any conflict leaves the branch intact and flags the dashboard. Fast-forward, merge cleanly, or stop and ask — never auto-resolve.

**One question genuinely needs your call**, since it's a values trade-off rather than a technical one: per-entry notebook files drive capture conflicts to zero, but you lose the ability to open one file and read the notebook straight through — in an editor, or on GitHub's web view. For a *lab notebook* specifically, that may matter more than it would for other data. My proposed compromise is having Portal regenerate a read-only `notebook.md` on commit: it reintroduces the contended file, but as a regenerable artifact whose conflicts are always resolvable by rebuilding rather than merging. Does that land right, or is straight-through readability important enough to want the primary storage to be one file?

Still unexplored: the command grammar's execution semantics (what `/open` does when the project's already open, what `/close` means for uncommitted work) and the project manifest format.

Your doc overturns one of my proposals, and the reason it does is the more valuable finding.

**I had the notebook wrong.** I proposed one file per captured entry with machine-timestamped names — which drives the capture conflict rate to literally zero, and would have made the notebook unreadable as a notebook. Your doc puts reading it end to end first, and explicitly rules out visible timestamp litter. I optimized a property of the merge algorithm at the expense of what the file is actually *for*. Day files (`YYMMDD-<few words>.md`) are the right call.

**But then your last line for TODO lists — "3WM should be structurally aware" — is the sentence that reorganizes the architecture.** The fix for day-file appends isn't a cleverer layout, it's that Portal shouldn't be using git's generic merge at all for files it understands. A notebook is append-only, so a two-device append conflict has a canonical resolution: keep both blocks, order them. Git can't see that. Portal can. So I've made **type-aware merge a real component of the Rust core** — a strategy table dispatched by file type — and recorded it as decision D1. Layout still helps, but it's now the supporting mechanism rather than the primary one.

Three things fell out of that which I think are genuine wins:

- **Ordering without timestamps.** Since every append is a commit, the merger can order the two sides' blocks from *commit* history — no timestamp anywhere in the prose. Your "where do merged entries go" question answers itself, with each side's block staying contiguous rather than interleaving line-by-line, which would scramble prose. (Clock skew between devices can mis-order two blocks within a day — cosmetic in an append-only log, not corruption.)
- **Check-off-on-phone + reword-on-Mac stops being a conflict.** I'd previously called that genuinely conflicting. With structured items it isn't — status and text are different *fields*, so a field-level merge takes each from one side. Only both sides rewriting the text is real. That's the concrete payoff of structural awareness.
- **The archive should be filtered, not moved.** Moving done items into an archive file makes the most common operation in the system — ticking something off — a two-file delete-plus-add, which is the worst case for merging. Since status already determines visibility, derive it: completed items stay put, the view filters them, and compaction to the archive is a deliberate low-frequency operation.

**One consequence for the spike list:** type-aware merge imposes a requirement on the git library that generic merge quality doesn't cover — it has to expose base/ours/theirs blobs and accept a self-computed resolution back into the index. A library could pass my original T4 and still make that awkward, so T4 now tests both. I also added **T6**, property-testing the merge rules against generated divergence scenarios, because D1 moves Portal from "git decides" to "Portal decides," which is the corruption risk in a new form. It's now the top item in the threat model, with git history as the backstop — every bad automatic merge stays recoverable since the pre-merge commits survive.

**The unified cross-project view raised something I don't think we'd noticed:** it's a read model spanning *every* project repo. On the Mac that's a local scan. On Android, cloning every project just to render a summary list may not be acceptable — and urgency changes with no one editing anything, since due dates approach on their own, so it recomputes on a timer. My proposal is a lightweight rollup index in Home, regenerated by whichever device has full clones, treated as a regenerable artifact whose conflicts resolve by rebuilding. But that's a real fork in the mobile storage story, not a detail.

Two open questions I'd want your call on:

1. **How visible should per-item IDs be in raw TODO files?** They're the join key that makes structural merge work across moves and reparenting, but visible IDs clutter a file whose hand-editability is the point. Trailing comment is my guess.
2. **Who picks the "few words" in a notebook filename?** They're human-meaningful, so either you supply them at the day's first append (friction at exactly the moment friction is most costly) or Portal picks something and lets you rename later.

Also noted in `notes.md` that you keep design detail docs in the project root, so I check for new ones rather than waiting to be told.

**User:** There’s something I ought to add: Every file type’s 3WM operation will generally require an OS-level rendering and user interface. This means that the core’s merge operation can call it, but it can’t be implemented, because the whole point of a merge conflict is that user intervention is required.

In a really optimal world, each file type would need to have logic for:
* How to “open” it on each of the supported OS’s
* A fixed function to check for merge conflicts, and to auto-merge when there are no conflicts — this could live in the common core
* A UX for 2-way diffs and 3-way merges on each platform
* In the future, other things — e.g., “generate / update index data for this file” so that we can implement useful search across projects; an “event / digest” structure for the files themselves, so we could implement useful concurrent editing; and so on.

Generally, I think each file type ought to be a logical bundle that supports a lot of these operations, and we should think of the API for that. Note that “generic binary file” is a useful type, here; its 3WM would be “open the three versions side-by-side in the appropriate app and ask the user to pick or generate something new.” 

This might help us resolve a lot of issues.

**Assistant:** This is a significant reframing — and it resolves several things I'd left open. Let me read the current state precisely before restructuring.
Now let me restructure the architecture around bundles.
Now the risks section, key decisions, and diagram.
That reframing does resolve a lot — including two things I'd left open and one I'd gotten wrong.

**The observation underneath it is the load-bearing one:** anything requiring human judgment can't live in the shared core, because judgment needs rendering and interaction. I had "merge" sitting in the core as one operation, which was quietly incoherent. The split you're describing is `merge3` (total, non-interactive — either produces a result or describes precisely what a human must decide) in the core, and `presentMerge` in each shell. **`ConflictSet` is the contract between them**, and I've flagged it as the highest-leverage interface in the system: it's what every type's core half emits and every platform's UI consumes. I'd want to design it against the notebook and TODO cases concretely rather than in the abstract, before either merge UI gets built.

Three things fell out that I think are real wins:

- **"Generic binary" as a real type retires a hack.** My keep-both-and-file-a-TODO policy was a special case bolted onto one situation. Your version — open all three side by side, pick or create — is a genuine resolution UX, and it's just what one bundle does. Better.
- **`capabilities` makes deferral first-class.** The stranded-conflict-on-a-phone problem stops being a special case and becomes ordinary behavior with a defined path: the platform declares it can't resolve this type, the conflict stays unmerged and visible, a capable device picks it up. (One residual I've noted as a risk: "deferred indefinitely because you never open the Mac" needs a visible answer on the dashboard rather than quiet accumulation.)
- **Native vs. delegated dissolves.** They were never two kinds of file — just two points on a spectrum of how many bundle operations a type implements itself. I've replaced the noun definition accordingly.

**The blob-read point is the cleanest fix of the batch.** "Use the local clone if there is one, else read the blob from GitHub" kills the mobile storage question outright *and* retires my rollup-index proposal, which I was uneasy about anyway — a derived cache living inside git, with staleness and contention baked in. Three consequences I've written down: it needs the network, so the unified view degrades to what's cloned locally when offline; reads should fan out in parallel since latency is per-repo; and a blob read returns committed state, so very recent uncheckpointed edits on another device won't show. Scoping to active projects bounds all of it.

**On the TODO correction** — you're right, and it dissolves the ID-visibility question I asked. If the native UX is the common path, hand-editing is the exceptional path, so visible IDs cost much less than I was pricing them at. Raw readability still matters; it just doesn't matter enough to trade away a reliable join key.

**One place I'd push back, gently.** The separate-TODO-app idea is a good forcing function for the interface and a bad thing to actually build in v1 — a second binary means IPC, an install story, and version skew between components that must agree on a file format. I've written it in as a *constraint on the interface design* ("if the interface is right, notebook and TODO should feel like clients of it, not special cases inside Portal") rather than as a deliverable. Same for bundles generally: module boundary and static registry, not a plugin runtime. Design so out-of-process is possible later; don't implement it now. This is the same instinct as `SpaceController`, which is already in `notes.md` as a reusable pattern.

**The real cost to watch** is that bundles make the *structure* clean without reducing the *work*: every type is N × 2 platform UI implementations. That argues for keeping the type list genuinely short and letting generic text and generic binary absorb as much as possible.

Recorded as decision **D2** (with D1 amended — it had assumed merge lived wholly in the core). **User:** The thing that got me thinking about TODO as a separate app is the question of binary files. PDFs *are* viewed by a separate app on both architectures, and so our merge3 implementation is opening another app no matter what, and getting back the name of a filename with the merge result. Note that Portal’s dependency on TODO is very little — all it’s saying is that “most projects have a TODO data structure at their top level, and we also include a TODO render as part of our top-level UX experience.” But if we said “most projects have a .png at their top level, and we open that with Preview as part of our top-level UX experience” it would be the same. 

So this makes me wonder — what could we really generalize from that?

**Assistant:** This is the right question to pull on. Let me think through it properly, because some of what generalizes is real and one tempting version is a trap.

**What your PDF observation actually reveals:** for a binary type, `presentMerge` is already *launch an external app over three files, get back a path to the result*. Portal is orchestrating a foreign process for that case regardless. So a separate TODO app isn't an exotic new capability — it's the exact same mechanism, applied to an app we happen to have written. Which means my pushback last turn was aimed at the wrong target: the *architecture* of a separate app is free, already required, and already designed. The only real cost is building a second binary. So the right posture is to define the resolver protocol so out-of-process is a deployment choice per type, then implement in-process for now and lose nothing.

That gives the first generalization, and I think it's the strongest:

**1. Every shell-side bundle operation is "invoke something over N files, optionally get a file back."** `open` is one file in, nothing out, edits in place. `presentDiff` is two in, nothing out. `presentMerge` is three in, one out. That's one protocol at varying arity, and it's *identical* whether the resolver is Portal's own SwiftUI view or Preview. Two-way and three-way stop being different operations — a viewer that can show three versions can show two.

**2. `capabilities` should be a runtime question to the OS, not a static table.** If resolution is "invoke an app that handles this type," then "can this platform resolve `.ai` conflicts?" is really "is anything registered to open `.ai` here?" — which macOS answers via UTI/role handlers and Android via Intent resolution. That deletes a hand-maintained per-platform matrix and makes deferral honest: Portal defers because the OS genuinely has no handler, not because someone forgot to update a list.

**3. The project manifest and the "file/URL collection" native type are probably the same file.** Opening a project is "launch a set of apps over a set of files"; `open` on a bundle is "launch an app over a file." Same verb, different scale — a project *is* a collection, and opening it is bulk-invoking bundle opens. We currently describe these as two concepts (`solution.md` decision 1 has a manifest, decision 4 has a collection type). I think that's one concept described twice, and collapsing it removes a noun.

**4. Your future "generate index data" operation and the cross-project urgent view are the same mechanism.** This is the one I didn't see until your question forced it. I'd said the unified TODO view means Portal must understand TODO semantics — but it doesn't, if bundles project their files into records: *here are my salient items, with text, urgency, timestamps, location*. Search wants text plus location; the urgent view wants items plus due dates; a digest wants events. All three are queries over one "extract structured facts from this file" operation. New cross-cutting features then stop requiring changes to every file type. **Define that operation now; do not build an index engine** — for v1 the "query space" is a list in memory.

**5. The biggest reframe, which I want your call on before I restructure anything: Portal is a shell, not a content app.** In the OS sense — it composes, routes, versions, and associates context, but owns no content types. Its irreducible core is: a project is a git repo plus a manifest of roles→paths; the Space/context association; the command grammar; checkpoint/branch/merge orchestration; and aggregation over bundle-extracted records. "Most projects have a TODO at top level and we render it" becomes a *manifest declaration*, exactly as your `.png`-with-Preview substitution suggests — and a project wanting a bibliography or a Gantt chart at top level just declares it.

The reason I want your call: it changes the nouns, and it can be oversold. The dashboard genuinely has Portal-owned aggregate content — the project list with states, deferred conflicts needing attention, the cross-project urgent view. So the honest version is that the dashboard is *both* Portal-owned aggregate panels and bundle-provided per-file panels, not "Portal owns nothing." Stated carefully it's a real simplification; stated loosely it turns the dashboard into a plugin canvas with no opinion.

**And the trap, named so we don't wander into it:** "everything is a plugin, Portal is a platform." Every example you gave lives inside one host app's UX. What pays is the *interface shape* — files in, file out; extract records; ask the OS what it can open. What doesn't pay is a plugin runtime, a manifest DSL, or a generic record store. The generalization is worth taking exactly as far as it deletes concepts, and no further.

**Two wrinkles the out-of-process path creates that in-process doesn't:** Preview will never tell you it's finished merging, so Portal has to own a thin "done — here's the result" wrapper around any external resolver, meaning the resolution flow is never *entirely* foreign. And "open three versions side by side" barely exists as an Android interaction, so the deferral path will be the common case on mobile rather than the rare one — good thing it's first-class.

I've recorded these as candidate generalizations in `notes.md` so they're not lost. Before I restructure `architecture.md`: **do you want #5 as the organizing frame** — Portal as shell, standard project structure as manifest convention rather than hardcoded layout? #1–#4 I'd adopt regardless, and #3 in particular I'd like to confirm is a genuine duplicate rather than two things I'm conflating.

**User:** On #3, I would add a note — the project manifest is an instance of “file / URL collection,” but there will be other instances of it. For example, in a research project, the potential bibliographic references accumulate in exactly that format. So that’s a reusable pattern.

I think #4 is a very good point, and it leads towards my agreeing with this idea of #5. I don’t think this means that “everything is a plugin, Portal is a platform.” What I think the pattern is is:

- Portal has its own native UX — the project picker and status view that’s on the front page, and the MessageBox that can be invoked anywhere. 
- Most apps have zero awareness of Portal and are just opened by it.
- Some apps, OTOH, have a useful awareness. TODO, lab notebooks, and maybe the thing we use for doing classical 3WM’s on text files would be examples of this. Those apps might (a) do information across the central Portal repo plus other things, as the TODO app does when it synthesizes a “main” TODO list; (b) render different information depending on whether you’re looking at a particular project right now or at the top level view; (c) register commands like “take a note” or “update a TODO item” for the MessageBox; (d) provide UX widgets like “showing the TODO list” which would be rendered in the Portal panel; (e) perform nontrivial versions of 2WD and 3WM.

So there’s some amount of panel-building here, but this API suggests that an app can interface with Portal in some fancy ways, and we may be building a couple of those apps very early on in this process.

**Assistant:** Your three-tier formulation is the thing that makes this bounded rather than a platform play. Let me check one structural question before I write it up.
Now the nouns — including the collection-as-reusable-pattern correction.
Written up. Your three-tier framing is what makes this bounded — the distinction between "an app can integrate deeply" and "everything is a plugin" is exactly that most apps are Tier 2 and are owed nothing, and Tier 3 is a small set we write ourselves.

Recorded as **D3** (Portal is a shell; one faceted integration surface at three tiers) and **D4** (Collection is a reusable pattern; the manifest is one instance). Your bibliography example is the thing that makes D4 worth having — I'd been about to collapse manifest and collection into one concept, which would have been wrong. Instance-of is right, and it puts a real requirement on the format: per-entry metadata general enough to carry both launch information and citation data, with a Project holding several collections distinguished by declared role rather than by fixed path.

Three things worth flagging from writing it up.

**Your (a) — cross-repo aggregation — is the one facet I'd narrow.** Rather than integrations reading repos, Portal runs `extract` across active projects itself and hands `render(scope)` the derived records. The TODO app never touches a repo; it receives items. This matters because the moment a Portal-aware app is a separate process, "read across projects" means handing it GitHub credentials or a broad proxied read API — and host-side aggregation keeps credentials entirely inside Portal for free. It also means your (a) and the future search indexing are literally the same mechanism rather than two. The check I'd want: is there something the TODO app needs from other projects that a record extraction can't express? If yes, this is too narrow.

**Your (b) is nearly "scope = Home," which is a nice consistency.** `render(scope)` where scope is a Project or the global view — and since Home is already a Project rather than a special thing, the global case is *almost* just rendering Home. The only difference is that global scope additionally carries the cross-project aggregate. One operation with a parameter, not two code paths.

**Your (d) is the expensive one, and it's asymmetric across platforms.** This is the finding I didn't have before working through it: the merge facets are trivially out-of-process — files in, file out, which Portal already does with Preview. But panel rendering across a process boundary is a different problem. macOS has real mechanisms (XPC remote views, app extensions); Android has essentially no clean cross-app UI embedding outside narrow, very limited widget frameworks. So a separate TODO app is plausible-with-work on the Mac and close to infeasible on Android *for the panel specifically*.

The consequence I've written in: v1 integrations are modules compiled into the shells, with the "app" framing as design discipline and interface constraint rather than a process boundary. That preserves what you're after — and since you said we may build a couple of these early, that's exactly the right shape: build them as real integrations against the real interface, just not as separate binaries yet. If a genuinely separate app later becomes a goal rather than a discipline, the Android panel is the wall it hits, and that's worth confirming before committing.

Two new open items from your (c): MessageBox verbs becoming extensible needs a namespace rule (so app verbs are distinguishable from Portal's own) and a discovery mechanism — an unbounded mystery vocabulary would be worse than a fixed one. Both are in `todo.md` under design work, ahead of any integration registering a verb.

The command grammar is now the obvious next thing — it's gone from a fixed vocabulary to an extension point, and `/open` on an already-open project and `/close` with uncommitted work are still undefined.

**On Android I had the asymmetry backwards, and your framing fixes it.** I said Android can't do cross-process view embedding, treating that as the constraint. But you don't *embed* on Android — you launch. Separate apps with home-screen widgets *is* the platform's idiom, so the thing I flagged as Android's weakness is only a weakness if you insist on a Mac-shaped solution there. That also puts this squarely on the existing grain: the Android client was never a port, and a summoned floating overlay is as much a Mac idea as a global hotkey is.

**On deferring the MessageBox — I worked through whether it holds, and I think it does.** Walking the vocabulary: `/open` is the project picker (better — you see the list), `/commit` is a button on the status widget, `/new` is a button, and capture is typing straight into the notebook widget, which beats `/note` on two counts: no syntax, and the day's existing entries are visible while you write. `/branch` and `/merge` are rare and deliberate enough that an affordance is fine. So the vocabulary doesn't lose much, and deferring it deletes three open problems at once — the command namespace rule, the discovery mechanism, and the `commands()` facet.

**What I'd keep is the distinction the original decision actually got right.** `solution.md` decision 9 said the *vocabulary*, not the console, was the real interface. That's still true and still load-bearing — the core keeps exposing `open_project`, `commit`, `branch`, `merge`, `note` as operations. What's deferred is the parsed-text front end, not the verb layer. I've amended decision 9 in place rather than striking it, since its central claim survives.

**The one condition I'd put on it:** capture friction must not regress. The entire problem statement is about context-switching cost and notes landing where they get lost, so "gesture, type, enter" has to stay exactly that — the summoning gesture landing focus directly in the notebook widget's input. If that holds, this is strictly better. If it doesn't, the deferral is a regression against the core problem rather than a simplification. I've added it as **T7**, and the reason deferring is safe rather than merely cheap is that the MessageBox returns as *one more widget in the panel* — an addition, not a re-architecture.

**Two implementation notes I put in while writing it up**, both permission-related since we're already carrying TCC surface from `SpaceController`: the panel wants to be a non-activating `NSPanel` at floating window level (the Spotlight/Alfred shape, appears over other apps without stealing full activation), and the summoning hotkey should use Carbon's `RegisterEventHotKey`, which needs no grant at all — a global event monitor would need Accessibility. Worth knowing before someone reaches for the obvious API.

**One consolidation worth confirming:** I've written the panel and the full dashboard as *one widget set at two presentations*, differing in size, density, and default scope rather than in content or construction. That deletes a concept if right — tell me if you were picturing them as genuinely different surfaces.

**Assistant:** Both are clean. Writing them up surfaced one real hazard and two questions worth your call.
Recorded as **D6**. Both semantics wrote up cleanly, and `open` in particular got simpler than expected: it isn't "launch things," it's **"make this project current"** — launching the manifest is just what that entails the first time, when nothing's running. That makes it idempotent and collapses what could have been two verbs into one.

**The one real hazard is in `close`'s ordering.** Quitting is asynchronous, and a save dialog can hold an app open for many seconds. If Portal commits when the quit request is *sent* rather than when termination is *observed*, it captures a stale working tree and silently drops your last edits — precisely the failure the whole checkpoint design exists to prevent, arriving through the one verb you'd most expect to be safe. The fix is discipline rather than difficulty (sequence on `NSRunningApplication` termination notifications), but it's easy to get wrong, so it's now risk #3 in the threat model.

That gives `close` several defined outcomes instead of one:

- **Save dialog cancelled, or an app won't quit** → abort. Don't commit-and-push a half-closed session; report which app is holding on. Cancelling a save dialog is you saying "wait," and the verb should honor that.
- **App ignores quit entirely** → time out, leave it, report it. Never force-kill something that may hold unsaved work.
- **Nothing changed** → no empty commit; push only if there are unpushed commits.
- **Offline or push fails** → close still succeeds. Commit is local, push is best-effort, existing reconcile-on-reconnect handles it. Closing a project must never block on connectivity.

Two things fell out that I didn't expect. **"Portal closes only what Portal launched"** needs no new machinery — the per-project PID tracking already maintained for `observeCurrentContext` answers it, which is the third time that one observation has served an extra purpose. And **`close` is the first verb with genuinely transactional character**: a sequence with a defined abort point and a best-effort tail. It isn't atomic, and I've written it as explicitly non-atomic rather than papering over that.

**A boundary on an existing pattern, worth naming:** there's deliberately no *implicit* close, even though checkpoint and branch both have automatic triggers. Going to lunch shouldn't quit your apps. The general rule that falls out — automatic triggers suit operations that are invisible and reversible, and are wrong for anything that closes windows or interrupts. Committing in the background is invisible and undoable; quitting an app is neither. Worth having stated, since the "one mechanism, multiple triggers" pattern will keep tempting us.

**Two questions for you**, both now in `todo.md`:

1. **How should `close`'s commit be tagged?** It's user-triggered but not user-titled, so it fits neither category cleanly. My proposal: treat it as implicit (machine-tagged) by default, promoted to explicit if you supply a title. Closing several projects a day shouldn't clutter the story `git log` tells — but a session boundary is genuinely meaningful, so I could see the argument the other way.
2. **How do you get back a manifest entry you closed by hand?** `open` deliberately won't relaunch it, which I think is right, but that means there needs to be some explicit "reopen the manifest" affordance with a home in the panel.

That's exactly the "must not drift, would drift invisibly" case, and it's what `solution.md` decision 7 was protecting. But the *trigger sources* aren't — idle detection and network reachability are platform APIs. So shells detect and inject `on_idle` / `on_disconnect` / `on_reconnect`, and every resulting decision is made in one place. Shedding the detection is what lets the state machine be genuinely portable rather than portable-with-holes.

**On open/reopen:** two adjacent buttons is right, and framing `reopen` as a *reconcile* rather than a *relaunch* makes it better than expected — launch whatever the manifest lists and isn't running, touch nothing that is. The division is clean — **`open` jumps to a different project, `reopen` restores the current one.**

Its natural sibling is "add this to the manifest," for when you've opened something by hand and want it remembered. Since the manifest is a Collection (D4), that's an ordinary edit rather than new machinery.

**Q1: what does "access from hardware you don't own" actually need to guarantee?**

It comes from requirement 9, which is deliberately provisional: *provide some level of access from borrowed or public hardware* — with the capability level and security guarantees left open. Requirement 8 (full access from your own phone) is settled and firm; Q1 is only about machines that aren't yours. There's a companion line in the requirements saying such access must leave behind no durable credentials or cached project content, also marked pending.

Two candidate scenarios were sketched as seeds, never confirmed or prioritized:

1. **Glance-and-jot** — a borrowed or public computer, where you want to see what's active and drop a quick note. Read-mostly, must leave no residue when the session ends.
2. **Real work from borrowed hardware** — actually editing real content: a `.tex` file, reviewing a contract. Highest value, highest risk, and the case that would most determine the security model.

**Why it's still open and why it matters here:** the answer decides how much has to live on-device versus server-side, and whether a hosted component is needed at all. Get it wrong in one direction and you over-build security for a case that never happens; in the other, you under-build for one that does. Scenario 2 in particular is the one that could pull a backend into a design that currently has none — and "no server to deploy, monitor, or page anyone about" is currently one of the architecture's better properties.

You'd confirmed earlier that this deserves a dedicated pass rather than being settled in passing.

**What's changed since it was written, though, is that Q1 has gotten cheaper to defer.** Three things now absorb it:

- The **content accessor** already reads projects without a clone, straight from GitHub blobs. A residue-free session on borrowed hardware is architecturally close to "the accessor with no local clone and nothing persisted."
- **Credentials never leave Portal** and are never persisted by the core — already true for the credential-mediation reasons, independent of Q1.
- The **integration/capabilities model** means a restricted environment is a platform with fewer capabilities rather than a new kind of client, and conflicts it can't handle simply defer to a device that can.

So my read is that Q1 is now a *scoping* question — what should be possible on borrowed hardware — rather than an architectural fork. Scenario 1 looks like it needs almost nothing new. Scenario 2 is where the real decision lives, and it's the one that could still change the shape of things.

Worth a dedicated pass, and it's the last open item that could move the design. Want to work through it now, or take a break from architecture and start on one of the queued design pieces — the `ConflictSet` contract is the highest-leverage of those?

**Assistant:** Four good notes. Item 3 is the most useful — your confusion found two real design smells, not just unclear prose. Let me fix those first, since they change what goes in the build spec.

All four handled. New doc: **`Clarity Protocol/solution/software-architecture.md`** — the build spec, organized exactly as you asked.

**On (3) — your confusion was diagnostic, and both names were covering design smells.** Recorded as D13.

`claims(path, content) -> bool` was a per-integration predicate, but that's not how Portal actually identifies files. The notebook and TODO list are known by *where they are* — the manifest declares `notebook -> notebook/`, `todo -> todo.md` — not by sniffing contents. So it becomes a central resolver with three tiers in priority order: role declarations, then extension/UTI, then a content sniff for text-vs-binary. Integrations register declarative claims (which roles they serve, which extensions they own) rather than each exposing a function that gets tried in sequence.

`parse`/`serialize` shouldn't have been in the API at all. Every facet consumes and produces *bytes* at the boundary — `merge3`, `extract`, all of it — and nothing outside an integration ever handles its parsed model. Parsing is internal. What the shells actually need is **typed domain operations**: the TODO integration exposing `list_items`/`set_status`/`add_item`, the notebook exposing `append`/`read_day`. That's what a widget calls. One real constraint falls out: those models cross the UniFFI boundary, so they have to be records, enums, and sequences — no generics.

**On (2) — you're right, and it's a ladder rather than a table.** I'd written each type as having one strategy, which is wrong in two ways you identified. Textual merge3 must be available to *every* type, not just a "generic text" type, so a structural merge can degrade into it partway instead of failing wholesale. And keep-both comes back as rung 4 — I'd dropped it when display-and-pick arrived, but display-and-pick needs a capable device, so it can't be terminal. Now:

1. Type-specific structural merge (declines what it can't parse)
2. Textual three-way — a core primitive, the workhorse for `.md`, `.tex`, source
3. Display-and-pick — needs a device that can render the type
4. Keep both, distinctly named, flagged — always available

Rung 4 being a guaranteed terminal is what makes the merge story total: there's always an answer, even when nothing can combine the file and no device present can render it. D11.

**On (1) — adoption, and it surfaced a hazard I think is more likely than the merge risks.** D12. Adopting a repo means Portal starts auto-committing to something *other people use*. Idle-timeout checkpoints landing on a collaborator's `main` would be genuinely bad — and unlike the merge-corruption threat, this one needs no bug at all, just working as designed on the wrong repo.

So checkpoint policy becomes per-project: auto-commit-and-push for Portal-created projects, **auto-commit-local-only for an adopted repo that already has a remote**, with adoption surfacing the choice rather than assuming. Also: because roles map to paths (D4), an adopted repo keeps its own layout instead of having Portal's imposed on it, and adoption never touches existing history, `.gitignore`, or hooks.

**On (4) — the doc.** `architecture.md` is the reasoning, `decisions.md` the rationale, and this is what you type. Five sections: module layout; the core's five mechanisms with signatures; the integration API with both halves; Mac flows; Android flows; plus cross-cutting error/threading/testing notes. Two things I did deliberately:

- **A first cut at `ConflictSet`** (§1.4) rather than leaving it as a TODO — it's easier to react to a concrete strawman than to design it from nothing.
- **Marked the thin ice inline.** Sections resting on unrun spikes carry **[unvalidated]** with the test number, so when you sit down to code you know which parts are specified from reasoning versus confirmed.

Worth flagging honestly: the doc is a *first* build spec, not a finished one. `ConflictSet` needs real design against the notebook and TODO cases, and the native format specs are still the gap between this and being able to scaffold a repo.

**Assistant:** `extract` first, then the lifecycle — which is the bigger piece and reshapes several of your other points.

**`extract`** is the "generate index data" idea you raised a few turns back, and I named it badly by not re-explaining it. It turns a file into structured records the host can aggregate: the TODO integration emits one record per item (text, status, due date, urgency, project); the notebook emits records per day entry (date, text). The cross-project urgent view is a query over those records, and future search is the same query surface. It's what lets the dashboard show "most urgent items everywhere" without Portal understanding TODO semantics or integrations touching repos.

Now the lifecycle.
The lifecycle is now `software-architecture.md` §2, and thinking it through end-to-end changed more than the individual notes did. Recorded as **D14**, **D15**, **D16**.

**Your heartbeat instinct was right, and it goes further than replacing `on_idle`.** If the shell reports `{active_projects, online}` on a timer, then disconnect and reconnect stop being separate events too — the core sees `online: false` across k consecutive heartbeats and branches. **Debouncing falls out for free** instead of being a mechanism someone has to build. It also sharpens D7 nicely: shells don't detect *events*, only *facts*. And I made it return `Vec<CoreEvent>`, so the same call is how the UI learns something happened without polling.

**Writing out the state space produced the finding I think matters most.** A project has six independent dimensions of state, and most bugs will come from combinations. Two are easy to get wrong:

- **Upstream state is `unknown` when offline.** You can't know whether you're behind without fetching, and rendering that as "up to date" is a lie the user will act on. That became invariant I5.
- **Connectivity isn't binary** — a captive portal is "online" while GitHub is unreachable, and those need different messages.

**And the invariant I'd most want to hold you to (I2): never leave the working tree in a conflicted git state.** When a merge conflicts, abort it and leave the branch unreconciled rather than sitting in a conflicted index. That makes a conflict a **task** rather than a **mode** — which matters enormously when it surfaces on a phone that can't resolve it. Your tree stays usable, you keep working, and the conflict waits somewhere visible. The alternative strands you in a state you can't exit on the device you're holding.

A related one (I4): **pending work is computed, not stored.** Divergence and unreconciled branches are derivable from git itself, so there's no synced metadata to drift. A disconnect branch is just `portal/disconnect/<device>/<timestamp>`; "needs attention" means it exists and hasn't merged.

**Two things I added that you didn't ask for but the lifecycle demanded:**

- **`drop_clone` must refuse when there's unpushed work or an unreconciled branch.** A phone with forty projects wants to shed clones, but this is otherwise a silent data-loss path wearing the costume of a cleanup command.
- **`SyncOutcome::HeldByPolicy`** — so an adopted repo under local-only checkpointing reads as *deliberate*. "Ahead by 12 commits" with no explanation looks like a bug.

**On shared repos (D15) — agreed, and I'd state the rule sharply: Portal must never push to a branch other people build from.** Your dedicated-branch model preserves both halves — continuous auto-save for you, deliberate human-reviewed integration for everyone else. v1 detects an existing remote and defaults to local-only.

**On `MergeOutcome` (D16) — yes, and it's better than just permissive typing.** `Conflicted { set: Option<ConflictSet> }`, where absence of detail is *itself* the signal to fall to rung 3. The ladder needs no special case for binaries; the type carries the distinction.

`sync` is the full round trip (fetch, integrate, push if clean) since that's what both the checkpoint machinery and the user's mental model want, decomposing into `fetch`/`integrate`/`push` for finer control. Branch create/switch/merge/delete are all in §2.6, with `switch_branch` checkpointing first per I1.

Back to facets when you're ready — `extract` is now documented properly in §5.1, and the remaining question there is whether the shell-side facet split still looks right to you now that the domain-operations idea has replaced `parse`/`serialize`.

If you’ve edited a file in a local app and haven’t saved changes within that app, there’s really nothing we can do to help you anyway, so treating that as “nothing changed” seems reasonable)
- `sync` becomes something that gets automatically called by the heartbeat loop, and which can also be manually called from the UI — it’s basically the “save” button. We can state an invariant here: At the end of a call to sync, the local git working tree should be clean. Thus:
  - Sync always commits (either with an autocommit tag or an explicit tag, depending on whether it was being called from a heartbeat etc or explicitly by the user)
  - Sync tries to push. This will fail if we aren’t fully connected, or if the remote has diverged — i.e., the usual failure modes of `git push`. If it succeeds, then sync succeeds and our state is converged between local and remote.
  - If sync fails, then local and remote have diverged, which is a state we can indicate to the user. There are a few ways to resolve this: (a) We go through a Merge task to unify them; (b) We decide that everything we’re doing right now is going to be a new branch, and we sync *that*; or (c) we just stay in this diverged state for now, which is eg common if we’re disconnected.
  - If we become reconnected, we try a sync immediately, which might bring us back into the fully synced or diverged state.

This pattern means that we’re always either (a) dirty with a nontrivial working tree, which is fixed by sync; (b) diverged with a clean working tree but not synced to origin, which is fixed by the merge flow; or (c) fully clean and synced. 

And then we can really structure the merge flow cleanly.

(We don’t need to update the doc immediately — we can discuss how we want these flows to work and then do one big update)

**Assistant:** Holding off on the doc. Thinking it through, your first note has a bigger consequence than it looks.

**Yes to core-internal autosave — and I'd go further: the shell shouldn't send data at all.** Your argument settles it: unsaved-in-app changes are invisible to us and unhelpable, so "not written to disk" *is* "nothing changed," and `git status` is the complete signal. Reads genuinely don't matter.

But I'd keep an external *tick*, for a reason that isn't about information: **Android owns when work is allowed to happen.** A timer thread inside the core is fine on macOS and fights the platform on Android, where background execution is gated and git I/O needs to run under WorkManager. So the split is *core owns all policy and state; shell owns permission-to-run*:

```rust
fn tick() -> Vec<CoreEvent>;      // no arguments — just "you may work now"
```

The core walks its clones, checks status, decides what to commit and sync. Optional accelerators, purely for latency: `notify_connectivity(reachable)` to retry immediately rather than waiting a poll, and `notify_changed(project)` from a file watcher. Neither is required for correctness.

**Now the consequence.** If offline just means "sync fails to push and we're ahead of origin," then **the disconnect branch stops earning its keep.** Its whole job was keeping offline work from tangling with main — but "ahead" already is that state, divergence is divergence regardless of whether the local commits happened online or off, and the merge work at reconnect is identical either way. The branch adds a name and a step, no information.

Notice you'd already half-made this move: your resolution option (b) is *"we decide that everything we're doing right now is going to be a new branch."* That's **elective** branching, chosen by a human at the moment of divergence, not automatic branching triggered by a dropped wifi signal. I think that's the right model and the automatic one should go. It amends `solution.md` decision 7 — the checkpoint half ("one mechanism, several triggers") survives intact; the branching half dissolves because one of its two triggers turns out to be unnecessary. It also means we don't need proactive disconnect detection at all, only optional reconnect acceleration.

**On sync — agreed, and it absorbs `commit`.** An explicit titled checkpoint is just `sync(title: Some(...))`; a heartbeat one is `sync(title: None)`. Same operation, and "it's the save button" is exactly right. One refinement: sync itself should be unconditional, but the *heartbeat* should only call it after the tree has been **quiescent** for a threshold — otherwise autocommit can catch a file mid-write from an app that's still saving. So the quiescence policy lives in the tick loop, and sync stays dumb.

**On the state model — I'd split your three into four, plus an error state.** Your (b) folds together two conditions with opposite implications:

| | Meaning | Who fixes it |
|---|---|---|
| **Dirty** | uncommitted changes | sync, automatically |
| **Ahead** | local commits not pushed — offline, or held by policy | nobody; resolves itself on reconnect |
| **Diverged** | both sides moved | a human, via the merge flow |
| **Synced** | converged | — |
| **Blocked** | push failed for a reason that is neither | a human, differently |

Ahead and Diverged both mean "local ≠ remote," but flagging a plane flight as needing attention would be wrong, and it would train you to ignore the flag. *Behind*-only isn't a resting state — sync fast-forwards it away.

**Blocked** is the one I'd add that isn't in your list: push can fail from expired auth, a deleted repo, rate limiting, or branch protection. None of those are "offline" and none are "diverged," and if they collapse into either, an expired token looks like being permanently on a plane. That's the failure I'd least like to discover a week late.

**And now the merge flow, which does structure cleanly — but I2 forces a specific shape.** If the working tree is never left conflicted, we can't use git's in-tree merge and let you resolve at leisure. Instead:

1. Fetch; compute base, ours (local HEAD), theirs (origin).
2. For each differing path, run the ladder **on blobs, in scratch space** — the working tree is never touched.
3. Rungs 1–2 resolve silently; the rest accumulate as `ConflictSet`s.
4. Present them on a capable device; unresolvable ones simply stay pending.
5. When all are resolved, write **one merge commit** and push.

Three things fall out. **You can keep working during a merge**, since your tree is untouched — though if HEAD moves before you finish, the affected paths need recomputing. **Partial progress is resumable**: a half-finished `Resolution` keyed by the two commit ids is machine-local scratch, safe to persist because it's derived and recomputable. And **the merge is atomic from the tree's perspective** — it either lands whole or hasn't started.

**Two things I'd flag before we write this down.** Sync means "commit everything in the working tree," which is exactly right for a notebook and questionable for an adopted source repo, where it'll happily commit build artifacts and half-finished edits if `.gitignore` is incomplete — another argument for the conservative adopted-repo default. And `close` now simplifies to *quit apps, then sync*, which is one fewer bespoke sequence.

Want me to fold all of this in, including dropping automatic disconnect branching?

**User:** I like this framing. Your note about this sync logic being wrong for a source project is a good one — so what if we just made “autosave” (i.e. sync on idle) a per-project option that we can control from a setting? You might want to turn that off for a source repo but leave it on for one that works more like docs, it’s really a different rhythm of work.

OTOH, one nice thing about autosave is that the commits it creates are marked differently from ones created by the user, so the equivalent of “go back to the previous commit” is “go back to the previous *human* commit,” which is still an easy and well-defined action. Your autosaves are simply committed in a way that could be easily dropped.

This would suggest we might in the future want a few other operations, like “squash all automatic commits that have a human commit ahead of them” and “rewind to the last human commit.” (In fact, we probably want a “rewind” command to be in the core’s API, because that’s one of the places where git really shines and we can now turn that into a user-facing feature — this would only work if your tree was clean, of course)

**Assistant:** **The setting you want already exists — it's `checkpoint_policy`.** `ExplicitOnly` *is* autosave-off; `AutoCommitLocalOnly` is autosave-on-don't-push; `AutoCommitAndPush` is the docs rhythm. One axis, three values, no new knob.

But your framing is the better one and I'd rewrite D12 around it: the policy is about **rhythm of work**, and "adopted shared repo" is merely one reason to pick a conservative value. Source repos want deliberate commits because that's how source work feels; a shared repo wants it because pushing would be rude. Same setting, two different reasons, and rhythm is the one that generalizes.

**On rewind — I think this is the feature that makes implicit checkpointing earn its keep, and it needs one constraint to be safe.**

Right now autosaves are insurance you hope never to use. With rewind they become a time machine, which is a categorically better thing to have. But: **rewind must be non-destructive.** Not `reset --hard` — that discards the commits after the target, which directly contradicts the promise the whole checkpoint design rests on ("every intermediate state is still there"). Instead, restore the past tree state *as a new commit*. History grows; nothing is lost.

That has a nice second-order effect: rewind becomes **casually safe**. You can offer "go back to how this was an hour ago" as a light action rather than a scary one, because rewinding a rewind is just another rewind. A destructive version would need confirmation dialogs and would still occasionally ruin someone's day.

And rather than refusing when the tree is dirty, **rewind should sync first** — that's I1 (work is always committed somewhere), and it means rewind is always available rather than conditionally greyed out. The current state gets preserved as a commit on the way past.

Two additions I'd make:

- **A timeline, not just "last human commit."** The tagging gives you human commits as landmarks and autosaves as ticks between them — which is a scrubber. `timeline(project) -> [{commit, kind, title, timestamp, device}]`, with `device` included because a multi-device history is otherwise confusing to read.
- **File-scoped rewind.** "Restore *this file* to yesterday" is probably wanted more often than whole-project rewind, and it's the same operation with a path filter.

One guard: rewinding while **Diverged** makes the subsequent merge genuinely confusing. Resolve first, then rewind.

**On squashing — I'd push back, and I think tagging already won this argument.**

Squashing is history rewriting. Once autosaves have been pushed, squashing means force-push, and your *other device* has those commits in its local clone — so it wakes up diverged from a rewritten remote, which is one of the nastier states in git and exactly what this design has been working to avoid. That's a real risk.

Against that, what's the benefit? Repo size (text deltas, negligible) and log noise — but **log noise is precisely what the tagging already solved.** If the log reads cleanly because the view filters by tag, the autosaves cost you nothing but bytes. You said it yourself: they're committed in a way that could easily be dropped. I'd argue they're better *not* dropped, since dropping is the only irreversible thing in the design.

If you want it anyway, the safe subset is **squash unpushed autosaves only** — nobody else has seen them, so no rewriting anyone observes. That gets most of the tidiness with none of the multi-device hazard.

**And one thing the human/auto distinction gives you for free that neither of us has mentioned.**

Autosave density is an *observed* record of where your attention actually went. The registry has states you set by hand — active, back-burner, archived — but the checkpoint stream knows the truth. The gap between "I marked this active" and "there hasn't been a checkpoint in six weeks" is **exactly the graveyard problem** from the problem statement: projects dying of neglect rather than decision. The dashboard could surface that gap without you having to notice it yourself, which is closer to the actual thing Portal is for than a manually-maintained status field is.

That's free from a mechanism built for another reason, which is usually a sign it's the right mechanism.

We've now got two turns of undecided material — the tick loop, dropping disconnect branching, the five-state model, the scratch-space merge flow, rewind, and the policy reframe. Want me to do the big documentation pass now?
