**On `extract` — you caught something worse than a naming problem, and it's worth stating plainly.** The claim that cross-project search and the TODO unified view were one mechanism was wrong, and the *way* it was wrong is instructive: search has a **generic consumer** (file → text plus location, queried by something that needn't know what a TODO is), while the TODO view's consumer is **the integration itself**. Urgency rules, due dates, hierarchy, archive filtering — TODO semantics and nothing else's.

And the consequence I'd missed until you pushed: that design **put the urgency policy in Portal's core**, which is exactly the content-type knowledge D3 says the shell must not have. The abstraction smuggled TODO semantics in while appearing to keep them out. I'd even written "the urgency policy must not drift" as a *justification* for putting it there, when the no-drift property comes free anyway — an integration's core half is itself shared code, so the rules live in one place either way. Just the right one.

The general lesson is now in `notes.md`: **an abstraction covering two cases isn't validated by covering them, only by covering them for the same reason.** Two cases that fit the same shape for different reasons will diverge the moment either grows.

`list_projects` was already in the core (under Registry), but framed as a detail while `aggregate` was the sanctioned path — so it was effectively hidden. It's now the promoted route, and nothing is lost in safety: `read` is a core function returning bytes, so integrations still never see credentials.

**On the git filesystem — the name did real work.** It validated the API's existing shape rather than changing it (filesystem surface + version operations + sync operations), and it clarified the module split: `gitfs/` with `registry/` and `types/` as clients. I wrote the non-POSIX caveats in alongside it, since the name invites expectations that are all wrong — and flagged that Android's `DocumentsProvider` is where that boundary will actually be tested. It should expose a *view*, not pretend to be a filesystem.

**On restart state — you're right, and the exception is narrower than it first looks.** Almost everything is derivable: clone presence from the filesystem, branch and dirtiness from git, and ahead/behind/diverged by comparing against the **remote-tracking ref** that `.git` already holds from the last fetch. The one thing that isn't is *failure* — "push failed because the token expired" appears nowhere in git state, so **Blocked** would vanish across a restart and reappear only on the next failed attempt. Hence a machine-local **sync journal**, with one constraint that keeps it honest: **losing it must be harmless** (projects read `Unknown` until the next tick). That's what stops it becoming a second source of truth alongside git. I also added `as_of`, since "diverged, as of two hours ago" and "diverged, just checked" shouldn't render identically.

**On the moving-HEAD case — your version is simpler than mine and I've replaced it.** No partial-state protocol: land the merge, recompute status the ordinary way, and if you're diverged again, merge again. Resolutions are keyed by blob so unchanged sides stay valid. "The state is whatever git says after the merge lands" is the same rule as everywhere else, which is the point.

`Rhythm` → `AutosaveMode { Continuous, LocalOnly, Off }`, with D19's framing about work rhythm kept as the guidance for choosing.

**On `tick`** — agreed, and it's now a §1 dispatcher that components register handlers with, with the filesystem as its first subscriber. The two other subscribers I can already name are v2 search indexing and the neglect-signal recomputation, so binding it to the filesystem would have meant either duplicating the scheduling contract or routing unrelated work through it.

**On metadata** — you're right that `set_state` was gesturing at this without a read side or a type. Writing it out surfaced that **three confusable names were sharing space**, so I've split them:

- **`ProjectMetadata`** — durable config, YAML, in the *project repo*: roles, autosave mode, active FileSet. A project is now self-describing — adopt it on a second machine and it already knows what it is.
- **`RegistryEntry`** — in Home, one small file per project: identity, remote, lifecycle state. Just enough to render the dashboard without reading every project.
- **`ProjectStatus`** — live, computed, never stored.

Plus `LifecycleState` (active/back-burner/archived/pinned) named apart from `SyncState` (dirty/ahead/diverged/synced/blocked). The rule for what goes where: things true of the project *everywhere* travel with it; things needed to *list* projects live in the registry. And your "your own damned problem" is written in as an explicit non-goal — whole-file RMW, no concurrency defense, merges as ordinary text if two devices ever touch it.

**On FileSet — this one paid off more than expected.** Since a FileSet *is* a Collection (D4) and a Collection is a file type with its own integration, **`open` on a project's FileSet is just the Collection integration's `open` facet**, iterating entries. So there's no separate "open a project" facet at all: the observation from several turns back that opening a project and opening a file are "the same verb at different scales" stops being an analogy and becomes literally one operation. Your directory point is what forced the entry type to be honest — `File | Directory | Url` plus an app override, since a directory has no default handler and "open VSCode at the project root" needs to name VSCode.

**On packaging — the honest answer is that the toolchains win.** Co-locating `todo/core`, `todo/mac`, `todo/android` reads far better and fights Cargo, Xcode, and Gradle simultaneously; each wants a homogeneous tree. So: language-split code, plus **`integrations.toml` as the thing that makes an integration one unit** rather than three that share a name — declaring each type's roles, extensions, and which facets each platform implements.

The reason that's worth having isn't documentation, it's **checkability**. A test asserts the manifest matches reality and reports the empty cells. Without it, "Android never got a TODO merge UI" is something you learn from a user-visible deferral months later; with it, the type × facet × platform matrix is one screen and a gap is a deliberate entry. It also makes D3's warning concrete — that matrix is the main lever on the project's size, so a short type list is a requirement rather than taste.

Two smaller things that fell out: **widgets never cross UniFFI** (they're UI-framework types), so the core never sees them and each shell keeps its own map. But the core *does* need to know what a shell can resolve, for deferral — so shells report capabilities at startup, as the union of *facets we shipped* and *what the OS can open*. That reconciles the runtime-OS-query idea with the shipped-implementation reality; both were true of different type tiers.

Queue is unchanged in priority: **`ConflictSet`**, then the **native format specs** (now including `ProjectMetadata` and `FileSet` schemas), then the **TODO cross-project logic** as the proving case for the integration surface. **T5** is still the cheapest falsification.

New file: **`solution/integration-todo.md`** — the full design, doubling as the worked example of what an integration is. And you were right that it would surface issues elsewhere; it found three.

**1. The integration surface was missing a primitive.** `list_projects` + `read` isn't enough — TODO must resolve *where* each project's list lives, which lives in `ProjectMetadata.roles`. So `get_metadata` belongs in the sanctioned surface. Small, but exactly what a proving case is for, and invisible from the abstract interface.

**2. Acting across projects needs a write path we hadn't designed — and there's a nice option.** Ticking an item off from the unified view means writing to a project that may not be cloned, but `write` requires a clone. Cloning a whole repo to tick one checkbox is a poor trade on a phone. The alternative: **GitHub's contents API commits a single file server-side, with the blob SHA as an optimistic-concurrency precondition** — so it either applies cleanly or fails and you fall back to cloning. That would let the phone act across forty projects while holding none.

I've written it as a *proposal* rather than folding it in, since it adds a write path that bypasses local merge. It's safe (the precondition makes it fail rather than clobber), but it's a real change to §2.10 and I'd want your assent. Without it, the unified view is read-only for uncloned projects — worth saying out loud rather than discovering in use.

**3. "Order must never be meaningful" was too strong**, and following it would have caused a real annoyance. Canonicalising order on every write means Portal silently rewriting arrangements you made deliberately. The precise version (**D28**): **order carries user meaning but not system meaning** — the file preserves your arrangement, merge just never *depends* on position, matching by ID and combining two orders with a deterministic list-merge. Determinism comes from the algorithm rather than from canonicalisation, which is what T6 actually needs.

Two smaller things the format work settled: **timestamps must live in the file here**, unlike the notebook's commit-derived ordering — an item's status can change several times within one commit, and `git blame` is fragile under re-indentation. And **deletion loses to modification**, so an item deleted on one side and edited on the other survives and gets flagged.

**On the roster (D27) — the answer to "how do we support PDF, PNG, and source types" is that we don't have to.**

Five integrations total, three nearly free: TODO, notebook, FileSet, generic text, generic binary. Source types need *no declaration anywhere* — type resolution is total, so valid UTF-8 lands on generic text. PDF and PNG are generic binary, opened by the OS and merged by display-and-pick, which for documents and images is genuinely the right interaction rather than a fallback.

That gives a rule worth holding to, since D26 made the cost concrete: **a new integration must justify itself against the generic behaviour it would replace.** "It's a distinct file type" isn't a justification; "the generic behaviour is wrong for it" is. A PNG integration might eventually earn its place with a side-by-side image comparison — but not before.

`ConflictSet` moved to deferred with the reasoning attached, so it doesn't read as forgotten.

Next, if you want to keep going in this direction: **notebook and FileSet to the same depth** — both are much smaller, and FileSet is load-bearing since the project manifest is one. Or the **remote write-through decision**, which is small but gates how useful the phone actually is.

**Assistant:** Three good ones. The archival design in particular has a property worth naming.
All three in, as **D29**–**D31**.

**On lenient dates** — writing it up surfaced a bug I'd have shipped. **The default time has to be field-specific**: a bare `@due(2026-09-01)` means *end* of that day, and defaulting it to midnight makes everything due today instantly overdue. `@touched` defaults to start-of-day (past event, only needs rough ordering), and `@today` needs no time at all since it's a date match by definition.

It also forced a rule I'd been assuming without stating: **serialization must round-trip untouched lines byte-for-byte**. A read-modify-write rewrites the whole file, so without that, one edit normalizes every hand-typed date in the list and buries a small change in a large diff. Worth noting the churn is harmless for *merge* either way — structural merge compares parsed fields, so `2026-09-01` and `2026-09-01T23:59Z` are one value — but it's not harmless for a diff a human has to read. Generalized to all native formats as D31, since it applies equally to the notebook and FileSet.

**On archival — your design has a property worth naming explicitly.** Writing a **new dated file per run** (`todo-archive/YYMMDD.md`) makes archival **merge-free by construction**. It's the notebook's day-file trick applied again: two devices archiving produce different filenames and can never append-conflict, where a shared `todo-archive.md` would conflict every time two diverged devices archived. That's now in `notes.md` as a recurring move to reach for whenever a design wants multiple devices appending to one file.

The frozen-ancestor copy is the right call over the simpler alternative (an ancestor breadcrumb string), precisely because it keeps the IDs — which is what makes unarchiving reattach rather than guess. Reading the archive unions the files and dedupes by ID, preferring a real archived item over a frozen copy.

I added two conditions to make the awkward case nearly unreachable: archive only items **terminal beyond a threshold** (30 days, to tune) and only while the project is **Synced**. If a device archives something another concurrently modified, "deletion loses to modification" keeps it live with a stray archive copy — untidy, not lossy, self-correcting — but restricting to long-dead items in synced projects means it essentially never happens. That's a nice use of the state model: a race removed for free by checking a state we already compute.

**On `write_through`** — your framing is better than mine and I've adopted it. Calling it *the dual of `read`* is exactly right: `read` already resolves clone-or-remote, and this is the write side of that. Two things I'd add:

- **Explicit, never transparent.** Your "you'd only want this if you're doing something really explicit and cross-repo" is the design constraint. Making it something `write` resolves silently would invite it into code that should be doing an ordinary read-modify-write.
- **With a clone present, it routes locally.** Otherwise a server-side commit leaves you Behind your own working copy — an easy trap, avoided by having the explicit call still do the right thing in both cases.

Two honest caveats recorded: it's a *host* capability rather than a git one (would need reimplementing off GitHub), and the contents API caps files around 1 MB — fine for a TODO list, not for a manuscript.

**T5 — "Does the Rust core actually cross-compile and bind?"**

Build the chosen git library plus UniFFI for `aarch64-apple-darwin` and `aarch64-linux-android`, call a function from both Swift and Kotlin, and do an authenticated HTTPS clone → commit → push against a private GitHub repo from each side.

*Passes if* a full round trip works from both shells. *If it fails*, the whole shared-core strategy needs revisiting — not just the git library.

**Why it's the one I keep pointing at:** it tests the single most load-bearing decision in the architecture. Everything in `software-architecture.md` §2 — the git filesystem, the tick loop, `sync`, the merge ladder — assumes a Rust core with real Swift and Kotlin bindings on both platforms. If that assumption breaks, the fallback isn't "pick a different git library," it's reconsidering the shared-core approach entirely, which would invalidate a large fraction of what we've designed.

TLS is the likely sharp edge: `gix` uses `rustls` throughout, while `git2` means juggling a TLS backend per platform, and Android's NDK toolchain is where cross-compilation problems usually surface.

The full set, for reference:

| | |
|---|---|
| **T1** | Can we switch to an arbitrary Space with public APIs? |
| **T2** | Is Mission Control's "+" reachable through the Accessibility API? |
| **T3** | Can we observe windows without a Screen Recording grant? |
| **T4** | Does `gix` support the merge we actually need — including base/ours/theirs access for scratch-space merges? |
| **T5** | Does the Rust core cross-compile and bind, with authenticated HTTPS from both shells? |
| **T6** | Property-test the type-aware merge rules (not a spike — the highest-value correctness work) |
| **T7** | Does the panel keep capture at "gesture, type, enter"? |

T1–T3 all bear on Q2 (Space automation), T4–T5 on Q3 (git library). T6 and T7 aren't feasibility questions — T6 is correctness work listed there so it doesn't get treated as ordinary unit testing, and T7 is the condition that deferring the MessageBox rests on.

## The queue

**Bytes-on-disk decisions — cheap now, migrations later**
- Name the identifiers that end up in file paths and committed content: `ProjectId` (it's the filename in `registry/<project-id>.toml` — slug, UUID, or `owner/repo`?), `ItemId` (alphabet, length, collision behavior), `RoleId`/`TypeId` (open strings or closed enum — UniFFI will care).
- Native format specs: notebook, TODO, collection, registry records. Source material is `02 file formats.md`; now also has to carry D33's ordering tiebreak.
- Specify `ProjectMetadata` and `FileSet` — the YAML schema for roles, autosave mode, active FileSet, directory targets, app overrides.

**API-shaping decisions that want settling before the code exists**
- Design the machine-local store. Three unsynced things, only two named: the sync journal and the Space↔project map. The third — `ProjectId` → clone path — is undesigned, and it's the one whose loss *isn't* harmless: losing the journal degrades gracefully by construction (D21), losing the clone map orphans working copies holding unpushed work.
- Decide how a permanently deferred conflict gets unstuck. Rung 4 as a user-invocable escape, or automatic past a threshold. This shapes the merge API, so it's before-not-after.
- Write the integration interface and `integrations.toml`, then port notebook and TODO onto it as the proving cases.

**Then buildable**
- Headless dev CLI over the core, and T6 property testing.
- Design the notebook and FileSet integrations to `integration-todo.md`'s depth (needs the interface first).
- Android TLS trust — blocks all Android networking, but nothing before Android work.

**UI cluster, gated on one spike:** panel/widget-hosting contract, adoption UX, timeline/rewind, neglect signal. These are four items but really one substrate, and T7 hasn't run.

**Spikes still unrun:** T1–T3 (Space automation), T6, T7.

**User:** Sounds good, let’s discuss that. For project IDs, I think that using the git repo name (owner and repo) is the best choice because (a) these are guaranteed by github to be unique and (b) this produces great human readability and ease of switching between Portal and directly mucking about with git whenever needed. Any reason not to do this?

**Assistant:** Three objections. Two are papercuts, one I think is decisive.

**The decisive one: not every project has an `owner/repo`.** D12 supports adopting a plain directory — `git init`, then scaffold — and D23's `AutosaveMode::LocalOnly` is a designed, supported configuration where a project never gets a remote at all. Those projects need a ProjectId from the moment they're registered, and there's nothing to derive one from. You'd need a second ID scheme for them, and then a migration when one later gains a remote — which is a rename of the identity, arriving through the exact path the next objection is about.

**The serious one: `owner/repo` is mutable, and this is a join key.** Repos get renamed as projects find their name; ownership transfers are rarer but real. GitHub redirects the URL, which is why this feels safe, but the identity underneath has changed. ProjectId isn't just the registry filename — it's the key into the clone-path map, the Space↔project map, and the sync journal, on two devices that have to agree. Rename on the Mac and the phone's registry still holds the old file: the merge sees a delete plus an add, which is the worst-shaped change in this system and the thing D30 went out of its way to avoid. Recoverable by hand, since it's all plain text and the project's actual content is untouched — annoying rather than catastrophic. But making it correct means a `renamed_from` field, a merge rule that recognizes the pair, and fixups across three machine-local stores. That's more machinery than the alternative costs.

Also worth noting on (a): GitHub's uniqueness guarantee is point-in-time, not historical. A freed name can be reused. Unlikely to bite one user, but it's a weaker guarantee than it looks.

**The papercuts:** GitHub is case-insensitive-preserving, macOS's filesystem is case-insensitive, and Android's is not — so `Zunger/Portal` and `zunger/portal` are one repo, one file, and two files respectively. Needs canonicalizing to lowercase or it's a real two-device bug. And the slash makes ProjectId a path *fragment* rather than a filename component, so anywhere it needs to be a single segment (an Android intent extra, a Space-map key) it needs escaping.

## What's right in the instinct, and where to put it

Point (b) is correct and worth preserving deliberately — but I'd separate where it applies. The place you actually muck about with git by hand is the *project clone*, and its path absolutely should be `owner/repo`. The Home repo's `registry/*.toml` is Portal's own bookkeeping; you read it to debug, not to work. So most of the ergonomic win survives an opaque ID, and it lands where you'd actually feel it.

This is also a tradeoff the architecture has already made once. D28: TODO items match by stable ID precisely so that the human-meaningful parts — order, text, hierarchy — stay free to change without breaking the join. Same shape here. A name that people read will get changed; a key that machines join on must not. One string can't be both, and the notes already flag "take a generalization only as far as it deletes concepts" — `owner/repo`-as-ID doesn't delete the rename problem, it just leaves it unhandled until it fires.

**Proposal:** ProjectId is a short opaque slug — lowercase alphanumeric, ~10 characters, generated at creation, never changes. `owner/repo` becomes a field on `RegistryEntry`, used for display everywhere and for the clone path. Renaming a repo then updates one field in one file, and nothing joins on it. As a bonus, ItemId is the next identifier on the list, so settling the alphabet and length once covers both.

**The alternative,** if you weight registry readability higher than I'm weighting it: a user-chosen slug at creation (`portal`, `ncii-paper`), decoupled from GitHub so there's less pressure to change it. You get readable registry filenames and stability — but uniqueness becomes the user's problem, and I'd expect the urge to rename it when a project's name changes, which walks back into the same trap with fewer guardrails.

Want me to write this up as a decision, or does the remoteless-project case suggest something different to you?

Notably, **a project's own repo never contains its ProjectId**: `ProjectMetadata` holds `name`, `roles`, `autosave`, `active` and no identity field, by design — the project is self-describing but doesn't know what Portal calls it. And TODO links are "ordinary markdown inline in the text" (§ format), so no structured cross-project reference exists anywhere in content either.

## Tier 2 — machine-local, unsynced

3. The **clone-path map** (`ProjectId` → path) — still undesigned, queue item.
4. The **Space↔project map** (Mac only).
5. The **sync journal** — one record per project.

All rebuildable in principle; D21 already requires losing the journal to be harmless. The clone map is the one whose loss actually costs something.

## Tier 3 — API surface only, never persisted

~25 core functions take `&ProjectId` (`sync`, `read`, `write`, `write_through`, `status`, `timeline`, `rewind`, all six branch ops, `get_metadata`, `set_metadata`, `set_lifecycle`, …), four return one (`create_project`, `adopt_directory`, `adopt_repository`, `observeCurrentContext`), and the TODO integration's six domain ops carry it through. These cost nothing whatever the representation — it's a parameter type.

## What the enumeration changes

**I over-weighted the rename problem.** Since the ID is durably written in exactly one place, a rename under `owner/repo` is: one file renamed in the Home repo (a delete+add merge if two devices disagree) plus re-keying three local maps. Real, bounded, recoverable — not the scattered mess I implied. I'd downgrade that from "serious" to "a papercut with a sharp edge."

**But the remoteless case survives untouched, and it's worse than I put it.** `RegistryEntry` already declares `remote: Option<String>`. The written architecture *already says* a project may have no remote. Deriving identity from `owner/repo` doesn't just fail an edge case — it contradicts a field type that's already on the page. Either that `Option` is wrong, or `owner/repo` can't be the ID.

**And the cost of an opaque ID is now precisely measurable: one unreadable filename.** Not "the registry is unreadable" — the file's *contents* still carry `name` and `remote` in plain text, `list_projects` is what renders the dashboard, and finding a project by hand in a directory of small TOMLs is one grep. Meanwhile the clone path — the thing you actually `cd` into when mucking about with git — is free to be `owner/repo` regardless.

So the trade is: one opaque filename, against a scheme that contradicts an existing optional field and needs a rename protocol. I'd still take the opaque ID, but on much narrower grounds than I first argued — the readability loss is nearly nil, not that the rename cost is large.

One thing worth deciding alongside it: whether `RegistryEntry.id` should exist at all, given it duplicates the filename. Storing it makes the file self-contained if it's ever moved or copied; omitting it removes a way for the two to disagree. I'd lean toward keeping it and having the loader treat a mismatch as the filename winning.

**User:** Well, let me add a further twist: What information lives in `registry/<project-id>.toml`? And what would happen if instead we had a *single* registry.toml file?

**Assistant:** ## What's actually in it

Four fields. `id`, `name`, `remote: Option<String>`, `lifecycle`. About 150 bytes:

```toml
id = "…"
name = "Portal"
remote = "git@github.com:yonatanzunger/portal.git"
lifecycle = "active"
```

The split rule (§4) is "things true of the project everywhere travel with the project; things the dashboard needs to *list* projects live in the registry." So this file is deliberately tiny — it exists to render a dashboard without reading every project.

## What happens with a single `registry.toml`

The original rationale (architecture.md, "Project registry") was: it'd be the most contended file in the system, and *"structured formats merge especially badly, since a botched three-way merge on JSON yields a syntactically invalid file rather than a marked-up one."*

**That argument is now largely obsolete, and I think it was already obsolete before you asked.** It's an argument about *git's generic line merge*. Portal doesn't use git's generic merge on its own types — and D1 names the registry explicitly: "structural field-level merge for TODO items **and registry records**." The registry is already a declared native type with its own merge rule. Under structural merge keyed by project, two devices adding different projects produce two new records and union cleanly; two devices changing lifecycle on different projects touch disjoint records. That is the *same* conflict profile per-file layout gives you. One buys isolation from the filesystem, the other from an algorithm we've already committed to building.

The contention argument also doesn't survive scale-checking: a single user creates projects maybe monthly and changes lifecycle rarely. It's the most contended file in the registry, which is a near-zero baseline. The TODO file changes orders of magnitude more often and is happily one file per project.

**What one file buys, beyond that:**

- **ProjectId stops being a filename.** No slash-as-path-fragment problem. No case-sensitivity divergence between macOS and Android — it's a string compared by one parser under one rule, not by two filesystems under two.
- **Readability, better than readable filenames gave you.** `cat registry.toml` shows every project at once. That's the thing you actually wanted from `owner/repo`, and a directory of readable filenames is a worse version of it.
- **One blob fetch on mobile instead of N.** This one's underrated. The phone often has no clone of Home, so per-file means listing a directory via API then fetching N blobs — and that compounds with the cross-project view's existing per-project fan-out. One file is one fetch.

**What it costs — one real thing:** blast radius. A botched merge or a bad hand-edit takes out *every* project's record, not one. And lifecycle state is the one field in there that isn't derivable from anywhere else — you could rebuild `name` and `remote` by scanning GitHub and local clones, but active/back-burner/archived/pinned is genuine user data that exists nowhere else. Git history is the backstop, as always, so "lost" means "recover from history," not "gone." Still, it's a real downgrade: merge-free-*by-construction* traded for merge-correct-*by-algorithm*.

## The synthesis this suggests

Make it record-oriented and lenient, and the tradeoff mostly dissolves. If one project is one record with independent parsing — the same discipline D31 already sets for native formats, parse leniently and round-trip untouched records byte-for-byte — then an unparseable record loses one project and is preserved verbatim for the user to fix, rather than invalidating the file. Per-record blast radius, single-file readability.

And that reopens the identity question in a better place. With a single file you can have both halves of what you wanted:

- The **record key is the human name** — `owner/repo` when there is one, freely renameable, right there when you open the file.
- A **stable opaque `^id` inside the record** is what merge joins on and what the three machine-local maps key against.

That's D28 applied one level up: the name carries user meaning, the ID carries system meaning, and neither has to pretend to be the other. Renaming a repo becomes a one-line edit that merges as a field change. The remoteless project just has no `owner/repo` and shows under its `name`. The slash and case problems never arise because nothing is a filename.

Cost: one more field, and `list_projects` returns both. Compared to a rename protocol plus a second ID scheme for remoteless projects, that's cheap.

**One thing I'd resist,** flagging it because it'll be tempting when we write the format: making the registry an instance of Collection (D4). It looks like one — an ordered set of references with per-entry metadata. But D20's lesson says an abstraction covering two cases is only validated if it covers them *for the same reason*, and a Collection exists to be `open`ed while the registry exists to be indexed. Different consumer, different reason. Worth checking deliberately rather than assuming.

**Assistant:** Let me check two things in the TODO integration before answering — the archive filename scheme and what §9 already says about IDs.
The reasoning is right in shape, and the scoping instinct is the important part — but there's a missing term in the formula, and I think the model itself is the wrong one for this system.

## The missing term

`2·log2(n)` is the point where a collision becomes *likely*, not where it becomes unlikely. That's the birthday bound's expected-first-collision point, which sits at roughly 50%. Running your own numbers:

- 64k items in 32 bits: p ≈ (2¹⁶)²/(2·2³²) = **50%**
- 600 items in 20 bits: p ≈ 600²/(2·2²⁰) = 360,000/2,097,152 ≈ **17%**

So the month-scoped version isn't "highly unlikely," it's a coin flip every few months. The corrected form needs the target failure probability in it:

**bits ≈ 2·log₂(n) − 1 + log₂(1/p)**

For 600 items at one-in-a-million, that's 18.4 − 1 + 20 ≈ **38 bits**, not 20. The `log₂(1/p)` term is where all the safety lives, and dropping it is what makes 20 bits look sufficient.

(Worth noting: the example IDs already in `integration-todo.md` — `^k3f9`, `^m2p1` — are 4 base36 characters, almost exactly the 20 bits you derived. So the document currently encodes your estimate.)

## But the birthday model doesn't apply here

Birthday-bound reasoning assumes blind generation — you emit an ID with no ability to check. Portal is never in that position. It's writing an item into a file it has already parsed, so it can generate, check, and regenerate on collision. That reduces the *unavoidable* exposure to exactly one case: **items created independently on two devices during a divergence window**, where neither can see the other's IDs.

That n is small — call it 20 items per side in a realistic window. Expected collisions ≈ a·b/N:

- 20 bits: 400/2²⁰ ≈ **1 in 2,600** per divergence window
- 30 bits: 400/2³⁰ ≈ 1 in 2.7 million
- 40 bits: negligible

So under the model that actually governs, 30 bits would do.

## The failure mode is what should decide this

Here's the thing that pushes me toward generosity anyway. **A collision doesn't fail loudly — it fails silently, and it fails as data corruption.** Two genuinely different items sharing an ID don't look like a collision to structural merge; they look like *one item whose text and status disagree between the two sides*. Field-level merge will then do exactly what it's designed to do — take status from one side and text from the other — and produce a coherent-looking item that never existed, from two real items, one of which vanishes.

That is precisely the "silently corrupt content" risk the threat model ranks first, arriving through a side door. And the safety net doesn't catch it: git history preserves the pre-merge commits, but nobody will know to go looking, because nothing appeared to go wrong.

Given that, I'd choose the length that's safe under the **blind** model even though we'll implement the check — so that correctness doesn't depend on the check being right everywhere it's needed (hand-added items getting IDs assigned, archive files not loaded at generation time, a future integration reusing the scheme). Check-at-generation becomes defense in depth rather than load-bearing.

Per-project lifetime items, generously, ~20k. At 40 bits blind: 20,000²/(2·2⁴⁰) ≈ 1.8×10⁻⁴. At 30 bits: 0.18 — unacceptable. So **40 bits**.

**Concrete proposal:** Crockford base32 — digits plus consonants, excluding I, L, O, U. Exactly 5 bits per character, so 8 characters = 40 bits on the nose. The excluded letters are the ones that matter in a file people hand-edit: no 0/O or 1/l/I ambiguity when someone retypes an ID, and dropping U makes accidental profanity essentially impossible. Same alphabet and length for `ProjectId` — one convention, and 40 bits is vast overkill for a few hundred projects, but the id is a secondary field next to the human name (D35) so nothing is cramped by it.

The honest cost: `^k3f9q7m2` instead of `^k3f9`, four more characters on every line of a hand-editable file.

## Scope, which needs stating explicitly

`ItemId` must be unique across **one project's live TODO plus its entire archive** — not per-file, not global. That follows from D30: reading the archive unions its files and deduplicates by ID. Your instinct to bound it by the unarchive window is right about *why we keep the IDs*, but the dedup is what sets the uniqueness requirement, and it spans all archive files. Bounding it would mean scoping the dedup too, which costs more than the bits save.

## Other use cases — I found one, plus a category error

Sweeping the design: notebook day files use `YYMMDD-<words>.md` with no ID, and collisions there are benign by construction (two files for one date, both render). TODO archive files are `todo-archive/YYMMDD.md`, and same-day archival on two devices produces the same filename with *identical content*, because archival is deterministic given its two conditions — no disambiguator needed. Merge scratch is keyed by git's own commit oids. FileSet entries have no IDs and presumably join on target path, which is worth confirming when FileSet gets designed rather than assuming.

The one genuinely unnamed identifier: the sync journal carries a `device: String` field (§2.9). It's machine-local with n=2, so entropy is a non-question, but it's undesigned — worth folding into the machine-local store item.

**And `RoleId`/`TypeId` are a different kind of question entirely.** They're vocabulary — `notebook`, `todo`, `fileset` — not generated identifiers. Nothing about collision probability applies; the actual question is open strings versus a closed enum, which is about extensibility and what UniFFI can express. I'd keep it out of this decision so it doesn't get answered by analogy.

## The distinction

**`TypeId` selects an integration** — the code that knows how to handle a kind of content. It's a global namespace, one entry per integration, declared in `integrations.toml`. The v1 roster (D27) is exactly five: `todo`, `notebook`, `fileset`, `text`, `binary`. It answers *"what code handles this file?"*

**`RoleId` names a slot inside a project** — a purpose that some path fills. It's declared per-project in `ProjectMetadata.roles`, mapping role → path: `notebook -> "notebook/"`, `todo -> "todo.md"`. It answers *"what is this path **for** in this project?"*

They connect at type resolution (§1.2), whose first and highest-priority rule is: a path under a declared role takes that role's type. That's how the notebook and TODO list get identified *by where they are* rather than by sniffing their contents — which is what lets an adopted repo keep its own layout (D12) instead of having Portal's imposed on it.

## Why they look redundant, and where they aren't

In v1 the mapping is nearly the identity function: role `todo` → type `todo`, role `notebook` → type `notebook`. That's why they're confusable.

The case that justifies two concepts is D4's collections. A project can hold *several* FileSets — the `active` manifest that `open` launches, and, say, a research project's `bibliography` — and D4 is explicit that these are "distinguished by the role each is declared under rather than by being distinct types." So: **one type, many roles.** Roles are also where user extension lives — adoption "chooses where roles live," and a bibliography is a role a user invents, not one Portal ships.

That asymmetry mostly answers the question on its own: `TypeId` is closed by construction (it's the set of integrations we shipped, enumerable at build time, and D26's checkable facet matrix *requires* it be enumerable), while `RoleId` wants to be open.

## Two gaps this surfaces

**1. Open roles can't currently resolve to a type.** `TypeClaim` declares `roles: Vec<RoleId>` — "roles this type serves" — as a static list in `integrations.toml`. But an integration can't statically claim a role name a user invents. So if someone declares `bibliography -> refs.toml`, resolution step 1 finds no claiming type, falls through to extension/UTI, and a `.toml` bibliography lands on **generic text**. Wrong answer, silently.

Either roles are effectively closed to what integrations claim — contradicting D4 — or `ProjectMetadata.roles` has to carry the type alongside the path, something like `bibliography -> (refs.toml, fileset)`. The second is more consistent with D24's principle that a project is self-describing, and it would let `TypeClaim.roles` shrink to a scaffolding default or disappear entirely — one fewer mechanism.

**2. The registry needs a merge rule but isn't in the type roster.** D1 says structural field-level merge covers "TODO items *and registry records*," but D27's five integrations don't include a registry type. D34 sharpened this: the registry is now a single structurally-merged file, and the merge ladder dispatches by type. So either it's a sixth integration, or the core special-cases its own bookkeeping outside the integration surface — defensible, since it isn't user content, but it needs saying rather than being an omission. Home's "one reserved role" is presumably this, and it's never been named.

Neither gap is hard, but both change what the `RoleId`/`TypeId` decision has to say. Want to take them in order — settle the open/closed question first and let it dictate the schema, or work out the resolution path for user-declared roles first and see what it implies?

The only roles that are truly native to Portal are the files that it itself uses for things like the registry — e.g., the “active files” FileSet that `open` is ultimately going to surface in the Mac UI.

**Assistant:** That's the D3 test applied to a namespace rather than to code — "who owns this vocabulary?" turning out to be the same question as "who owns this behavior?" And I think it's right, with a sharper consequence than you may have intended.

**Check the two Portal-native roles you named — neither actually uses the role mechanism.**

The `active` FileSet is already an inline field on `ProjectMetadata` (`active: FileSet`, D25), not a role→path binding. And the registry, post-D34, is a fixed `registry.toml` at a known location in a reserved repo — it doesn't need a per-project path declaration because Home isn't a varying case. Home's "one reserved role," mentioned once in `architecture.md` and never named, looks like it may be machinery that was never needed.

So if the integration-owned roles move to their integrations, and Portal's own two turn out not to be roles at all, then **`RoleId` as a Portal-level namespace dissolves entirely.** What replaces it is narrower and more honest: *Portal stores a per-project binding on behalf of each integration, and doesn't know what any of them mean.* Portal is the registrar, not the owner. It knows that in this project, whatever `todo` is, it lives at `todo.md`.

**That kills the first gap outright.** The reason user-declared roles couldn't resolve to a type was that `TypeClaim.roles` is a static list an integration can't extend with names a user invents. Once the binding is keyed *by* type, resolution is a lookup with the answer already in the key — no `TypeClaim.roles` at all. Two concepts deleted rather than added, which is the test from `notes.md` for whether a generalization is earning its keep.

**What survives, and it's the interesting residual:** you still need a discriminator when one type has several bindings — D4's project holding both an `active` manifest and a `bibliography`, both FileSets. But that label is now scoped *under* the type rather than living in a global namespace, so `fileset:bibliography` can't collide with anything and carries its own resolution. The thing that was doing real work in `RoleId` was never the name; it was the type association, and that's now structural.

**The second gap is orthogonal and doesn't move.** The registry still needs a merge rule (D1 promises structural field-level merge for registry records) while not being in D27's five-integration roster. Restructuring roles doesn't answer whether the registry is a sixth integration or core-internal bookkeeping outside the integration surface. D34 made that more pressing, not less.

**One risk worth naming before we commit.** "Portal stores per-integration binding data it doesn't understand" is one short step from "Portal stores arbitrary per-integration config," which is the generic record store the architecture explicitly refused to build ("no manifest DSL and no generic record store"). The discipline that keeps it honest is keeping the binding to *paths only* — where your data lives, nothing else. If an integration wants settings, those belong in its own file in the project, where they merge under its own rules and cost Portal nothing.

The open question this leaves is what the binding's shape is: `Map<TypeId, Path>` is too thin for the notebook (a directory) plus TODO (a file plus an archive directory) plus multi-instance FileSets. Want to work that out, or first settle whether the registry is an integration — since if it is, it's a sixth binding and wants to fit the same shape?

If it knows that there’s a `.todo` file type and that the registered TODO integration handles it, that’s all it cares about.

One thing that becomes interesting in this case is that some of the logical “files” we want are actually directories. Checked `02 file formats.md` — and it confirms the case that forces one addition. The notebook is inherently a folder, and its day files are `.md`. Under pure extension typing they'd resolve to generic text and silently lose append-union merge.

So the rule you need is the one your FileSet example already implies, generalized: **containment in a typed bundle is itself a type-resolution rule.** A `whatever.notebook/` directory declares that `.md` files directly inside it are day files. That's precisely what the role rule was doing — "a path under a declared role takes that role's type" — except the declaration now travels with the directory name instead of living in per-project state. Same power, no metadata.

With that, yes: file types and their handlers are all you need.

## Two flavors of bundle, and the distinction does work

Your `.app` point is sharper than tidiness. It's the difference between:

- **Opaque bundles** (`.app`) — tag it, don't recurse, hand the whole directory to the handler. Merge is display-and-pick or keep-both at directory granularity. Essentially generic binary, one scale up.
- **Transparent bundles** (`.notebook`, `.fileset`) — recurse, but the bundle types its own contents. Its manifest gets a custom type; everything else merges by its own type, exactly as you described for FileSet's PDFs.

That's a real bit in the design and it should be declared, not inferred. Note it also answers "what does merge mean on a directory," which the merge ladder currently has no story for.

## What this deletes

`RoleId`, `TypeClaim.roles`, per-project bindings, and the "choose where roles live" step in the adoption flow. Plus a good signal: `resolve_type(project, path)` stops needing project metadata and becomes context-free — it only needs the path's own ancestry.

**And it resolves the registry gap for free.** The registry just becomes a file type. Check it against the facets: structural `merge3` ✓ (which is what D1 promised and D34 now depends on), domain operations ✓ (`list_projects`, `set_lifecycle`), and a widget — the dashboard's project picker. That's a complete integration, and it clears D27's bar easily, since generic text merge on the registry is exactly the outcome D34 needs to avoid. The roster goes to six, and Home's mysterious "one reserved role" disappears entirely.

## The honest costs

**Cross-project TODO lookup changes shape.** Today it's `get_metadata(p).roles["todo"]`. Now it's "find the `.todo` files," which forces a question roles had answered by fiat: what if there are three? Plausibly "all of them" — arguably better, since a project might legitimately want several lists — but it's a decision, not a detail. And `get_metadata`'s original justification evaporates; `integration-todo.md` records that it was added to the sanctioned surface *specifically* to resolve role→path.

**`ProjectMetadata` shrinks to almost nothing** — name and autosave mode, since the `active` FileSet could itself just be a `.fileset` in the project, found by name. Worth asking whether it still earns being a file.

**Renaming a file changes its type.** `todo.todo` → `todo.txt` silently becomes generic text and loses structural merge; under roles the binding survived renames. It's how every OS works and the loss is visible (the widget stops rendering it), so I'd accept it — but it's a genuine regression in robustness, and it's the one thing roles were better at.

**Adoption gets simpler rather than harder**, which is worth saying because it looks like a loss. An adopted repo keeps its layout automatically. And the flexibility roles seemed to offer was partly illusory: pointing Portal at an existing `TODO.md` still required that file to be in Portal's format, so it was never "adopt the todo list you already have."

**Downstream:** this changes the format specs materially — `todo.md` and `notebook/` become `something.todo` and `something.notebook/`. That's the queue item that was about to become transcription, so better to hit it now.

On the declaration shape: I agree with it, with one structural amendment and two smaller pushbacks.

## First, note what just happened to D13

D13 explicitly moved *away* from per-integration `claims` predicates to central resolution, and its stated reason was that "the notebook and TODO list are identified by *where they are* rather than by sniffing their contents, so a chain of per-integration predicates was the wrong shape." That premise was the role mechanism. We just deleted it — so the rationale expired, exactly as the registry-layout rationale did. Second time in one session; the `notes.md` entry about re-checking premises is earning its keep.

But D13 had *other* concerns worth preserving, and they argue for a hybrid rather than a straight return to predicates.

## The amendment: declaration is data, the predicate is a veto

A pure predicate chain loses three things:

- **Conflict detection.** If two integrations both claim `*.todo`, a predicate chain silently resolves by order. As data, it's a load-time error — which is what it actually is.
- **Cost.** Depth-first over a repo × N integrations is N predicate calls per path, each possibly touching content. Suffix matching is a hash lookup.
- **Checkability (D26).** The manifest's whole value is being statically inspectable. Code isn't.

So: **match declaratively, validate procedurally.** The declaration carries path patterns; the optional predicate can only *reject* a match, never claim one. That's exactly your `.app` case — identify by name, then optionally validate the directory structure — and it keeps everything above.

```toml
[notebook]
paths    = ["*.notebook"]
bundle   = "transparent"
contents = { "*.md" = "notebook" }        # what replaces the role rule
core     = ["merge3", "diff", "domain"]
mac      = ["open", "presentMerge", "widget"]

[app]
paths    = ["*.app"]
bundle   = "opaque"                        # tag it, don't recurse
validate = true                            # optional structure check, may reject
```

The `contents` table is the piece that has to be explicit, because it's what carries the notebook's day-file typing — the one thing roles were genuinely doing. It also stays declarative, where "`.md` directly inside a `.notebook`" as a predicate would not be checkable.

I'd also lean toward `bundle` being a static property rather than something the path check returns per-call, for the same checkability reason — unless you can think of a type that's sometimes opaque and sometimes not.

Your depth-first point holds and gives the right nesting semantics: pre-order traversal, check the directory before descending, **outermost wins**. A `.app` inside a `.fileset` stops at the app. Nested transparent bundles compose naturally.

## Two pushbacks

**The assigned unique ID may be solving the wrong problem.** If two integrations declare the same type name, that's a configuration bug — assigning them distinct IDs papers over it silently. Names must be unique in the manifest, which D26's checkable manifest already enforces, so the name *is* the unique identifier and a collision should be a load-time error. And nothing persists a TypeId: types are re-resolved from paths on whatever device is looking, so there's no cross-device or cross-run stability requirement that a numeric handle would satisfy. With a roster of six, I'd skip the indirection unless you see a use I'm missing.

**URL typing has no v1 consumer.** The only place URLs appear is `FileSetEntry::Url`, and URLs never merge — they're content Portal doesn't store — so URL typing feeds only shell-side `open` dispatch. Both platforms already route by scheme, and D3 says capabilities are a runtime query to the OS rather than a table we maintain. So it'd be a hook nothing calls. I'd leave the shape open for it and not build it, unless you're picturing a Portal-aware integration claiming particular URL patterns.

## First, the premise is weaker than D26 claims

D26 says co-locating "fights Cargo, Xcode, and Gradle simultaneously; each wants a homogeneous tree." That's overstated. Cargo workspaces take members at arbitrary paths; SPM targets accept an explicit `path:`; Gradle source sets have configurable `srcDirs` and Android modules can live anywhere. None of them *require* the current layout — they just default to it.

So this is a real choice, not a forced one. (Third rationale this session that didn't survive re-examination. That's now a pattern rather than a coincidence.)

## But check whether "four places" is the right frame

Two of the four aren't scattered fragments of one app — they're *the Mac app's TODO UI* and *the Android app's TODO UI*. The architecture separates exactly that boundary everywhere else, on purpose: non-interactive logic shared, presentation and judgment per-platform (D2, D7, and the `notes.md` entry that judgment can't live in the core because it needs rendering).

Under that reading, a TODO integration is: one core module, one manifest entry, and two platform UI components that belong to two different programs. Which is just how any cross-platform app with a shared core is laid out. The manifest exists precisely to make that one thing conceptually (D26), and it already does that job.

So the question I'd want to answer first: **is the discomfort about layout, or about ownership?** If it's ownership, the manifest is the answer and the layout is fine. If it's layout, the options below differ meaningfully.

## The option space

**A. Status quo** — three language trees plus a central manifest. Every toolchain gets its default; one build; static registration. Cost: adding a type touches four places, and the layout contradicts the "it's its own app" framing.

**B. Per-integration directory, sub-split by language** — `integrations/todo/{core,mac,android}/` with the manifest fragment alongside. Cargo workspace member, Gradle module, SPM target with an explicit path. The *code* co-locates; the three build files still each need a registration line, so it's not literally one place, but it's one directory to read, move, or extract.

**C. Separate repos, submoduled or vendored.** Genuinely independent. For one user shipping six integrations as a single product, this looks like pure overhead — submodule friction, cross-repo refactoring, version skew, and a manifest check that now has to aggregate across repos. `architecture.md` already flags integrations as "where this design over-builds."

**D. True plugin runtime.** Explicitly rejected for v1 (D3), and D26's instruction is to *design for* out-of-process later without implementing it. Nothing here changes that.

**E. Note the cost isn't uniform, which may dissolve the problem.** Generic text and generic binary have no core facets at all and delegate opening to the OS. The registry's platform side is a widget. Only TODO, notebook, and FileSet genuinely span everything. So "four places per integration" is the worst case for three types, not the standing tax on six.

## What I'd weigh

**For co-locating:** D3 says "a separate TODO app hooking in through the same interface is a design constraint on the interface." If that path matters, the layout should make extraction a *move* rather than a *refactor*. That's optionality on a direction the architecture already claims to care about, not aesthetics.

**Against:** `notes.md` records that your risk isn't Rust or design — it's platform scaffolding, with little Android experience and decades-old macOS experience. Non-default Gradle and Xcode layouts are precisely the category where days disappear into error messages that assume the convention you departed from.

**My lean is a middle path:** make each integration's core its own **crate** in a Cargo workspace, with its manifest fragment beside it, and leave the Mac and Android UI in the Mac and Android apps where the architecture already puts presentation. Two places, both honest, and the platform toolchains keep their defaults.

The non-obvious benefit: separate crates make interface leakage a **compile error** rather than something you catch in review. An integration that reaches into core internals stops building. That's the same kind of pressure the TODO proving case applied when it exposed the missing `get_metadata` — except continuous and automatic. If the facet interface is ever going to support a genuinely independent integration, this is the cheapest way to keep it honest.

Which axis is actually bothering you — the extraction path, the reading experience, or the four-places-to-edit cost when adding a type?

**User:** The thing that bugs me is that, in my experience, a single app needs to be “in one place” — with one parent top-level directory — to be reasoned about well. This tends to be very good insurance against interface leakage, because any connections across that boundary have to be happening over API’s, or get very obvious otherwise. A nice thing about option B, for example, is that it could easily evolve into option C in the future — and moroeover, it forces a discipline of building the thing as an isolated thing that means that these integrations will be built correctly. That’s going to be important as I think of more things that I want to integrate.

**Assistant:** That argument beats mine, and I think I can say precisely where mine was wrong: I was grouping by *where code runs*, but cohesion here runs by integration, not by platform. The Mac TODO widget's real collaborators are the TODO core module and the Android TODO widget — which have to present the same concepts — not the Mac app's `SpaceController`. Grouping by platform is the build systems setting the architecture again, which is the same mistake D26 made when it claimed they forced it.

And the concrete version of your point: if the Mac TODO widget lives inside the Mac app target, *nothing stops it calling Mac app internals directly* instead of going through the facet interface. That's the leakage, and it's exactly what would make a future independent TODO app impossible.

## What makes the boundary real rather than cosmetic

A directory only enforces something if there's a narrow thing to depend *on*. If `integrations/todo/core/` depends on all of `portal-core`, the boundary is decoration.

So the move that makes this work is extracting a **`portal-integration-api`** package — the facet traits, `ConflictSet`, `MergeOutcome`, `Target`, the domain-op plumbing — that integrations depend on *instead of* `portal-core`. Mirrored on the platform side: a `PortalIntegrationKit` Swift package and its Kotlin equivalent holding the widget protocol and the bridged types, with the **app depending on the integration and never the reverse**.

Then leakage is a compile error, extraction to Option C is mechanical, and — the part I'd value most — the facet interface gets continuously tested for sufficiency. The TODO proving case found the missing `get_metadata` by being designed carefully; this finds the next one automatically, by failing to build.

## Two things that make B cheaper than I framed it

**Auto-discovery is available in all three.** Cargo workspace `members` takes globs (`integrations/*/core`); `settings.gradle` can enumerate directories; `Package.swift` is Swift, so it can too. That turns "add an integration = touch four places" into genuinely "add a directory" — which is the claim Option B needs to actually deliver rather than just look tidier.

**The manifest gets stronger, not weaker.** D26 has one authored `integrations.toml`; this makes it per-integration fragments discovered and assembled. D26's stated value was "the facet matrix visible on one screen" — that survives as a *generated* artifact, and a generated matrix can't drift from reality the way an authored one can.

## Two warnings

**Don't let the crate split become a UniFFI-boundary split.** Integrations should be ordinary internal Rust crates aggregated by `portal-core`, with one UniFFI boundary at the top producing one `.so` and one set of bindings. T5 showed that the cross-compilation and binding path is the fiddly, expensive part; six binding surfaces would multiply the Android packaging problem by six for no gain.

**Do the scaffolding once, early, with one integration.** `notes.md` flags platform scaffolding as your actual risk area, and non-default Gradle and Xcode layouts are where that risk concentrates. Building this shape with TODO alone — before there are six — is much cheaper than retrofitting, and it fits the headless-dev-CLI sequencing already in the queue.

## One thing to watch

Better isolation lowers the marginal cost of adding an integration — and D27 currently leans on that cost as a brake ("the facet matrix is the main lever on the project's size," "a short type list is a requirement rather than a preference"). If adding one gets easy, the brake has to become the explicit justification rule rather than the incidental friction. Worth naming now, because you've said you expect to think of more things to integrate, and that's exactly when a rule that used to be self-enforcing quietly stops being.

**Assistant:** Both of those land, and the second one has a sequencing consequence I'd argue for.

## The integration API has two halves, and only one of them is new

What an integration **provides** is the facet set — `merge3`, `diff`, domain ops, `open`, `presentMerge`, `widget`. That's the part we've been designing.

What an integration **consumes** is a *restricted view of the same core API the shells get*: `read`, `write`, `write_through`, `list`, `list_projects`. D20 already established this ("integrations do their own cross-project work with `list_projects` and `read`") but never named it as a surface.

Naming it makes the restriction load-bearing rather than incidental. An integration must **not** reach `sync`, `rewind`, the branch operations, or acquisition — not for tidiness, but because those are lifecycle operations belonging to the user through a shell. An integration that could call `sync` could push on the user's behalf, silently violating the autosave-mode contract (D19/D23) for a project deliberately set to local-only. Same reasoning that already keeps credentials inside `read`.

So the layering:

```
portal-integration-api    facets to implement  +  host services you may call
portal-core               implements the services, aggregates integrations
                          (the full surface — sync, branches, tick — is shells-only)
```

And a packaged integration reads as one thing:

```
integrations/todo/
  integration.toml     name, paths, bundle behaviour, facets per platform
  core/                Rust crate — depends on portal-integration-api, never portal-core
  mac/                 Swift package — depends on PortalIntegrationKit, never the app
  android/             Gradle module
  cli/                 terminal presentation
```

## The CLI is the third client, and that's the whole point

Two clients never validate an abstraction — they let one of them quietly shape it. `notes.md` already carries the sharper version of this from D20: *an abstraction covering two cases isn't validated by covering them, only by covering them for the same reason.* Mac and Android are both GUI apps on OSes with document handlers; they can agree for reasons that don't generalize. A scriptable client with no UI framework, no Spaces, and no OS launch semantics is the case that would catch it.

It's also a direct empirical test of the D2 boundary. If a facet can be implemented in a terminal — `open` shells out, `presentDiff` prints, `presentMerge` is an interactive picker — it was never a rendering concern. If it genuinely can't, that's the core/shell line being *found* rather than asserted.

**So I'd flip the order: build the CLI as the second platform, not the third.** Core plus integration API plus CLI, then Mac, then Android. It proves the platform axis of the manifest before either expensive platform exists, it's the cheapest possible place to discover a mis-specified facet (you find out in Rust, not in Swift *and* Kotlin), it's the natural driver for T6 property testing which is already the highest-value correctness work, and it has zero platform scaffolding — the thing `notes.md` identifies as your actual risk. The queue already has the CLI for sequencing reasons; this gives it a second, stronger job.

## One line worth drawing explicitly

"Easy to grow along every dimension" is the exact sentence that precedes over-building, and this design has already flagged integrations as where it would happen — D3 says explicitly not a plugin runtime in v1, and `architecture.md` says no manifest DSL, no generic record store.

The distinction that keeps it honest: **a well-specified static interface is not a plugin runtime.** We're building a compile-time-checked API, a layout convention, and a declarative manifest. We're not building dynamic loading, a versioned ABI, sandboxing, capability negotiation, or a manifest language with conditionals. Worth writing into the decision, because the isolation we're adding is precisely what makes the runtime feel like the natural next step.

Let’s explore the shape of the API a bit more before we do a writeup because I suspect we’re going to learn quite a bit here.

**Assistant:** Let me work it concretely. Here's the split, and several things fall out of it.

## The consumed surface

```rust
// portal-integration-api — what an integration may call
fn read(project, path) -> Result<Vec<u8>>;
fn list(project, dir) -> Result<Vec<Entry>>;
fn write_through(project, path, bytes, expect: ContentHash) -> Result<()>;
fn list_projects(filter) -> Vec<RegistryEntry>;
fn status(project) -> ProjectStatus;
fn textual_merge3(base, ours, theirs) -> MergeOutcome;   // rung 2, per D11
```

Shell-only, and the exclusions are safety rather than tidiness: `sync`, `rewind`, all branch operations, `create_project`/`adopt_*`/`clone`/`drop_clone`, `set_lifecycle`, `set_metadata`, `tick`. These are lifecycle acts belonging to the user through a shell — an integration that could call `sync` could push on behalf of a project deliberately set to local-only, silently breaking the D19/D23 contract.

`status` is in the list because D30 requires it: TODO archival runs *only while the project is Synced*. That dependency existed and was never named.

## What we learn from it

**1. `list` just acquired a requirement it didn't have.** With roles gone (D37), every integration doing cross-project work must *discover* its files: `list_projects` → `list` → `read`. Previously `get_metadata` handed over the path. So `list` now needs the same clone-or-remote resolution `read` has — on a phone holding no clones it must hit the GitHub tree API. The current spec says that for `read` and is silent for `list`. That's a real gap D37 created.

**2. Integrations get `write_through` and not `write` — and that's a simplification, not a restriction.** `write` requires a clone; `write_through` works either way and routes to the local path when a clone exists (D29). Since every domain operation is a read-modify-write, the integration always has the content hash in hand. So integrations never branch on clone presence, and every integration write carries a concurrency precondition by construction. `write` stays a core/shell primitive.

(This needs an "expects absent" variant for file creation, which D29 didn't specify.)

**3. `merge3` and `diff` must be pure functions over bytes — no host services during a facet call.** This is the one I'd most want in the writeup. If `merge3` can read arbitrary files, then T6 — the highest-value correctness work in the project — needs a mocked filesystem, and the invariant that a merge is *identical regardless of which side is "ours"* becomes much harder to guarantee, because the result could depend on ambient state. Purity makes property testing tractable and determinism structural.

So the rule is: **domain operations may call host services; merge and diff may not.** That also resolves a reentrancy question the synchronous-core design would otherwise have (§8: core is blocking, shells own concurrency) — the core calling into an integration that calls back into the core.

**4. There are two shapes of facet, and only one of them can ever be proxied.** `merge3`, `diff`, `open`, `presentMerge` have fixed signatures — generic machinery can forward them. **Domain operations are integration-defined**, which is why D13 constrained them to records, enums and sequences for UniFFI. That makes them the concrete obstacle to ever running an integration out-of-process: the fixed facets would proxy generically, domain ops would need generated per-integration marshalling. Worth recording as the honest answer to "can this become Option C," rather than discovering it later.

**5. The Rust and platform kits have different facet sets.** Widgets never cross UniFFI (D26), so `widget` exists in `PortalIntegrationKit` and has no Rust counterpart at all. That asymmetry should be explicit in the API docs or it will read as an omission.

**6. The CLI slots in with no new machinery**, which is a small validation of the manifest design: `open` shells out, `presentDiff` prints, `presentMerge` is an interactive picker, `widget` is simply absent — and per-platform facet absence is already first-class in the matrix.

## Open questions I'd want to settle

- **Does anything need `resolve_type`?** FileSet dispatches `open` per entry, but handler resolution is shell-side. I can't find a core-side integration that needs it — which would be good news, since it keeps the consumed surface smaller.
- **Does `get_metadata` survive at all?** Its stated justification was role→path resolution, which D37 deleted. If nothing else needs it, the honest move is removing it rather than leaving it as a vestige.
- **Who reports capabilities now?** `register_shell_capabilities` assumed the shell knew. If the integration owns its platform code, the integration's platform half knows what it implements and the app aggregates — a small inversion, but it changes the call.

Want to push on any of these, or should I take a pass at the facet side — the *provided* half — where I suspect the domain-operation shape has more to teach us?

- There are other things that each integration should receive. For one thing, for any project that *is* cloned, it should get a pointer to the directory — 99% of integrations are going to be editing and using files in the ordinary way, just like “application opening the file,” and this is perfectly aligned with the way the git fs behavior works.
- There are things that should come from the client side. For example, in the Mac widget, it should know whether it’s currently rendering in the “home” view or the view of a particular project, and have the directory pointer for that project and/or the main repo as appropriate. We should think about those aspects of the API as well.
- As you note in #5, the Swift and Kotlin API’s should *include* the calls to the Rust core and just proxy those over, so that integrations don’t need to be calling directly to the underlying things and the API can be simple and language-specific. 
- I’m not sure who needs resolve_type but it wouldn’t at all surprise me if someone needed it in the future, or if they wanted to call methods of higher-level APIs like `open()`.
- I’m not sure about get_metadata; I suspect we can nuke it.
- Capability reporting — it feels like when an integration is attached to the Portal (and how does that attachment happen? Is it compile-time?) then that adds information about how particular types are handled, and the integration’s manifest tells us how to do that on each platform. Is that right?

**Assistant:** Taking these in turn — two of them change the shape meaningfully.

## `write_blob`, and what the name stops carrying

Agreed. The one thing `through` was doing was hinting at the precondition, so the signature has to carry that now — which argues for making it explicit rather than an opaque hash:

```rust
fn write_blob(project, path, bytes, expect: Expect) -> Result<()>;
enum Expect { Hash(ContentHash), Absent }   // Absent covers creation, which D29 left unspecified
```

## The directory pointer — I'd narrow what it's *for*

You're right that most integrations want to touch files normally. But I'd separate two needs that look alike:

**Content Portal reasons about** — anything it parses, merges, or diffs — must go through the byte API, because that's the path that works without a clone and the path that's interceptable. Note this isn't a restriction in practice: `read` *already* resolves to the local clone when one exists, so an integration gains nothing by using a raw path for its own content.

**Handing a location to something that only speaks filesystem** — a PDF viewer, an image loader, an editor over a directory — genuinely needs a path, and no byte API substitutes.

That second need is better served by a primitive than by exposing the clone:

```rust
fn materialize(project, path) -> Result<PathBuf>;   // read-only; fetches to cache if no clone
fn clone_path(project) -> Option<PathBuf>;          // directory-scale tools only
```

`materialize` gives a usable path *always*, on the phone too, so integrations never branch on clone presence — the core decides where the bytes came from. Materialized files are explicitly read-only; writes go back through `write_blob`. `clone_path` stays for the genuine directory-scale case ("run this tool over the project root"), and being `Option` forces the absence case to be handled at compile time rather than discovered on Android.

The discipline worth writing down: **if Portal merges it, it goes through the byte API.** Otherwise the no-clone case breaks silently and `merge3` stops being property-testable.

## Client-side context — and this is where `Scope` grows up

D5 already has widgets rendering at a scope ("a Project or the global view"). What you're describing is that scope should *carry* context rather than just naming it:

```swift
enum Scope { case home(HomeContext); case project(ProjectContext) }
struct ProjectContext { let id: ProjectId; let name: String; let cloneURL: URL? /* … */ }
```

The structural point underneath: **host services reach the integration through an injected context, never through a global.** That's what makes a fake context possible in tests and the CLI, and it's the only version that could ever be proxied out-of-process. Symmetric on both sides — a `Host` handle in Rust, a `PortalHost` in Swift/Kotlin carrying the scope.

## Kits proxy the core — with one honest caveat

Agreed, and it's more than convenience. It means integration platform code never touches UniFFI-generated types, so the binding surface can change without breaking integrations, and each kit can be *idiomatic* — `async/await` and typed errors in Swift, `Flow` in Kotlin — rather than the lowest common denominator UniFFI produces.

The cost is a façade maintained in three languages: every host service is added three times. Worth noting the CLI doesn't fully de-risk this, since a Rust CLI exercises the `Host` trait but not the proxy layer. The CLI validates the *facet split*; the kits' shape is first tested by the Mac app.

## `open` is needed, and it subsumes `resolve_type`

Stronger than "someone might want it" — **FileSet already requires it.** D25 says opening a project is `open` on its FileSet, dispatched to the Collection integration, "whose implementation iterates its entries." So FileSet must invoke `open` on entries whose types it doesn't know. That's a host service, today.

And it makes `resolve_type` unnecessary: the integration says "open this target," the host resolves the handler. I'd keep `resolve_type` out until something needs it that `open` doesn't cover.

Worth naming the consequence, because it's structural: **the API is recursive** — an integration can invoke a facet on other types. That's exactly what makes "opening a project and opening a file are the same operation" literally true rather than an analogy.

## `get_metadata` — agreed, remove

Nothing left needs it: `list_projects` returns names, and the remaining field (autosave mode) is a shell-side settings concern. It stays a shell-facing call, leaves the integration surface.

## Attachment and capabilities — yes, and it splits the call

Attachment is compile-time, per D3's "static registration, no dynamic loading" — the crate is a workspace member, the Swift package a dependency of the Mac app, the Gradle module included. Your framing is right, with one refinement:

`register_shell_capabilities` currently conflates two different things. The **static** half — "TODO implements `presentMerge` on Mac but not Android" — comes from the manifest and is known at build time, so the core can simply have it compiled in. The **dynamic** half — what the OS can actually open, via UTI role handlers or Intent resolution — genuinely varies at runtime and is the only part that needs reporting.

So the runtime call shrinks to just the OS-resolvable set, and the deferral logic (D2's "a platform that can't resolve this defers to one that can") reads the union. Cleaner, and it means a manifest gap can't be masked by a runtime report.

That leaves the *provided* half — the facet signatures themselves — where I think the domain-operation shape still has things to teach us, since it's the one facet whose signature varies per integration and the one thing standing between this and out-of-process. Want to go there next?
