I think that the Notebook integration, which needs to deeply work with an entire directory in a single repo, is going to be far more the common case — and you’re usually only doing that when the repo is checked out. 

Let’s think about how these integrations get invoked, after all. One path is when you are trying to `open` a file/directory of a named type on a particular platform; in this case I think it makes good sense to assume that we do have a clone and are working locally. Another path is when the integration is running from the “home” space (or the platform equivalent thereof) but actively wants to go out and seek information from other projects — which means it’s calling `list`, `read`, and so on. (NB, we may want to rename `read` to `read_blob` for parity and to highlight that this is a pretty unusual way to read!) 

So I think that in the common path it makes a lot of sense for the integration to expect a clone. Maybe the invariant is one tied to calls like `open()`, that can promise that the clone must exist before it calls the type’s open method?

**Assistant:** You're right, and I can be precise about where my rule was wrong.

For `merge3` it was **redundant** — the facet signature already takes `base/ours/theirs` as bytes, so it never touches a filesystem regardless. The purity property I wanted is structural already. I generalized a constraint that was doing its work at the signature level into a rule that then over-reached into `open`, where it's simply wrong: opening a notebook means handing a real directory to real code, and pretending otherwise would be abstraction for its own sake.

## The invariant, and the principle under it

I'd state it as: **`open` guarantees a clone.** If none exists, the host clones before invoking the type's `open`. So an integration's `open` path gets a non-optional local directory and works with it normally.

The principle that makes it defensible: *explicit engagement justifies a clone; ambient reading does not.* Opening is a user saying "I am going to work in this thing," which earns the fetch. The cross-project view is passive background reading, where cloning every repo to read one file is exactly the bad trade D29 was designed around.

## But it attaches to `open`, not to scope

I was about to hang this on `Scope` — project scope implies a clone — and that's wrong, because of a capability the design already committed to: the content accessor reads a project **without** a local clone, which is what lets the phone render a project's TODO list without holding a working copy. That's project-scoped and cloneless by design.

So:

- **`open`'s target is guaranteed local.** Non-optional path.
- **A widget's `ProjectContext` keeps `cloneURL: URL?`.** Project scope alone promises nothing.
- **Domain operations stay clone-agnostic** via `read_blob`/`write_blob`, because they run in both modes — the notebook's `append` from the capture panel, TODO's `set_status` from the cross-project view.

Which gives the two paths you described, cleanly separated by *how you got there* rather than by what you're looking at:

**Engaged** — entered through `open`, clone guaranteed, work with the directory normally. Notebook's deep directory work, FileSet launching a project. The common case, as you say.

**Ambient** — entered at Home scope or against an uncloned project, no path, `list` + `read_blob` + `write_blob`. TODO's unified view today, search in v2.

## `read_blob` — yes, on semantic grounds

I'd take it, though I'd justify it by semantics rather than rarity: at Home scope it isn't unusual, it's the only way to read. What `_blob` accurately signals is whole-file, no seeks, possibly remote — precisely the non-POSIX expectations D22 warned the "filesystem" name would invite. And `read_blob`/`write_blob` as a matched pair reads far better than `read`/`write_through` did.

**`materialize` demotes.** Its main job was giving a path where no clone exists, which `open`'s invariant now covers. What's left is narrow: a widget rendering binary content from an uncloned project — a PDF thumbnail on Android. Real, but I'd defer it until something needs it.

## Three consequences of the invariant worth recording

**`open` acquires latency and a failure mode it didn't have.** Cloning is network work that can be slow, offline, or Blocked. So `open` becomes progress-reporting and fallible on the host side, and on Android it inherits the existing background-kill risk — a clone triggered by opening can be killed mid-flight.

**It creates a storage policy question on the phone.** If opening auto-clones, a phone accumulates working copies. `drop_clone` refuses when there's unpushed work (D14), so they don't vanish on their own. Whether an Android `open` clones permanently or transiently is a genuine v1 decision, and it isn't made anywhere yet.

**It gives `open` a precondition worth stating as an invariant** alongside I1–I6, since it's the thing every integration's `open` implementation is entitled to rely on.

Shall I fold all of this into the writeup now, or keep going into the domain-operation shape first?

**User:** I like all of these, and I think the consequences are good. Note that `drop_clone` refuses when there’s unpublished work but (a) if you have a network connection then sync() puts you into a safe-to-drop state, so the same kind of “timeout” logic that drives syncs could, on even longer timeouts, also drop a highly inactive clone — unless the user asked you to make sure it’s always available on-device, which is a great user setting; and (b) if you’re working in a detached state on a bunch of directories, yes, your disk will get full, what did the user *expect?*

This is feeling more like a natural usage flow. And `open` failing because the clone takes a while, and it needing a progress indicator, is quite fine.

(Oh: One pattern we should follow is that functions which take a long time should return streaming progress information that the UX can display)

I’d also say that we should keep `resolve_type` in the API. I know we don’t have a use for it yet, but there’s a smell here — status functions are almost *always* useful. 

So given that, let’s sketch what our API looks like now.

**Assistant:** Good — and the auto-drop insight is nice: that's the third time "only act while **Synced**" has removed a race for free (D30's archival, now clone reclamation). That's a pattern worth recording in its own right.

Here's the sketch.

## Cross-cutting: long operations

```rust
trait ProgressObserver {
    /// Returning `Cancel` aborts at the next safe point.
    fn on_progress(&self, p: Progress) -> Flow;
}
enum Flow { Continue, Cancel }
struct Progress { phase: Phase, fraction: Option<f32>, detail: String }
enum Phase { Resolving, Fetching, Cloning, Merging, Writing, Pushing }
```

Making the observer's *return value* the cancellation channel means one mechanism covers both, and cancellation is checked exactly where progress is reported — which is where aborting is safe. No separate token.

## The core surface (shell-facing)

```rust
// ─── Loop ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
fn tick();
fn notify_connectivity(online: bool);           // accelerator, never correctness
fn notify_changed(project: &ProjectId);

// ─── Acquisition ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
fn create_project(name: &str, autosave: AutosaveMode) -> Result<ProjectId>;
fn adopt_directory(path: &str, opts: AdoptOptions) -> Result<ProjectId>;
fn adopt_repository(path: &str, opts: AdoptOptions) -> Result<ProjectId>;
fn clone(project: &ProjectId, obs: &dyn ProgressObserver) -> Result<()>;
fn drop_clone(project: &ProjectId) -> Result<()>;        // refuses on unpushed work
fn set_keep_local(project: &ProjectId, keep: bool);      // exempt from auto-reclaim

// ─── Sync and state ──────────────────────────────────────────────────
fn sync(project: &ProjectId, title: Option<String>,
        obs: &dyn ProgressObserver) -> Result<SyncOutcome>;
fn status(project: &ProjectId) -> ProjectStatus;                    // ← integration too

// ─── History ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
fn timeline(project: &ProjectId, limit: u32) -> Vec<TimelineEntry>;
fn rewind(project: &ProjectId, to: CommitId, paths: Option<Vec<String>>) -> Result<()>;

// ─── Branching ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────
fn list_branches / create_branch / switch_branch
   / branch_from_divergence / merge_branch / delete_branch

// ─── Content ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
fn read_blob(project: &ProjectId, path: &str) -> Result<Vec<u8>>;   // ← integration
fn write_blob(project: &ProjectId, path: &str,
              bytes: &[u8], expect: Expect) -> Result<()>;          // ← integration
fn list(project: &ProjectId, dir: &str) -> Result<Vec<Entry>>;      // ← integration
fn clone_path(project: &ProjectId) -> Option<PathBuf>;              // ← integration
enum Expect { Hash(ContentHash), Absent }

// ─── Types and merge ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
fn resolve_type(path: &str) -> TypeId;                              // ← integration
fn textual_merge3(base: Option<&str>, ours: &str, theirs: &str)
        -> MergeOutcome;                                            // ← integration
fn merge_file(...) -> MergeOutcome;                                 // core-internal

// ─── Registry ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
fn list_projects(filter: LifecycleFilter) -> Vec<RegistryEntry>;    // ← integration
fn set_lifecycle(project: &ProjectId, state: LifecycleState) -> Result<()>;
```

## The integration host surface

Everything marked above, and nothing else — no `sync`, no branching, no acquisition, no lifecycle mutation:

```rust
trait Host {
    fn read_blob(&self, project: &ProjectId, path: &str) -> Result<Vec<u8>>;
    fn write_blob(&self, project: &ProjectId, path: &str,
                  bytes: &[u8], expect: Expect) -> Result<()>;
    fn list(&self, project: &ProjectId, dir: &str) -> Result<Vec<Entry>>;
    fn clone_path(&self, project: &ProjectId) -> Option<PathBuf>;
    fn list_projects(&self, filter: LifecycleFilter) -> Vec<RegistryEntry>;
    fn status(&self, project: &ProjectId) -> ProjectStatus;
    fn resolve_type(&self, path: &str) -> TypeId;
    fn textual_merge3(&self, base: Option<&str>, ours: &str, theirs: &str) -> MergeOutcome;
}
```

Keeping `resolve_type` is cheap in a way worth naming as a rule: **read-only, side-effect-free introspection costs nothing to expose and can't be misused; mutation is what needs justifying.** (Small caveat — under D37 a type may carry a validation predicate, so `resolve_type` isn't strictly pure. Worth deciding whether validation runs here or only during traversal.)

## What an integration provides

```rust
// Rust — pure functions over bytes; no Host access during these
trait Merge { fn merge3(&self, base: Option<&[u8]>, ours: &[u8], theirs: &[u8]) -> MergeOutcome; }
trait Diff  { fn diff(&self, a: &[u8], b: &[u8]) -> DiffModel; }
// domain operations: integration-defined signatures, receive &dyn Host
```

```swift
// Swift kit — proxies the host surface idiomatically; integrations never touch UniFFI types
protocol PortalHost {
    func readBlob(_ p: ProjectId, _ path: String) async throws -> Data
    func writeBlob(_ p: ProjectId, _ path: String, _ bytes: Data, expect: Expect) async throws
    func list(_ p: ProjectId, _ dir: String) async throws -> [Entry]
    func listProjects(_ f: LifecycleFilter) async throws -> [RegistryEntry]
    func status(_ p: ProjectId) async throws -> ProjectStatus
    func resolveType(_ path: String) -> TypeId
    func open(_ target: Target) async throws        // recursive: host resolves the handler
    var scope: Scope { get }
}

enum Scope { case home(HomeContext); case project(ProjectContext) }
struct ProjectContext { let id: ProjectId; let name: String; let cloneURL: URL? }

protocol OpenFacet   { func open(_ target: LocalTarget) async throws }
protocol DiffFacet   { func presentDiff(_ model: DiffModel) }
protocol MergeFacet  { func presentMerge(_ set: ConflictSet) async -> Resolution? }
protocol WidgetFacet { func render(_ host: PortalHost) -> View }
```

**`LocalTarget` is doing real work there** — it carries a non-optional local URL, so the clone-guarantee invariant is enforced by the type rather than by documentation. A widget's `ProjectContext.cloneURL` stays optional because project scope promises nothing; only `open` does.

## The manifest fragment

```toml
name     = "notebook"
paths    = ["*.notebook"]
bundle   = "transparent"
contents = { "*.md" = "notebook" }
core     = ["merge3", "diff", "domain"]
mac      = ["open", "presentDiff", "presentMerge", "widget"]
android  = ["open", "widget"]
cli      = ["open", "presentDiff"]
```

## What's new here, and what's still open

**New decisions in this pass:** `set_keep_local` plus tick-driven reclamation of inactive Synced clones; progress-with-cancellation as a uniform pattern for long operations; `LocalTarget` encoding the clone invariant.

**Open:**
- **Does `write` survive at all?** In engaged mode an integration has `clone_path` and can use ordinary filesystem writes; in ambient mode it uses `write_blob`. The clone-requiring `write` may have no remaining caller.
- **Whether `Phase` is a closed enum or free-form** — closed is checkable, free-form lets an integration report its own stages.
- **Android's `open` storage policy** — transient or permanent clone, now partly answered by auto-reclaim but not fully.
- **The domain-operation shape**, still the one facet whose signature varies and the real obstacle to out-of-process.

Want to close out the domain ops, or is this the point to write everything up?

We can defer the `open` storage policy, I think we have enough structural flexibility that it can change over time!

Propagation complete. Here's what landed:

**Five decisions recorded:**

- **D39** — an integration is one directory, depending on `portal-integration-api` rather than `portal-core`. Supersedes D26's layout; keeps its manifest insight, now as discovered fragments assembled into a *generated* matrix (which can't drift from reality the way an authored one could).
- **D40** — the API's two halves, the restricted host surface, `merge3`/`diff` purity, recursive `open`, the `read_blob`/`write_blob` renames, `write` and `get_metadata` removed, `resolve_type` retained.
- **D41** — clones acquired on engagement, reclaimed on inactivity; the engaged/ambient split; `LocalTarget` encoding the invariant in the type.
- **D42** — streaming progress with cancellation via the observer's return value; no `Phase`.
- **D43** — the CLI is the first client, changing build order.

**Also updated:** `software-architecture.md` (module layout, §1.2, §2.6, §2.10, §4, all of §5, §8), `architecture.md` (the D37/D38 propagation I'd paused, plus the roster), `integration-todo.md`, `todo.md`, `notes.md`, and `system-design.json` (integrations component rewritten, CLI added, stale risk id fixed).

Three things worth flagging:

**`integration-todo.md`'s "what this exercise surfaced" section got more interesting, not less.** It originally recorded that the proving case found a missing primitive — `get_metadata`. That gap was real but the *fix* moved: with roles retired, the primitive TODO actually needs is `list`, which in turn must resolve clone-or-remote. I rewrote it to say that rather than deleting it, because "the gap was real, the answer changed" is the more useful record.

**The queue shrank in two places.** The adoption UX item lost role assignment entirely. And `ProjectMetadata` is now so thin — autosave mode, with `active` expressible as a `.fileset` found by name — that I reframed that item around whether it still earns being a file.

**`notes.md` now records the expired-rationale pattern as a recurring failure mode**, since it happened three times today: the registry layout, D13's central resolution, and D26's toolchain claim. Two were genuinely obsolete; D26's was simply overstated when written. That's worth a periodic audit pass of its own at some point.

Ready for domain operations whenever you are — that's the last piece of the facet surface, and the one thing standing between this design and a future out-of-process integration.

## Gaps in that list

**Notebook can't be read as a notebook.** `02 file formats.md` says the primary use is "see the whole thing and scroll forwards and backwards, jump to specific dates or bookmarked spots" — but `read_day` is per-date and there's no way to enumerate days. Needs something like `list_days(project)` or a range read. Also missing: renaming a day file, which the design explicitly invites ("Portal picks the summary words itself, and the user renames later if they care").

**FileSet has nothing**, despite being the project manifest. At minimum it needs entries/add/remove/reorder — and D8 already named the natural affordance ("add this to the manifest," for something opened by hand that should be remembered), which is a FileSet domain op.

**TODO has no `unarchive`**, though D30 specifies its semantics in detail (reattach by parent ID, prefer a real archived item over a frozen copy).

## Four things the list surfaces

**1. `list_projects` is both a host service and a domain operation — and that clarifies what the exclusion rule actually means.** It's in the `Host` trait *and* it's the registry integration's own operation. That's not a duplication: the core's registry module simply *is* the registry integration, and `Host` re-exports it, with the host dispatching so integrations never call each other directly (same shape as `open`).

But look at `set_lifecycle`: excluded from `Host`, yet it's the registry's own domain op. So the rule isn't "these calls are dangerous." It's **an integration may not act on things it doesn't own.** The registry archiving a project is the registry doing its job; the TODO integration archiving a project would be a violation. That's a sharper and more defensible statement than the one currently in D40, and I'd want to reword it.

**2. The dashboard needs to trigger operations it isn't allowed to call.** D38 made the dashboard the registry's widget — but a dashboard's job includes opening a project, syncing it, dropping a clone, pinning it. All excluded from `Host`. D38 created this and D40 didn't notice.

The resolution I'd propose: **widgets emit actions, they don't call lifecycle APIs.** `WidgetAction::Sync(project)` goes to the app, which makes the call. The safety property survives intact — an integration still can't autonomously push — while its UI can offer the button, because a user pressing a button *is* the user acting. It's also the same shape as D3's existing "Portal owns a thin wrapper around foreign resolvers."

**3. Every mutation is read-modify-write against a content precondition, which constrains what a mutation may be.** `write_blob(expect:)` fails if another device changed the file; the sane response is re-read, re-apply, re-write. That's only safe if the operation is a function of parsed state rather than a delta. `set_status(id, Done)` re-applies cleanly. A hypothetical `toggle_status(id)` would not — it'd flip twice. So: **domain mutations must be expressed as absolute state or as additions, never as toggles or increments.** That's a real constraint on every future integration, and it's not written down anywhere.

**4. `plan_session()` doesn't look like a domain operation.** The design describes it as "a deliberative flow, not a query" — a whole UX flow behind a single core call. Everything else in the list is data in, data out. I'd expect this to decompose into data (ranked candidates) plus a widget that runs the flow, with the flow itself living platform-side where judgment and rendering belong. Worth examining before it gets built as specified.

Also worth noting for the UniFFI shape: not all domain ops are project-scoped (`today_view()` takes no project), and every one returns a *view* rather than a model — `TodoItemView`, not `TodoItem` — which is what D13's "records, enums and sequences" constraint is really about. Views are snapshots, never live objects.

Where do you want to start — the exclusion rule and the action channel, or filling the gaps?

**Assistant:** A domain operation is the integration's **core half exposing typed operations to its own widget, across the UniFFI boundary.** It exists for one reason: D13 made `parse`/`serialize` internal, so a Swift or Kotlin widget can't hold the parsed model and needs typed calls instead of bytes. Hence D13's constraint that they be "UniFFI-expressible — records, enums, and sequences."

Put next to the other facets, it's the only one that's *intra*-integration but *inter*-language:

- `merge3` / `diff` — called by the core's merge ladder
- `open` / `presentDiff` / `presentMerge` / `widget` — called by the shell
- **domain operations** — called by the integration's own platform half

So: no, not an API to other integrations, and I'd argue it must stay that way. A general inter-integration API would create a dependency graph among integrations and destroy exactly the isolation D39 just bought. The one apparent exception is `list_projects`, and the honest description there is **promotion**: a small number of operations get lifted into the `Host` surface, at which point they're host services and the fact that the registry implements them is an implementation detail. Promotion is a deliberate act by the API, not something an integration can do to itself.

**Your read of that table is right, and it was sloppy of me.** It conflates three different things:

- genuine domain ops — `set_status`, `append`, `add`, `reparent`
- integration *features* that happen to live core-side — `today_view()`, `plan_session()`; widgets do call them, but they're queries and flows, not content manipulation
- something that's really a host service — `list_projects`

That conflation is in the source documents, not just my summary, which is a decent sign the concept needs the tightening you're pointing at.

So yes — one integration at a time is the better method, asking for each: what does it *do*, which facets does that require, and what genuinely has to cross the language boundary. And your three are well chosen, because they span the range:

**Generic text** is the degenerate case — no core facets, no domain ops, merge *is* rung 2, opening delegates to the OS. It's the test of whether the interface is honest about an integration that does almost nothing. If generic text turns out to need something, the design is wrong somewhere.

**FileSet** is the recursive one: its `open` dispatches to types it doesn't know, it's simultaneously the project manifest and a free-standing bibliography, and it's a bundle whose contents are ordinary files merging by their own rules. It tests recursion, the bundle model, and D4's "one pattern, several instances" all at once.

**TODO** is the maximal case — hierarchy, cross-project reads, writes into uncloned repos, an archive with its own merge story. It's already the proving case that found two gaps.

Which order? I'd suggest **generic text first** — it's fast, and establishing what the *floor* looks like will sharpen what counts as a real domain operation before we hit the two hard ones.

## The facet reference list

**Declaration** (manifest, not code): `paths`, `bundle` (opaque/transparent), `contents`, `validate` (optional veto predicate)

**Core, Rust:**
1. `merge3` — structural three-way merge; pure
2. `diff` — produce a `DiffModel`; pure
3. domain operations — typed calls for its own widget

**Shell, per platform:**
4. `open` — invoke over a `LocalTarget`
5. `presentDiff` — render a `DiffModel`
6. `presentMerge` — render a `ConflictSet`, return a `Resolution`
7. `widget` — render at a scope

**Missing from that list: `scaffold`.** Adoption "scaffolds whatever Portal-native files are missing," and `create_project` must produce an empty notebook and TODO — but nothing owns *knowing how to create an empty instance of a type*. It's unassigned in every document. That's a genuine eighth facet.

## Two structural things fall out before we even get to the walk-through

**OS-delegated `open` should be a host service, not a per-integration facet.** Your `NSWorkspace.shared.urlsForApplications` + `UTType` logic is identical for generic text, generic binary, and any future type without its own opener — and it has real precedence rules to get right: the FileSet entry's `app` override (D25) beats the user's preference for that type, which beats the OS default. Writing that once in the kit and treating **absent `open` ⇒ host delegates to the OS** is better than each integration reimplementing it.

**If `textual_merge3` is a core primitive, textual *diff* should be too.** D11 promoted textual merge to a rung available to every type. Line diffing is exactly as generic and every text-ish type wants it. Otherwise generic text ends up owning a primitive that structural integrations also need to call.

## Generic text

| Facet | Verdict |
|---|---|
| paths | none — reached by content sniff (valid UTF-8) |
| bundle | no |
| `merge3` | **none.** Declines; rung 2 *is* its merge |
| `diff` | none, if textual diff becomes a core primitive |
| domain ops | **none.** There's no typed structure to manipulate |
| `open` | none — host delegates to the OS |
| `presentDiff` | needed; no OS-provided text diff UI either |
| `presentMerge` | **needed, and it's the hard one** |
| `widget` | none |

You're right that this isn't degenerate, and the reason is `presentMerge`. Three options:

1. **Launch an external merge tool** over three temp files and read back the result — exactly D3's Tier 2 pattern ("presentMerge for a PDF already means launching a foreign app over three files and receiving a result path"). FileMerge ships with Xcode, VS Code has a 3-way editor, `git mergetool` is configurable. But it depends on the user having one configured, and there's no equivalent on Android.
2. **Conflict markers in one file, opened in the user's own editor**, then parsed back on save. Needs no configuration, works with whatever editor they already like, and is the flow every developer already knows.
3. **Build a real 3WM UI.**

I'd propose **2 as the v1 baseline**, with 1 as an override when a mergetool is configured. It's the only one that always works, and it costs a marker writer and a parser rather than a UI. Worth noting it doesn't violate I2 — that invariant is about *git* state, and this file lives in merge scratch space, never the working tree.

## Generic binary (PDF, PNG)

| Facet | Verdict |
|---|---|
| `merge3` | none — rung 3 |
| `diff` | none — no meaningful binary diff |
| domain ops | none |
| `open` | none — host delegates to the OS |
| `presentDiff` | open both side by side; for images and PDFs this is genuinely the right interaction |
| `presentMerge` | display-and-pick: open all three, user chooses or supplies a replacement |
| `widget` | none |

## The comparison is the interesting part

**Binary's merge UI is easy and text's is hard**, which inverts the intuition. Picking one of three files is three buttons plus a launch — Portal's thin "done, here's the result" wrapper. *Composing* a merged text result needs a real editing surface, and none is provided by either OS.

Which means **§5.4's "three of them nearly free" is wrong.** Generic binary is nearly free. Generic text is not: it's a merge UI, a diff UI, and — since text conflicts are far and away the most common kind — the one whose absence would be felt daily. That's a correction I'd make to the roster before it drives any sequencing.

It also raises a question worth answering now rather than at rung 3: **what does `presentDiff` mean when it's "just open two files"?** If binary's `presentDiff` is a launch and text's is a real UI, they're barely the same facet. Might be that binary implements neither and the host's OS delegation covers it, the same way `open` does.

Want to settle the text `presentMerge` approach, or keep walking — FileSet next?

**User:** Let’s dive into this more because I think it’s really core to understanding what the merge and diff experiences are like.

Let’s start from Portal’s goal here. In one case, the user is explicitly asking Portal to compare a current state to a prior state — what are our current edits (working tree vs HEAD), compare current state to a potential rewind, etc. Here the user really wants a left-and-right, optimally with visual markers indicating where there’s a change. Visually displaying this has well-known UI patterns for text, and not really for images, although that would be a fascinating (and very separate) research problem.

In the merge case, Portal needs to do a few things:
- Given a proposed 3WM, figure out if there are any conflicts
- If there are no conflicts, perform the actual merge and provide the final output, some fourth version that’s the “merged” file. (Which might be equivalent to one of the other three but might not)
- If there are, then call the presentMerge UX and get back that fourth file.

So really, there are four things that the integration needs to implement:
- Check a proposed 3WM and find conflicts in it. (There’s a good default implementation of this for text)
- Apply a 3WM when we know there are no conflicts. (There’s a good default implementation for text)
- Present a 3WM UX
- Present a diff UX

For images, “present a diff UX” would be to open those two files with hints to the app that might cause them to be displayed side-by-side — not just in two windows, if at all possible, and if in two independent windows then those windows need to have titles that make which is which be clear. “Present a 3WM UX” in that case would open *three* windows in a similar way.

**Assistant:** This reframing is better than what's written, and it clarifies something the current docs muddle. Let me take the four responsibilities, then push back on one of them.

## The four are right, but check and apply want to stay one call

The distinction you're drawing is real: *detecting* conflicts and *producing* the merged bytes are different jobs with different defaults. But for essentially every implementation they share all their work — diff3 finds the conflicts and produces the output in one pass; a structural merge parses both sides once. Splitting the call means parsing twice.

And the existing `MergeOutcome` already encodes exactly your disjunction: `Clean(bytes)` or `Conflicted { set: Option<ConflictSet> }`. One call, two outcomes, no double work.

What your framing *does* surface that isn't written down: the merge flow is genuinely **two-phase across files**. §3 lands one merge commit only when every path is resolved, so the flow is check-everything, then commit-everything — you can't apply file-by-file as you go. That works with a single call by holding the clean results in memory until the whole set resolves. Worth stating explicitly, because "run the ladder per path" currently reads as if each path completes independently.

## The genuinely new idea: `presentMerge` returns the file

You've described the merge UX as producing "that fourth file" — and that's a real simplification over what's currently designed, where `presentMerge` returns a structured `Resolution` that the core then assembles into bytes.

Returning bytes directly:
- matches how external tools actually behave — FileMerge and VS Code produce a file, not a resolution structure
- matches display-and-pick exactly: the user picked a file, that *is* the answer
- deletes the resolution-assembly logic entirely

Most importantly, it shrinks the problem that's currently parked as **"the highest-leverage interface in the system and it isn't designed yet."** `ConflictSet` stops being a *resolution protocol* — a two-way contract where the UI must express its answer in the core's vocabulary — and becomes *optional rendering detail*, a one-way hint. That's a much smaller thing to get right, and getting it wrong is much less costly.

**It can't vanish entirely, though.** Parsing is internal to the integration's Rust half (D13), so a Swift TODO widget *cannot* parse a `.todo` file to find out what conflicts. Structural types need the core to hand over conflict detail. But generic text and generic binary don't — a text merge UI works from three files, and display-and-pick works from three files. Which is precisely why D16 made the set `Option`. That decision anticipated this correctly; what changes is that the Option is now the common case rather than the binary special case.

## Defaults are inheritable, not just a fallthrough

You said "there's a good default implementation of this for text" for both halves, and that's a slightly different claim from what D11 says. D11 frames rung 2 as *falling through* when rung 1 declines. Your framing is that text's implementations are **available as defaults an integration can inherit per-operation** — a structural type might do its own conflict detection but delegate assembly, or vice versa.

That's more useful, and it's what "a structural merge may call textual merge for sub-parts" was gesturing at without saying. Worth making explicit in the API: text's check and apply are named, callable primitives, and an integration inherits either independently.

## The image case gives us a concrete API requirement

"Open two files" is not sufficient — as you say, the user must be able to tell which is which. That's a requirement the current facet signature doesn't carry: `presentDiff(_ model: DiffModel)` has nowhere to put labels.

So both presentation facets need **labelled versions**:

```swift
func presentDiff(_ versions: [(label: String, target: LocalTarget)])
func presentMerge(_ versions: [(label: String, target: LocalTarget)],
                  detail: ConflictSet?) async -> Data?
```

And the pragmatic mechanism for the labels is **the temp filenames themselves**. You can't force most Mac apps to tile windows, but nearly every app titles its window from the filename — so materializing as `invoice-BEFORE.pdf` / `invoice-AFTER.pdf`, or `diagram-BASE/MINE/THEIRS.png`, gets the labelling for free and degrades gracefully when the app opens separate windows. Side-by-side becomes a best-effort hint on top of a labelling scheme that always works.

## One correction to D3

D3 collapsed two-way and three-way diff into "one varying-arity operation." That's right about *shape* and wrong about *contract*: a diff presents and returns nothing, a merge presents and returns a file. Arity isn't the distinction that matters; producing a result is. Your framing separates them correctly, and it also names diff's two distinct callers — "what have I changed" (working tree vs HEAD) and "what would this rewind do" — which are the same operation and both two-way.

Want me to record this batch — the merge/diff facet reshape, labelled versions, `presentMerge` returning bytes — before we continue to FileSet? It's enough of a change to the merge model that I'd rather not stack more on top of it unwritten.

**User:** Let’s clarify a bit further. The overall merge flow for a commit, which git handles pretty well, is:

1. You always have to start from a clean working tree — you can’t merge while there’s something in the tree. So the merge will essentially create a new working tree which we might then commit.
2. You identify the commits that are base, yours, and theirs.
3. For each file changed from base in either yours or theirs, you run 3WM. If it finishes without conflicts, you update that file in the current working tree with the new output.
4. If it finished with conflicts, you trigger a UX (which defaults down, in the CLI, to opening a file with the default text editor that’s full of change markers) and the user can edit that particular file using the merging UX. The user might even go back and forth between a few files this way, but it’s a big PITA.
5. At any point the user can abandon the entire merge; you just dump the working tree and go back to the previous HEAD.
6. Once the user has edited and merged all the files, you have a working merge, which you can then commit. NB that this is just an ordinary working tree so you can easily do things like “run all the tests” before doing so.

I think this is exactly the flow we should be mimicking in Portal’s overall merge flow.

In an integration, the logic of attempting a 3WM or computing a diff (two different functions) would certainly belong in the common core of the integration; that is, every client is going to require the same logic for it. But the flow of calling that might be entirely within the integration’s own logic.

In terms of the flow above, we might compress that to:

1. The merge flow is initiated by the Portal *client* and has the same flow in each case, but must initiate from there, not the core. Common helpers may be in the core.
2. We start with a clean working tree and identify base, yours, and theirs. (Portal’s job) We list the set of files in all three and do a three-way zipper so that for each file that appears in any of the three, we know B/Y/T for that file.
3. For each file, Portal identifies the file type and calls the integration’s merge3() function — and NB it’s calling the *client-level* merge3 which is going to attempt a straight 3WM and failing that will open a UI. It might use some Rust helpers and/or common default functions. This function might return “abort everything” or “here’s the updated file” (which may well be deleted, that’s a valid outcome!)
4. The user has a chance to review the merge E2E, which often means popping open file pairs in the UI 3WM view — including files that had been automatically merged. The user can basically point at any of the files that are open in the current commit and ask to view and further edit them this way.
5. Ultimately, the user can then either accept the merge (i.e. commit and sync) or abandon it.

**Assistant:** This is right, and mimicking git's flow is the correct instinct — but there's one place Portal *must* deviate, and the reason is specific to Portal rather than to git.

## Why the merge can't happen in the project's working tree

Git can merge in place because git never commits without being told. **Portal autosaves.** A tick loop running against a tree full of conflict markers would commit them — and then push them. That's not a wrinkle, it's the worst failure the system can produce.

The alternatives are: suspend the tick loop while a merge is pending (which makes a conflict a *mode*, exactly what I2 exists to prevent), or merge somewhere else.

**So: a git worktree.** `git worktree add` gives a second working directory backed by the same object store. That satisfies everything in your flow while keeping the invariant:

- It's a real directory, so real tooling works — including "run all the tests before committing," which is a genuine capability the scratch-space design couldn't offer
- Abandon is `worktree remove`; the primary tree never knew anything happened
- The project stays usable during a pending merge — you can keep working
- Autosave keeps running against the primary tree, harmlessly
- Disk cost is only the checked-out files, since the object store is shared

I2 restates as **"the project's working tree is never left in a conflicted git state,"** which is the property that actually mattered.

The one real cost: your editor is pointed at the project, not the merge worktree. Mitigated because Portal opens the files for you — you don't navigate there — but it's worth knowing that "review the merge" means working in a different directory.

**Worth verifying:** libgit2 has worktree support, but T4/T5 didn't exercise it. That belongs in the next spike rather than being assumed.

## Two paths, not one

Your flow is the *human* merge. The common case has no human in it: the tick loop syncs, finds Diverged, and needs to reconcile two devices that didn't really conflict.

- **Automatic:** in-memory merge (libgit2's `merge_trees`, which T4/T5 verified returns an in-memory index touching nothing). All clean → commit, push, done. No worktree, no user.
- **Human:** any file conflicts → abandon the in-memory attempt, and either defer (this device can't resolve the type) or materialise a worktree and run your flow.

Both mechanisms earn their place rather than one replacing the other.

## What this deletes

**Deferral becomes "don't start."** §3 currently has conflicts detected, surfaced, and left pending with per-conflict state. Under this flow a phone simply doesn't begin a merge it can't finish; the project reads Diverged and the dashboard says where it can be resolved. Simpler, and no new state.

**The resumable `Resolution` scratch disappears.** §3 designed a half-finished `Resolution` keyed by the two commit ids, "machine-local scratch, safe to persist because it's derived." The worktree *is* that, natively — some files merged, some not, resumable by construction. A designed mechanism replaced by one git already provides.

## Reconciling "client-level `merge3`" with purity

We just fixed `merge3` as a pure Rust function so T6 can property-test it. Your client-level `merge3` — attempt, and open a UI on failure — is a different thing, and both should exist under different names:

- **`merge3`** (core, Rust, pure): attempt the structural merge, return clean bytes or conflict. This is what T6 tests.
- **`resolve`** (kit-provided, platform): call `merge3`; on conflict call `presentMerge`; return the result or abort.

The orchestration is identical for every integration, so **the kit provides `resolve` by default** and integrations rarely override. Purity survives, and you get the client-level entry point.

## Three gaps your flow catches

**Deletion isn't expressible.** `MergeOutcome` is `Clean(bytes) | Conflicted` — there's no way to say "the merged result is that this file is gone," which as you note is a perfectly valid outcome. Needs `Clean(Option<Vec<u8>>)` or an explicit variant.

**The zipper is over the path set, not the changed files.** §3 says "for each differing path," which glosses over add/delete and, more importantly, **rename** — T4 verified the library handles rename-plus-edit, but if Portal builds its own path set naively, a rename becomes delete-plus-add and loses the edit.

**`presentMerge` becomes user-invocable, not conflict-triggered.** Your step 4 lets the user open *any* file in the merge, including auto-merged ones, and edit it. That makes it a merge *editor* rather than a conflict resolver — it must work with no conflicts present. Broader than the current facet, and a better framing.

This is a substantial rewrite of §3 and it touches I2, D14, D16, and the deferral story. Let me write it up before we go further — I'd rather not stack FileSet on top of an unwritten merge model.

Written up. Two decisions, propagated across seven files.

**D44 — the human merge mirrors git's flow, in a worktree.** The load-bearing insight is the one you spotted: Portal can't merge in place *because Portal autosaves*. Git merges in place safely only because git never commits unless told. That's now recorded as the reason for the deviation, and it's in the threat model as a near-miss — a tick loop against a conflict-marked tree would commit and push markers, which is the worst failure the system can produce.

Two paths: automatic (in-memory, clean-or-abandon, no user) and human (worktree, git's flow, tests runnable before commit). Deferral simplifies to "don't start."

**D45 — merge and diff facets.** `presentMerge` returns bytes; `ConflictSet` demoted to optional rendering detail; labelled versions with the labels riding on filenames; `MergeOutcome::Clean(Option<...>)` so deletion is expressible; textual merge and diff as inheritable defaults.

**Three things deleted rather than designed:**
- The resumable `Resolution` scratch format — the worktree *is* that, natively.
- Per-conflict pending deferral state — replaced by not starting.
- The `resolve()` helper — you were right, it's an `if` in two different loops and naming it adds nothing.

**Two corrections that affect sequencing:**

The roster's "three nearly free" is now "two." Generic text needs a real `presentMerge` and `presentDiff` because neither OS ships a three-way text merge UI — and text conflicts are the most common kind, so it's the one whose absence would be felt daily. Added to the queue as a decision to make (markers-in-your-editor vs. external tool), not settled.

Added **T8**: libgit2 worktree support is unexercised by T4/T5. The whole human merge path rests on it, and if it fails, the fallback is in-place merging with the tick loop suspended — which is exactly what D44 rejected, so a failure reopens the decision. Worth knowing before building on it.

Also flagged `scaffold` as a genuine eighth facet — nothing currently knows how to create an empty `.todo` or `.notebook`, though both adoption and `create_project` need it.

FileSet next, whenever you're ready.

**Assistant:** Three good catches. The `Option` fix applies to `diff` too — diffing a file that was added or deleted is exactly the working-tree-vs-HEAD case.

All three done.

**Signatures.** All of `merge3`, `textual_merge3`, `diff` and `textual_diff` now take `Option` on every side. `diff` needed it for the same reason you gave for merge — working-tree-vs-HEAD on an added or deleted file is exactly the two-way case with one side absent. `textual_diff` added to the `Host` surface alongside `textual_merge3`.

**`create` rather than `scaffold`** — better name, and it's now a core facet returning `Vec<(String, Vec<u8>)>` rather than a single blob, because a bundle creates a directory plus usually a seed file.

**On `delete`:** I looked for what it would need to do beyond removing a path, and the only answer is *satellite state outside the type's own path*. Exactly one thing in the design has that — TODO's `todo-archive/` sits beside the list rather than inside it. So rather than adding a facet to support that, D46 records the constraint instead: **a type keeps all of its state inside its own path.** Hold that and `delete` is generic forever.

That also resolves something that was open independently: making `.todo` a transparent bundle containing its own archive answers "where does the archive live," and D30's dated-file-per-run scheme carries over unchanged. Added to the queue.

One consequence worth flagging: generic binary implements no `create`, and that's the first case where an *absent* facet changes what Portal offers the user — there's no "new PNG" — rather than just how it behaves. Probably fine, but it's a new kind of thing the facet matrix expresses.

**`integration-generic.md`** written, covering both types. Two things in it I'd draw your attention to:

The sniff's failure modes are now stated with a deliberate bias: **fail toward binary.** UTF-16, stray bytes, and unknown encodings land on binary, which costs a worse merge UI; the reverse error would let a textual merge corrupt a file. Better to state the direction than discover it.

And §6 makes the sequencing argument explicit: these two implement *zero* core facets between them, which proves the optionality claim — but "generic" isn't "free," and the cost is unevenly placed. Picking one of three files is a chooser and a launch; composing merged text needs a real editing surface. The text merge UI is the one to build early because it's the one that gets used daily.

**Assistant:** My first thought is that I got something wrong earlier and it changes the shape.

**A FileSet is references, not containment.** D4 says "an ordered set of *references* to files and URLs" — the project manifest points at `src/`, `paper.tex`, a URL, all of which live wherever they already live. But when we were working through bundles I wrote "a FileSet's PDFs are just PDFs and only its manifest is custom," treating it as a transparent bundle, and that's now in both `integration-api.md` and `integration-generic.md`.

If FileSet is a reference list, it's **a plain file, not a bundle at all**: no `contents` table, no recursion question, no `delete` concern. A bibliography that wants to *hold* its PDFs is just an ordinary directory plus a `.fileset` referencing them — which works without any bundle machinery. That's a correction I should make before we go further.

**Second: it needs no item IDs, and the contrast with TODO is instructive.** TODO needed stable IDs because an item's text isn't a stable key — items get reworded and reparented. A FileSet entry's *target* is its natural key: `src/` is `src/`. Editing an entry means changing its app override or label, not its identity. Changing the target itself is delete-plus-add, which for a handful of entries is untidy rather than lossy — the same standard D30 already accepted for archival.

So `merge3` is a union keyed by target, and the fiddly part of the TODO merge simply doesn't arise here.

**Third, and the one I'd actually want to resolve: does D4's Collection generalization pass its own test?**

`notes.md` records the D20 lesson — an abstraction covering two cases is validated only if it covers them *for the same reason*. Manifest and bibliography have the same *structure*, but I'm not sure they have the same *verb*. `open` on a project manifest launches everything, which is the point. `open` on a bibliography would launch forty PDFs, which is almost certainly not what anyone wants — a bibliography is for browsing, and its natural interaction is a widget.

Same data shape, different consumer. That's exactly the pattern that turned out to be wrong for `extract`.

The cheap fix is per-entry metadata that D4 already permits — a `launch` flag, default true — so a bibliography's entries simply aren't launched. But it's worth deciding whether that's a real answer or papering over two types that merely look alike.

One thing that falls out nicely if the generalization does hold: since instances are just multiple `.fileset` files (D37), **multiple launch configurations come free** — "open for writing" and "open for reviewing" as separate files, with `open project` picking one. That's a capability nobody designed; it's a consequence of the layout.

Where do you want to push — the bundle correction, or the manifest/bibliography question?

**User:** So let’s think a bit about what these various FileSets are used *for* and see if that clarifies what their structure ought to be.

One FileSet is “the set of things we open when we open a project.” Really, there might end up being multiple FileSets whose use case is “open these as a group;” e.g., a real estate project might have one bundle for photos and blueprints, one for contracts, and so on, and if I could double-click a bundle and have it pop up, that would be great. The docs in these would be combinations of URLs and docs that are part of the project.

Within FileSets like these, we may want some grouping; e.g., always open this URL in one window and all the other URLs in another window.

Another common use case is “the things I’m collecting while researching a topic.” Now again I’ve got a combination of URLs and docs, but it’s going to be particularly important to annotate each doc with a short blurb — some combo of auto- and human-generated — about what’s in it. The UX for this should show those things.

FileSet is going to be especially useful for searching, so one thing that we’ll probably implement in it (not necessarily in v1) is to really index the payload of each document, as well as these annotations, to make it straightforward to search for things.

Another common verb is going to be “snapshot” — URLs are fragile things, and it’s good to be able to push a button and convert either one or all of the URLs in a FileSet into local files.

Another one is “add” — just add a file, directory, or URL to a FileSet. That’s going to require some good UX.

This makes me think that the “meat” of a FileSet is a single file, but it’s going to end up requiring more: for example, when we add search capabilities, it’s going to need to put its index somewhere — unless we end up adding a search capability to Portal as a whole that supercedes that.

**Assistant:** These use cases resolve the question I raised, and they surface two things that aren't in any document.

## The manifest/bibliography worry dissolves

Your real-estate example has "double-click the contracts bundle and it pops up" — so a launch group isn't specific to the project manifest, and a research collection isn't structurally different. **One type, two gestures, both always available:** `open` launches the entries, the widget browses them. You use whichever fits. A forty-item reading list is simply one you don't press `open` on, and a per-entry flag covers mixed cases.

D4 survives its own test. The consumers differ, but they differ in *which verb the user invokes*, not in what the type has to be.

## Window grouping is new — but it isn't new machinery

"Open this URL in one window and the others in another" isn't per-entry metadata, it's a partition over entries. But notice what a group actually *is*: one `open` invocation carrying N targets. Passing several URLs to `NSWorkspace.open(_ urls:)` gets tabs in one window; passing them separately gets separate windows.

So grouping maps exactly onto D3's "every shell operation is invoke-something-over-N-targets." A `group` label on each entry, and `open(fileset)` becomes *partition by group, one `open` call per group*. No new concept — the grouping is literally the batching.

## Annotations force D4's "arbitrary metadata" to become real, and they split in two

D4 said entries carry "arbitrary per-entry metadata," which was fine as a placeholder and won't survive a format spec. Your use case names the field: a short blurb.

But it should be **two fields, not one** — auto-generated and human-written — because merge has to treat them differently. An auto-summary can be regenerated and overwritten freely; a human note must never be lost. Collapsing them into one field means a regeneration pass can silently destroy something a person typed, which is the same class of error D31 exists to prevent.

## Snapshot is the real test of whether FileSet is a bundle

This is where your "the meat is a single file, but it'll need more" bites, and I think the deciding question is: **if you delete a reading list, should the papers go with it?**

I'd say emphatically no. A snapshotted paper is a document you'd want to open, cite, and search independently — it isn't the FileSet's private state, it's project content that the FileSet now points at. Which means snapshotting doesn't push FileSet toward being a bundle: it produces an ordinary file in the project and updates the entry to reference it (or adds a `snapshot:` field beside the URL, keeping the original address as provenance).

So my earlier correction holds, but now for a tested reason rather than an assumed one: **FileSet is a plain file.**

**The search index is the one thing that genuinely would be FileSet's own state** — derived, disposable, meaningless outside it. And I don't think it should be FileSet's problem, because D20 already assigned that job elsewhere: search is deferred to v2 "as a genuinely generic facet with a genuinely generic consumer." Indexing document payloads is exactly that. If FileSet builds its own index, we've rebuilt search inside one integration — the same mistake `extract` made in the other direction.

That does create a dependency worth naming: **FileSet stays a plain file only if v2 search really is generic.** If search ends up per-integration, FileSet becomes a bundle and this gets revisited.

## One gap the snapshot verb exposes

**Nothing in the `Host` surface can fetch a URL.** Snapshotting needs the network, and the host services are all repo-local by construction.

I'd keep it that way and put the fetch shell-side: the platform loads the URL and hands bytes to a domain operation that files them and updates the entry. That keeps network capability out of the integration API — the same reasoning that keeps credentials inside `read_blob` — while the *logic* (naming, entry rewriting) stays in the shared core where it belongs.

Worth deciding deliberately, though, because "add `fetch_url` to Host" is the obvious shortcut and it would quietly hand every integration an outbound network capability.

Shall I work the entry schema and domain ops from here, or do you want to settle snapshot's mechanics first?

**Assistant:** FileSet is written up — `solution/integration-fileset.md` plus **D47**.

The design settled as: a plain `*.fileset` file, entries keyed by target with no IDs, one editable `note`, grouping that turns out to be nothing more than how `open` batches its invocations, and no `presentMerge` at all.

Three things I'd point at:

**The "single note field" reasoning ended up stronger than my original two-field proposal.** You're right that the separation isn't machine-vs-human, it's *durable* vs *regenerable* — and once the regenerable material (index data) is out of the file entirely, one field is correct and the merge rule is simple: never lose it.

**FileSet needs no merge UI**, which is a nice result. Union-by-target handles the common divergence with no human involved, and the rare same-field conflict falls to generic text's marker-based resolution through rung 2. That's the ladder paying for itself — a whole per-platform UI avoided because the type is text underneath.

**The snapshot boundary is worth remembering as a pattern.** Nothing in `Host` can fetch a URL, and the tempting fix — add `fetch_url` — would hand every integration an outbound network capability. Instead the widget fetches with the platform's own loader and passes bytes to `record_snapshot`, which files them and rewrites the entry. Capability stays on the platform, logic stays in the shared core. Same shape as keeping credentials inside `read_blob`.

I also fixed the bundle mischaracterization that had propagated into three documents ("a FileSet's PDFs are just PDFs") — the notebook's embedded images are the honest example of a transparent bundle's contents.

**One dependency worth tracking:** FileSet stays a plain file only while v2 search is genuinely generic. If search ends up per-integration, FileSet needs somewhere to put an index and becomes a bundle. That's recorded in both the decision and the document as the condition that would reopen it.

Remaining in the roster: **notebook** is the last integration undesigned to this depth. Its open questions are already named — enumerating days for the scroll-and-jump reading UX, renaming a day file, and how the capture surface interacts with append-union.

**User:** Sounds good. Let’s do notebook, and then do the design for search after that — even though we might not implement it immediately, it looks like its design may influence other factors.

On notebook, I’ve been thinking more about its proper structure and function, and I’ve found a couple of interesting things that make it more than just a chain of .md files. There are a few components that we really need inside this thing.

1. The notebook proper: This is just a sequence of entries. A single entry might get written to in little bits (e.g., I’m working with one hand and making notes with the other, all in a single session). Sequence is important to maintain, and we have “bookmarks” every day that are important to be able to jump to. If editing is happening on multiple devices simultaneously, there are two scenarios: Either I literally have two devices next to me and I’m moving back and forth while I do experiments, in which case it’s safe to assume reliable network on both, or I’m working fully asynchronously between them, in which case relative sequencing between the two is not very important. It’s important that the output of this is a file that’s easy to view and play with and so on, so .md with .tex inclusions is a good format.

2. Another thing that happens is sticking a named “bookmark” into the lab notebook — the equivalent of one of those little flag sticky notes — so I can jump back to an important item in the future. Sometimes these are short-lived (e.g., I’ve taken notes that I know I’m going to use when writing a talk in the future) and sometimes they’re long-lived (key insight that I know I’m going to keep referring back to).

NB that search will be very important in notebooks, too.

3. These lab notebooks sometimes need to include images and things like that as well. Need to basically be able to paste them in *in situ*. (Store as separate files but link from the .md so the renderer shows them properly?)

4. There are certain kinds of lists that get generated when writing lab notebooks that have a different dynamic. For example, “list of ideas for blog posts,” “list of things I need to take care of before we can do XYZ,” and so on. Unlike the rest of the notebook, this is a file that keeps getting edited. Structurally, these seem like a pile of small, separate, named files that we both link to from within lab notebooks, and keep a notebook-level FileSet of so that we can quickly jump to any one of them.

5. Usually, a project will have a single lab notebook, and there will also be a “master” lab notebook at the root project level. That said… borders tend to be fuzzy, and sometimes I’ll want to grab a chunk from one lab notebook and either copy or move it into another one. It should be easy to grab and transfer a range within a lab notebook (including one that spans multiple days) and all the files it references and put it in another one which may live in another project.

NB the choice of editor for these files feels like a very open question! There isn’t a great native .md editor on any of these platforms, I hate Word and Pages with a passion, and really what I *want* in life is to extend the .md format with a bunch of additional things (more powerful table forms, comments à la Google Docs, and more) but all of this is a separate task.

**Assistant:** This is rich, and item 4 exposes a bug in what's already written down.

## The contents pattern is wrong, and it matters for correctness

The notebook's declaration currently says `contents = { "*.md" = "notebook" }`. But your side-lists — "ideas for blog posts," "things to do before XYZ" — are files that **keep getting edited**, and if they live inside the bundle as `.md` they'd be typed as notebook and get **append-union merge**. That rule is licensed entirely by the notebook being append-only. Applied to a mutable list, it would silently duplicate edited lines instead of merging them.

So the contents pattern must claim only what's actually append-only: `"YYMMDD-*.md"`, not `"*.md"`. And the general rule worth writing down: **a bundle's contents table is a claim about semantics, not about file extensions** — anything mutable inside an append-only bundle must fall through to ordinary resolution.

That also answers what the side-lists *are*: whatever they naturally are. "Things to take care of before XYZ" is a `.todo`. "Ideas for blog posts" is probably generic text. The notebook doesn't need to own them — they just live inside its directory and resolve normally, which is exactly what a transparent bundle is for.

## Bookmarks have to be inline, and item 5 is what forces it

A named bookmark could live inline in the `.md` or in a side index. Item 5 decides it: if you can grab a range spanning several days and move it to another notebook in another project, **an index of file-and-offset pointers breaks on every move**, while an inline anchor travels with the text for free and merges as ordinary content.

Two requirements interacting to eliminate an option is usually a sign the surviving one is right.

The cost is that enumerating bookmarks ("show me my flags") becomes a scan. That's the same shape as search, which is next on the agenda — so v1 scans, and both get the index together later.

On short-lived vs long-lived: I'd resist making that a property. A bookmark is a sticky note; adding an expiry or a lifecycle field to a sticky note is the kind of machinery `notes.md` keeps warning about. Sorting the flag list most-recent-first probably gives you the whole benefit for nothing.

## Moving a range: copy first, delete second, never the reverse

This is the most demanding requirement and it's a **cross-project write**, which makes it the second real customer for `write_blob` after TODO's cross-project tick-off — good validation that D29 wasn't built for one case.

The important rule: there is no transaction spanning two repositories, so a move is *copy, verify, then delete*. Worst case is duplicated content, which is visible and fixable. The reverse ordering risks loss. Same instinct as "deletion loses to modification."

The pleasing part: **moving a range is just appends into the destination's day files.** Dated content lands in the destination's day files for those dates, which is exactly the append-union operation the notebook already performs and already merges correctly. No new merge semantics — a moved range is indistinguishable from having written it there.

Two things to name: referenced images have to travel with the range (they're inside the source bundle and must be copied into the destination's), and links from elsewhere in the source notebook into the moved range will break. The second is acceptable — they're markdown links — but it should be stated rather than discovered.

## Your multi-device analysis retroactively justifies D33

The architecture asserts that clock skew mis-ordering two blocks within a day is "cosmetic in an append-only log." That was an assertion. Your two scenarios turn it into an argument: side-by-side with reliable network means tiny divergence windows, and fully-async means relative sequencing between devices genuinely doesn't matter. **The ordering rule never has to be *right*, only deterministic** — which is precisely what D33 provides and all it provides.

Worth writing into the notebook design, because it's the justification for accepting a merge rule that is otherwise a bit lossy about intent.

## The editor question defuses itself for v1

The key separation: **the notebook's widget is the capture surface, not the editor.** Capture is "gesture, type, enter" (T7) and Portal owns it. Reading and editing delegate through `open` to whatever the user configures — which is exactly what the app override exists for, and why generic text implements no `open`.

So Portal ships no markdown editor, has no opinion about which one you use, and your extended-markdown ambition stays a genuinely separate project that would later arrive as a Tier 2 app or a widget. Nothing in this design needs to anticipate it.

## The one question I'd put back to you

Do the side-lists need a `.fileset`, or does the directory listing suffice? D27's discipline says a mechanism should justify itself against the generic behaviour — and "what files are in this notebook" is already answerable by `list()`. A FileSet adds ordering, grouping and notes. For four or five side-lists, is that worth a file, or is "show me the non-day files in this notebook, most recently edited first" the whole feature?

But the fact that FileSets (and maybe also TODOs!) would be nested inside a notebook raises the fact that Widgets, especially within the Mac UX, are things that might nest, and we should design their API for that.

If I’m viewing a notebook in a natively-designed UX, what I would expect is a left-hand navbar with two expandable sections — one showing the FileSet(s) (consider the need to manage sections here, archive old items, and so on), one showing the bookmarks, and one showing the dates, possibly with autogenerated summaries as hovertext. 

(Side note: This suggests that the notebook might actually use two other FileSets within it, one to track all the notebook files and one to track the bookmarks. The primary data would still be inline in the files, as you say, but the mechanism of a place to store titles, annotations, and so on may be really useful as a repeatable component)

The main panel would show an infinite scroll of the lab notebook, with an edit experience in it — honestly, I may want to do some implementing now. **Assistant:** Widget nesting is a real gap, and it has a clean answer.

## Nesting is `open` for views

The notebook widget can't import the FileSet integration — D39 forbids integrations depending on each other. So it must ask the host, exactly as it would to open a file:

```swift
func widget(for target: LocalTarget, presentation: Presentation) -> AnyView?
```

The host resolves the type and returns the right widget. Notebook never learns that FileSet exists; it says "render whatever is at this path."

That makes **widget hosting the in-process twin of `open`**, and it's the second recursive surface in the API. Worth naming the principle explicitly: **all cross-type dispatch goes through the host.** That's what keeps the integration dependency graph flat rather than becoming a web, and it's the property that makes D39's isolation hold as the roster grows.

Two things it needs:

**A presentation hint**, because your navbar FileSet and your main-panel FileSet are genuinely different — compact shows names, full shows notes and affords editing. That isn't a pure size question, so it's a small enum rather than something layout can infer. Keep it to two or three values; more is speculation.

**A depth bound.** A `.fileset` referencing its own notebook, or any cycle, would recurse forever. The host has to cap it — cheap to add now, mysterious to debug later.

## `Scope` needs to generalize, and D38 already made part of it redundant

Currently `home | project`. A nested FileSet widget is scoped to *a file*, which is neither. And since D38 made Home structurally unspecial — it's a project containing a registry file — `home` was already redundant:

```swift
enum Scope {
    case global                      // across all projects: TODO's today view, search
    case project(ProjectContext)     // Home is just one of these
    case file(FileContext)           // project + path — what a nested widget gets
}
```

That's a simplification D38 enabled and nobody noticed until nesting forced the issue.

## Pushback on the two internal FileSets

Your side note is onto something real — "a place to store titles and annotations" *is* a reusable component — but I'd apply it unevenly, using one test: **does the FileSet hold data that exists nowhere else, or does it mirror data that lives inline?**

**The bookmarks FileSet fails that test.** We just established bookmarks must be inline so they survive a cross-notebook move. A FileSet of them would be a *derived index* of those anchors — a second source of truth that goes stale whenever someone hand-edits a day file or moves a range. It's also the exact thing we deferred to generic search for FileSet's own indexing, and it would require extending `Target` to carry fragments, since a bookmark is a position rather than a file. Three reasons pointing the same way: enumerate by scan for v1, let search subsume it.

**The day-files FileSet probably fails it too**, for a different reason. Day files are *already* ordered by their date-prefixed names, so the FileSet adds only the autogenerated summaries — and those would go stale on a move, when the natural home for them is the day file's own **frontmatter**. YAML frontmatter is standard markdown, hidden by renderers, travels with the file, and can never disconnect from what it describes.

**The side-lists FileSet survives**, and it's the interesting one: those files have no natural ordering, benefit from grouping and annotation, and the annotation genuinely exists nowhere else. That's the case that earns a file.

So the general rule, which I'd want in the notes: **per-file metadata belongs in the file; a FileSet earns its place when it supplies ordering or grouping the filesystem doesn't already give you.**

## On wanting to implement

Not crazy at all, and there's an architectural argument for it beyond the itch: **widget nesting is the one part of this API the CLI cannot validate.** D43 puts the CLI first precisely because it proves the facet split is platform-neutral — but a terminal has no nested views, so the nesting contract stays untested until something renders it. The notebook viewer is the most demanding host we've designed (two or three nested widgets plus an infinite-scroll editor), which makes it the best validator.

They're not in tension. The CLI proves the core boundary; a notebook viewer proves the composition boundary. If the extended-markdown editor is where you want to spend energy anyway, starting from the Clarity Agent UX and building toward this is a reasonable path — it just wants the `widget(for:presentation:)` contract settled first, so what you build plugs in rather than needing retrofitting.

Want me to write up notebook plus the widget-nesting API now? The nesting piece touches D5, D26 and the kit definitions, so it's a decision in its own right rather than a detail of the notebook.

You would only get recursion if something called open() whenever it was opened, and yes, you should be careful then.

It’s possible that the notebook UX would have its own entirely custom rendering of the FileSet for the navbar. So long as the FileSet format is standardized, this works fine, and that may be quite useful.

I think this is revealing a deeper truth about these integrations: Some (most) of them are just opening ordinary applications for the view / edit experience, and Notebook is a good example of that. Some of them also provide widgets that *can* be rendered within another app, including Portal itself. Nearly all of them also provide facet implementations that can be directly called by Portal when needed. **Assistant:** Conceded on the depth bound — rendering shows a *link*, and only `open` recurses. The care belongs at `open`, where a FileSet containing itself would loop, and that's a much narrower thing to guard.

## Custom-rendering a foreign format: formats are public, code isn't

If the notebook renders a `.fileset` its own way in the navbar, it needs that file's *data* — but it can't call FileSet's domain ops, since D39 forbids integrations depending on each other.

The resolution is the distinction you drew: **the format is standardized.** D13 says "parsing is internal," and that's right about *facets* — no facet traffics in parsed models — but it shouldn't mean the format is secret. A native format with a written spec is a public contract, like TOML.

The clean mechanism is a **format library, not an integration**: a tiny `portal-format-fileset` crate that the FileSet integration and anyone else both depend on. That keeps D39's rule intact — integrations depend on libraries, never on each other — and avoids two parsers drifting apart. It also raises the stakes on the format specs, which stop being "how we serialize" and become an interface.

## The reframe is the important part

You've put your finger on something the current documents blur. An integration can supply **three independent things**:

1. **Facets Portal calls** — `merge3`, `diff`, `create`. Non-interactive, always in the core.
2. **An application** — the view/edit experience, which may be an ordinary app Portal merely launches.
3. **A widget** — a view renderable inside *another* app, including Portal's own panel.

The current `software-architecture.md` lumps `open`, `presentDiff`, `presentMerge` and `widget` together as "shell facets," as though they're one kind of thing. They aren't: (2) and (3) are independent, and most integrations have only one of them. Generic binary is all (2), delegated to the OS. TODO is mostly (3). Notebook is the one that wants both — a real editor *and* a capture widget.

## "Notebook could be an ordinary app" needs one rule to be safe

If a Portal-aware notebook app linked the core and ran its own tick loop, you'd have two processes autosaving the same repository — racing on quiescence detection and sync, with git's locking only protecting the index.

The rule that avoids it entirely, and which matches what you described: **a Portal-aware app edits files, not repositories.** It writes to the working tree like any editor; Portal's autosave notices and commits. It reaches into Portal only for the narrow things an editor can't do alone — presenting a merge, resolving a type. One tick loop per project, always Portal's.

That's worth stating as a rule because the tempting shortcut — "just link the core, it's right there" — breaks the autosave invariant in a way that would be very hard to diagnose.

## Widget scope becomes a declaration, and that's what the configurable dashboard needs

Your observation that widgets are naturally scoped — notebook and FileSet to a *file*, TODO to a *project or globally* — means scope isn't something a widget passively receives. It's something the widget **declares**, in the manifest:

```toml
widget_scope = ["file"]              # notebook, fileset
widget_scope = ["project", "global"] # todo
```

And that turns out to be exactly what a user-configurable Portal panel requires: to offer sensible placements, Portal has to know which widgets can render where. Two requirements meeting on one declaration is usually a sign it's the right shape.

**One caution on the configurable panel.** It's a classic scope trap — "user-configurable dashboard" can quietly become a layout engine. The minimum that delivers the value is *which widgets, in what order*, with scope filtering the choices. Not resizable grids. Worth writing the boundary down now, since this is precisely the kind of thing `architecture.md` already warns Portal would over-build.

I'd also make that config **machine-local**: Mac and Android form factors differ enough that a synced layout would be wrong on one of them, and it's cheap to lose.

---

That's three writeups now: the notebook design, widget hosting and scope declaration, and the integration-shape reframe (facets / application / widget as independent things). The third is the one that touches the most existing text — D3, D5, D26 and §5 all describe the shell side in the older lumped terms.

Want all three, or shall I do the reframe first since the other two sit on top of it?
