## What it says

An integration supplies **three independent things**, each optional:

| Aspect | What | Out-of-process? |
|---|---|---|
| **Operations** | `merge3`, `diff`, `create`, domain ops — the core calls these | No — it *is* the core |
| **Application** | the view/edit experience, invoked over targets: `open`, `presentDiff`, `presentMerge` | Yes, and usually is |
| **Widgets** | a view rendered inside another app's view tree, including Portal's panel | No |

The correction is sharper than a relabelling, and the v1 roster is the evidence: **integrations choose between the last two rather than accumulating both.** Generic binary and generic text are pure application, delegated to the OS. TODO, FileSet and Registry are widget-side — Registry has no application at all. Notebook is the only v1 type that genuinely wants both, and it's the case that exposed the conflation.

The underlying reason: an application is *invoked with targets and may be a separate process*; a widget is *rendered into a host's view tree and cannot be*. D3's "in-process versus out-of-process is a deployment detail" is true of one and false of the other, and asserting it across the whole shell surface is what hid the seam.

Three things fall out. **A Portal-aware app edits files, not repositories** — depends on the kit, never `portal-core`, so Portal's autosave commits as it would for any editor; linking the core would put two processes on one repo racing on quiescence with only git's index lock between them. **Widget scope becomes a manifest declaration**, which the widget's own contract and the configurable panel both need. And **`Scope` generalizes to `global | project | file`** — `home` had been redundant since D38 made Home an ordinary project, and nothing forced the issue until nesting needed `file`.

The manifest now groups by aspect rather than platform, so an absent `[widget]` table is a visible statement rather than a missing string. D26's checkability is untouched.

## Three things I did *not* decide

1. **What language a format library lives in.** "Formats are public, code isn't" resolves the notebook-renders-a-`.fileset` case in principle — a shared library, not an integration dependency — but a Rust crate doesn't help a Swift navbar. Either implement the spec per language (reintroducing the exact drift the library prevents) or let format crates ride the existing UniFFI surface. The aggregation machinery already exists, so I lean to the second, but it's a real choice.
2. **Whether a Portal-aware app gets host services at all.** Presenting a merge and resolving a type are what an editor can't do alone, and an out-of-process app has no injected `Host`. Simplest v1: it gets none. `Host` was shaped to be proxiable, so IPC is an addition rather than a reversal — but it turns the app boundary from a convention into an interface.
3. **Whether notebook's editor app is v1.** I wrote the proposal as: widget in v1 (it's T7's subject, on the critical path), editor app permitted as parallel work.

`solution/integration-notebook.md` is written. Two things came out of it that I didn't expect.

## The bug

The notebook's bundle declaration was `contents = { "*.md" = "notebook" }`. That's wrong, and it's a *silent* wrong. Your side lists — "blog post ideas," "things to do before XYZ" — are files that keep getting **edited**, and typed as notebook they'd receive append-union merge, a rule licensed entirely by the notebook being append-only. Applied to a mutable list it duplicates edited lines instead of merging them: nothing is lost, nothing errors, the file just quietly fills with near-duplicate paragraphs. Now `[0-9]{6}-*.md`, with the general rule stated: **a `contents` table is a claim about semantics, not about file extensions.**

That also settles what the side lists *are* — whatever they naturally are. A `.todo` is a `.todo`. The notebook doesn't own them; they just live in its directory and resolve normally, which is what a transparent bundle is for.

## D33 is unreachable

This one's more serious. D33 orders append-union by commit time, ties broken by commit object id. **D40's pure `merge3` can reach neither** — it gets three byte strings and no `Host`, deliberately, so T6's invariants stay tractable. D33 predates D40, its mechanism was written into `architecture.md` prose rather than into the facet signature, and the two have contradicted each other silently since.

The requirement is fine; only the means fails. Proposed replacement: order the two appended regions by **byte-lexicographic comparison of their contents**. Deterministic, side-independent, needs nothing beyond the three inputs, satisfies T6 structurally. What it costs is any correspondence to wall-clock order — and your own multi-device analysis is exactly what licenses paying it. Side-by-side means a divergence window of seconds; fully async means relative sequencing doesn't matter. **The ordering rule never has to be right, only deterministic.** That was an assertion in the architecture; now it's an argument. Flagged for ratification, not applied unilaterally — D33 is annotated, not rewritten.

The diagnostic worth keeping: *ask where a rule is enforced, not just where it's stated.* A rule enforced by a type signature can't silently expire. This is the fourth expired-rationale in this project and the first with a structural tell.

## The rest

Merge is now written as an algorithm rather than a description — prefix test, contiguous regions, blank-line separator, deletion loses to modification, and an honest fallback to `textual_merge3` when someone hand-edited the interior (degradation, not failure). Bookmarks are inline anchors, forced by the move-a-range requirement. Moving a range is copy-verify-delete, and pleasingly needs *no new merge semantics* — a moved range is just appends into the destination's day files, indistinguishable from having written it there.

Two small design calls I made rather than deferring:

- **`create` returns the empty `notebook.fileset`, not just the directory.** Git can't represent an empty directory, so a directory-only `create` commits nothing. The fileset is a real part of the structure, unlike a `.gitkeep`. A seed *day file* would be wrong — a dated entry for a day you wrote nothing is noise in an append-only log.
- **Notebook does implement `open`**, resolving to today's day file and handing that to the host. Absent `open` delegates to the OS, which is right for files and useless for directories — you'd get a Finder window. "Open my notebook" in practice means "put me where I'm writing."

Your #2 answer is recorded as settled: holding a `Host` proxy *is* the definition of Portal-aware, which makes D3's tier boundary testable rather than a matter of authorship — and needs no new interface design, because `Host`'s exclusions already mean a proxied app structurally cannot run a tick loop or push. D40 justified those exclusions on in-process safety grounds; they turn out to have been the out-of-process boundary all along. Only the transport is open.

This is good research, and three of its findings land on Portal core rather than on widgets. Recorded as **D49**, with `solution/widget-contract.md` as the full writeup.

## What it corrects in our design

**`WidgetFacet { render(host) -> View }` had a latent bug.** One member, conflating model and view construction — and SwiftUI destroys and recreates views on every re-layout and scroll recycle. A widget that opens its file in `makeBody` re-reads from disk constantly and loses edit state on reparenting. For the notebook capture widget that means losing text mid-typing, which is the one thing capture must never do. Now three members.

**`PortalAssembly` fills a gap I hadn't noticed.** D39 says attachment is compile-time — workspace member, package dependency — but never named *who does the wiring*. Without a dedicated module it lands in Portal core, which then imports every integration: exactly the inversion D39 exists to prevent. One thin module makes the invariant structural.

**The descriptor split is what the configurable panel actually needs.** D48 said placements are filtered by declared scope; you can't enumerate, configure or persist them if you must instantiate to ask. `WidgetDescriptor` is the manifest's `[widget] scope` in runtime form, and a test asserting the two agree extends D26's checkability to widgets.

## One correction back

`makeModel(url:)` is file-scoped, which covers notebook and FileSet but not TODO's project/global widget — it has no URL. It should take a `Scope` (D48's three-case enum), and the registry should key by `(descriptor.id, scope)`. That preserves your free property — two widgets on one target share a model — and extends it to the project and global cases, where two TODO widgets in different placements should likewise share state.

## The finding that matters most

**"Only Portal knows when a snapshot matters" breaks a premise in §2.3, and in the useful direction.**

The autosave loop rests on *"not written to disk is nothing changed"* — sound, because Portal can't help with a change inside a foreign app it can't see into. **In-process widgets are the exception:** `isDirty` makes unflushed edits visible. So the rule holds for apps Portal merely launched and fails for its own capture panel. Two consequences, neither optional: a project is quiescent only when the tree is stable *and* no widget in it is dirty; and the tick must `flush()` before committing. Otherwise Portal sees a quiet disk while you're typing into the notebook and takes a checkpoint missing exactly the work in progress.

That changes what a checkpoint *means*, which is why it's the load-bearing part of D49 rather than the platform craft.

**Your three-phase quiesce has three callers, not one.** `rewind` (§2.7), a merge landing back in the primary tree (D44), and a fast-forward on `sync` all rewrite the working tree. None of them said so. This is D44's hazard aimed at a different holder of state — Portal acts in the background, so anything holding state must be told before it acts. Third instance of that pattern now.

**The vnode gotcha is a Portal-core constraint, and we're defended by accident.** `notify_changed` was specified as an accelerator "for latency only and never for correctness," with `git status` as the authority. A silently-dead watcher therefore costs latency, not data. Had we made it authoritative — the tempting optimization, since `git status` is O(working tree) — this would have been silent data loss. I've upgraded that line from a caveat to a stated invariant, because the temptation is permanent.

## Two smaller additions

Per-model undo interacts with `rewind`: two undo mechanisms at two granularities, and `prepareForExternalChange()` should **invalidate** the stack, since undoing across an external rewrite is incoherent. And since v1 widgets are in-process and get the host by direct injection, **v1 ships no out-of-process Portal-aware app at all** — the host-proxy transport is gated on the notebook editor, not on v1. That's a real de-risking of the critical path.

Open, and listed in the doc: whether Compose justifies the same three-member split (if not, the kits diverge and D48's "same aspects on both shells" weakens), `PortalKit` vs `PortalIntegrationKit` naming before either reaches code, and what happens to a dirty widget on `close` — D6 aborts on a refused save dialog, and the widget analogue is unstated.

Next up would be the widget **nesting** contract, which D49 deliberately doesn't cover — and which the notebook viewer is the right thing to validate it against.

**Assistant:** Let me be precise about what was actually said, because there's a conflation worth separating first.

The `.todo` is **contained** in the notebook — a side list like "things to take care of before submission" lives inside the `.notebook` bundle and resolves as an ordinary `.todo` (notebook doc §2). That's a filesystem fact and it's settled. Whether the notebook's *viewer* renders it inline is a completely separate question, and only the second one is nesting. Your actual line was "FileSets (and maybe also TODOs!) would be nested inside a notebook" — file containment — and then the observation that this *raises* the view question.

So let me lay out the candidates honestly, because I think the inventory is thinner than it first looked.

**The notebook navbar has three sections, and two of them aren't nesting at all.** Bookmarks are inline anchors enumerated by scan — the notebook's own data, rendered by the notebook. Dates likewise. Neither involves another integration.

**The third section — the side-list FileSet — has a strong argument for not being nesting either**, and it's yours: "the notebook UX would have its own entirely custom rendering of the FileSet for the navbar." I think that's right, and for a sharper reason than convenience. A navbar section is a collapsible list of names. FileSet's widget is a browse/annotate/snapshot surface. Those aren't the same thing at two densities — they're different things. Handing FileSet's widget a "compact" hint and hoping a navbar section falls out is exactly the speculative generality we keep killing.

**Which leaves one credible case: a `.todo` rendered beside the notebook text.** And it's plausible — "things to take care of before submission" is precisely what you want visible while writing the day's entry, ticking items as you go. But it's plausible, not demonstrated, and it has a competitor: if the notebook viewer is an ordinary app, clicking that side list calls `host.open` and Portal dispatches it to whatever handles `.todo`. That's the existing recursion, not widget nesting.

Two more I generated and don't believe:

- *Dashboard rows showing each project's most urgent item.* Sounds like Registry-widget-hosts-TODO-widget, but D20 already settled that cross-project TODO logic is TODO's own — so it's the registry widget and the TODO widget side by side in Portal's panel. Depth 1.
- *FileSet rows showing previews of their entries.* Genuine nesting, except generic binary has no widget to nest — it's pure application. You'd have to build preview integrations first, which D27 says must justify themselves.

## The part that changes the calculus

I went in expecting nesting to be expensive and I don't think it is, because **D49 already dissolved the costs.** I was worried about child model lifetime, scope propagation, and cycles. But D49 has Portal owning all model lifetime in one flat registry keyed by `(descriptor.id, scope)` — so a nested instance isn't a child of anything. The notebook asks Portal for an instance at `file(before submission.todo)` and gets one from the same registry the panel uses. No parenting, no lifetime question, and scope doesn't propagate because it's per-instance. Cycles were already handled: render shows a link, only `open` recurses.

So "nesting" reduces to **one method on `PortalContext`: ask the registry for an instance at a scope and render it.** That's nearly free.

What *isn't* free is the thing I'd been calling the nesting contract: `widget(for:presentation:)` and the compact/full hint. That's the speculative part, and after this inventory it has no validated consumer — the one case that would have needed it (FileSet in a navbar) is better served by rendering the public format directly.

## So my read

Allow nesting, because given D49 it costs one method. **Don't build the presentation hint.** A nested widget renders as itself; if it doesn't fit, that's the layout's problem, not the widget's. That keeps the generalization to exactly where it deletes a concept and no further.

The trigger that would bring the hint back: two or more hosts wanting the *same* widget at genuinely different densities. One host wanting a different *shape* isn't that — that's the format-library path.

Two questions back to you. First, do you actually want the side-list TODO visible *beside* the notebook text, or is clicking through to it fine? That's the only thing standing between "nesting exists" and "nesting isn't needed." Second — and this may matter more — if the notebook viewer is an ordinary Mac app rather than a widget in Portal's panel, does it host widgets at all, or does it just render files? Because an ordinary app holding a `Host` proxy is Portal-aware by our new definition, but "may call domain operations" and "may instantiate other integrations' widgets" are separate grants, and I don't think we've said whether the second one comes with the first.

**Assistant:** Agreed, and I over-escalated it last turn — deferring nesting left me reaching for the next thing to call blocking, and this didn't earn it. It's a per-format judgment made when you get there.

One sharpening, though, because I think the axis isn't quite complexity. **CSV cuts against itself as an example:** it's the simplest format imaginable and the one where implementations most famously disagree — embedded newlines, quote doubling, BOMs, line endings. Simplicity and safety-to-reimplement aren't the same axis. What actually predicts drift is how many edge cases a format has that ordinary data doesn't exercise, and a small format can be dense with those. Our `.fileset` already has one waiting: notes are free text that may contain the delimiters, so escaping is unsettled (`integration-fileset.md` §7), and escaping is exactly where two parsers quietly diverge.

So the test I'd actually apply is **what the second reader does with the parse**, not how hard the parse is:

- **Read-only, for display** — reimplement freely, even a fiddly format. Drift shows a slightly wrong label. Cosmetic, visible, cheap to fix.
- **Writes, or feeds a merge** — shared library, however simple the format looks. Drift corrupts.

The second case is stronger than it sounds because of D31: any writer owes *round-trip untouched lines byte-for-byte*, and that obligation is far harder to honour in a second implementation than parsing is. Two parsers agreeing is achievable; two serializers agreeing byte-for-byte is where you get the "one edit normalized the whole file" bug.

And our one real case — the notebook navbar reading a `.fileset` — is read-only display. So it's reimplement-and-move-on, which lands where you did, and now we also know what would flip it.

The rule: when something's priority rises right after a neighbour is dropped, check whether anything about *it* actually changed.

What survives as architectural is small and already settled: integrations depend on libraries and never on each other (D39), and **the written spec — not any implementation — is the interface** (D48). That's what makes reimplementation safe when it's the right call, which is the whole reason the choice can stay flexible.

**Assistant:** ## #1 — Append-union ordering

First, a correction to how I framed this in the notebook writeup. I said "D40 makes D33 unreachable," which treated the signature as fixed and sent me looking for a workaround inside it. The honest question is **whether the signature is right**, and there are three options, not two.

**(a) Byte-lexicographic** — `first = min(A, B)` on the appended regions.
**(b) Hash comparison** — order by `SHA-256(A) < SHA-256(B)`.
**(c) Widen the signature** — pass `MergeContext { times, commit_ids }` alongside the three byte strings, and do what D33 said.

I under-rated (c) last time. It doesn't actually violate D40's *reasoning* — D40 was worried about I/O needing a mocked filesystem, side-independence being hard to guarantee, and reentrancy. A struct of scalars is none of those: it's trivially constructible in a property test, and side-independence survives as long as you order the *(content, time) pairs* rather than the argument slots, which is exactly what D33 specified.

### What kills (c) anyway, and it isn't purity

**D33 implicitly assumes one append per side per merge, and that's false in Portal specifically.**

Portal autosaves on quiescence. Two devices diverging over a day produce *many* implicit commits each. So region `A` is the concatenation of appends from N commits, and "the timestamp of A" is ill-defined — you'd have to pick the first, or the last, arbitrarily. Pick the last and a device that made one late append after a long pause sorts after a device that was writing continuously all day, even though most of its content is older.

So (c) doesn't deliver true chronological order. It delivers *order by an arbitrarily-chosen representative timestamp* — arbitrary, like the others, but now costing a widened universal signature that five of six integrations ignore, plus a `git log -1 -- <path>` per file per side to find the right commit at all.

With multi-commit divergence being the normal case rather than the exception, D33's benefit was mostly illusory. That's independent of the purity argument and I think it's the decisive point.

### So (a) or (b), and it's genuinely close

| | (a) lexicographic | (b) hash |
|---|---|---|
| Spec cost | one line | one line + pin the hash and byte order |
| Explicable | yes — "`-` sorts before `T`" | no — there's no answer to "why is this first?" |
| Bias | **systematic**: bullets (`-`, `#`, `>`) always sort before prose | none |

The bias is real and worth naming. If one device is where you jot bullets and the other is where you write things up, one device's content systematically comes first — for months, consistently enough to notice. Against that: both options are arbitrary, a reader can't distinguish bias from randomness in any single merge, and a pinned hash is one more thing two implementations must agree on exactly.

**I lean (a)**, on the grounds that when every option is arbitrary, the one that's explicable during debugging beats the one that's statistically tidier. But if the systematic bias bothers you more than the extra spec clause, (b) is defensible and I won't argue hard.

### Two details either way

**Identical regions duplicate, deliberately.** If both devices appended the same text, you get it twice. Deduping would be a content-aware judgment `merge3` shouldn't make, and two genuinely separate identical entries silently becoming one is worse than a visible duplicate you delete.

**"At least one blank line" between the regions**, not "insert a blank line" — otherwise a region already ending in whitespace accumulates it.

Which way on (a) vs (b)?

**Assistant:** Fair — I skipped the setup. Let me rebuild it concretely.

## The situation

A notebook is a directory of day files. Today's is `260811-titration curves.md`, and this morning it already had some content — call that **base**. It's synced; both your Mac and your phone have it.

Then you work from both. Mac appends:

> *Ran the titration again at 40°C. pH settled at 4.2.*  ← call this **X**

Phone appends:

> *NB buffer was two weeks old.*  ← call this **Y**

Portal autosaves on both, so both commit. Neither has seen the other. Now they sync, and git has two commits with a common ancestor, both modifying the same file.

## Why this needs a special merge at all

Both sides added text **at the same position** — the end of the file. Generic three-way text merge sees two different insertions at one point and calls it a conflict, writing `<<<<<<<` markers into your lab notebook. For the single most ordinary thing you can do with a notebook — jot something on each of two devices — that's unacceptable.

**Append-union** is the type-aware rule that avoids it (this is what D1 exists for). Notebook's `merge3` knows the file is append-only, so it recognizes `ours = base + X` and `theirs = base + Y`, and produces `base + X + Y`. Nothing lost, no conflict, no markers, no user involvement. That's the "union of both sides' appends."

## The ordering question, and why it isn't cosmetic

`base + X + Y`, or `base + Y + X`? Both keep everything. Reading it back, one has the buffer note before the titration result and the other after — mildly different, neither wrong.

**But it can't be a free choice, because of a race.** Suppose both devices are online and sync at nearly the same moment — which is exactly your scenario 1, two devices side by side, moving back and forth. Both fetch, both compute the merge locally. Mac pushes first and wins. The phone's push is rejected, so the phone must now merge *its* merge against the Mac's.

If both produced the same bytes, that second merge is a no-op. If they ordered differently, the phone now merges `base+X+Y` against `base+Y+X` — and append-union, seeing two divergent extensions of `base`, dutifully unions them into **`base + X+Y + Y+X`**. Your entries appear twice.

So the rule has to be **side-independent**: computing it as "Mac vs phone" must give byte-identical results to computing it as "phone vs Mac." That's the T6 invariant, and this is the concrete failure it prevents. Secondarily, a nondeterministic merge can't be property-tested at all, which is what T6 is for.

## Why it's awkward

The obvious ordering key is *when* each was written. But `merge3` is pure — D40 gives it three byte strings and nothing else: no clock, no commit ids, no repository. So the rule has to be computable **from the content itself**.

D33 said "order by commit time, ties broken by commit id." That was written before D40 made the function pure, and it also assumed one commit per side, which Portal's autosave makes false — a day of offline work is many commits, so there's no single timestamp for X anyway.

So: given only the bytes of X and Y, pick a deterministic winner. **(a)** whichever sorts first bytewise — simple and explicable, but systematically favours bullets over prose. **(b)** whichever has the smaller hash — unbiased, but needs a hash pinned in the spec and gives no human-readable reason.

Does that land? And does the tradeoff read differently now that the stakes are "duplicated entries in a sync race" rather than reading order?

**User:** Got it. I think that the answer (b) is the good one, because:

1. This situation needs to be rare — multiple concurrent file writes from different devices is the classic “demons blow out of your nose” situation, unless you’re implementing full PAXOS consistency on the underlying files. If we want to do that at some point we definitely can, but that’s not even v2, it’s v3. Supporting this use case — simultaneously dictating notes to two machines — is just weird.
2. And given that, anything that looks too predictable gives humans the wrong impression and may create a bad implicit API contract.

So we should just pick something and go with it, and the choice of answer really doesn’t matter.

**Assistant:** Agreed, and your point 2 is stronger than point 1 supports — worth separating them.

The *divergence* isn't rare at all: appending on two devices without syncing between is the ordinary offline case the whole design is built for. So the ordering rule fires routinely and **you will see its output often**. What's rare is the double-merge race, which is only when side-independence actually *pays*. Which means a rule that looks meaningful gets observed constantly, invites a theory about how the notebook orders things, and that theory is false — Hyrum's Law on a merge rule. Illegibility is doing real work here, not just avoiding a bias.

A small bonus falls out of (b): **hash equality implies content equality, so no tiebreak is needed at all.** D33's entire tiebreak problem — the thing T4 surfaced and D33 was written to fix — simply doesn't arise.
**#1 closed as D51.** D33's mechanism superseded, the tiebreak it existed to specify turns out to be unnecessary, and `merge3` stays pure — the first time D40's signature has been tested against a real demand for external context and held. I added the double-merge race to T6's required scenarios, since it's the only case where a side-dependent rule actually fails, and it fails silently by duplicating rather than by erroring.

---

## #3 — How a permanently deferred conflict gets unstuck

Here's the shape. The merge flow lands **one merge commit, only once every conflicted file is resolved** (§3). Separately, **deferral** exists: when a conflict arrives on a device that can't render it — a `.psd` conflict reaching your phone, say — Portal defers it to a device that can.

Those two combine badly. A project can sit **Diverged indefinitely**, waiting on a file that no *available* device can resolve. The risks section says this needs a visible answer on the dashboard rather than silence, but never says what the answer is.

**Why it's worse than it sounds.** Diverged isn't a passive state. While diverged, autosave can't push — so every subsequent edit piles up locally, on both devices, each accumulating unpushed work. And D30's "only act while Synced" gating means other machinery quietly stops too: TODO archival, clone reclamation. One unresolvable file freezes the whole project's sync, not just that file. The blast radius is much larger than the stuck file.

**Three candidate answers:**

**(a) Rung 4 as a user-invocable escape.** Keep-both is already the bottom of the ladder (D11). Offer it as an explicit button: "resolve this by keeping both versions." The project unsticks, nothing is lost, and the user cleans up later with full context. Honest, but requires the user to be present and to understand what they're accepting.

**(b) Automatic keep-both past a threshold.** Same mechanism, no user. Guarantees the project never freezes, at the cost of the system making a content decision unprompted — and the notes are full of reasons to distrust that.

**(c) Let the merge land partially** — commit the resolved files, leave the conflicted one pending. This is the structurally different option, and it's the one I'd want to examine hardest, because it attacks the coupling rather than the symptom: one unresolvable file wouldn't hold the other forty hostage.

My instinct is (a) plus something like (c), and that (b) is the wrong default — but (c) has a real cost I want to think through with you, because "partially merged" is a state git doesn't natively have and Portal would have to represent it.

Before I go further: **is deferral actually a real scenario for you?** It assumes a device that genuinely can't render a conflict. With two trusted devices and Android's roster deliberately thin on merge UIs, that's plausible — but if in practice you'd always just resolve on the Mac, this whole problem shrinks to "the Mac is the resolver" and the answer gets much cheaper.

**User:** I think “the Mac is the resolver” is likely to be the right way to deal with it in most cases. There are actually *two* user-invocable escapes that we can do — the other one, which I might try before Rung 4, is to run the version of the “generic binary” comparator that we can run on even the weakest CLI, namely “show the user the filenames and ask them to pick one.” Rung 4 would be “pick both” which is the sort of silly hack you can do by hand on git in a terminal but could even be an option in this “last-ditch picker.”

In fact, we can think of the “generic binary” picker as:

1. Show the names of the (up to) 3 relevant files.
2. Provide the ability to view them by invoking open() — which may or may not be possible on this device. (Some files aren’t openable on *any* device! Imagine some weird-ass binary format)
3. Provide the user the ability to choose between options: keep base, keep yours, keep theirs, save any one of those three as a new file and also keep one of them, and so on.

That’s both a “generic binary” handler and the perfect last-ditch way out for this case.

**Assistant:** That's a better decomposition than mine, and it dissolves more than the immediate problem. Let me check it against the ladder before recording.
That decomposition kills the problem rather than answering it, because of one thing you separated that I'd left fused: **viewing and choosing are independent, and only viewing can fail.** The picker is a menu over three opaque blobs — it needs no ability to render anything, so it runs everywhere, including the CLI, including a phone, including for a format nothing on earth can open.

Consequences, in rough order of how much they shrink the design:

**The permanently-stuck state stops being structurally possible.** Every device can resolve every conflict; some just can't *see* what they're resolving. So deferral becomes a **preference** — "I'd rather do this on the Mac where I can look at it" — not a necessity. The dashboard question shrinks from "how do we surface an unresolvable conflict" to "how do we surface a pending one," which the neglect-signal machinery already handles. My option (c), partial merge landing, is unnecessary — good, it was the expensive one.

**Rung 4 stops being a rung.** Keep-both isn't a separate terminal strategy, it's one option in the picker's menu — and a *better* one than the automatic version, because the user says which version is canonical and which becomes the sidecar, rather than the system guessing. The ladder goes from four rungs to three, and rung 3's caveat "requires a device that can render the type" disappears.

**The picker isn't generic binary's facet — it's kit code.** If it's the universal terminal for *every* type, it belongs where absent-`open`-delegates-to-the-OS already lives: written once in the kits, not owned by one integration. Generic binary just has nothing better to offer.

**And the runtime capability report stops being load-bearing.** §5.3 has shells reporting what the OS can open, so deferral can read the union. With a universal picker, "no device can resolve this" is never true — so that report degrades from a correctness input to a UI hint about whether viewing is worth offering. That's the second time this session an accelerator turned out to be safe precisely because it wasn't authoritative.

Two refinements I'd add:

**When viewing isn't available, the picker's default should be non-destructive.** Choosing blind between three blobs is a good way to discard the real content. So on a device that can't open the type, "keep one, save the others alongside" should be the pre-selected option, and picking a single version should be the deliberate act. The escape stays available; it just isn't the path of least resistance.

**The picker offers deferral as the recommended path, not as a competing mechanism.** "You can't view these here — resolve on your Mac, or choose blind." That keeps deferral's value without letting it strand you.

One implementation wrinkle worth naming: "save one as a new file *and* keep one" produces two files, and `presentMerge` returns a single `Data?` (D45). Rather than widening that signature, the picker writes the sidecar itself next to the targets it was handed — which, since the human merge runs in a worktree (D44), lands it in the worktree and commits with the merge. No new mechanism, but it needs saying.
**#3 closed as D52.** Four rungs became three, the picker moved from generic binary into the kits, the capability report demoted to a UI hint, and the permanently-stuck state stopped being structurally possible — all from separating viewing from choosing.

Now **#2, the machine-local store.** Let me check what's actually specified before framing it.
**#2 — the machine-local store.** Checking the existing text changed my view of this substantially, so let me put the reframe first.

## It's probably derivable, which changes the shape

D21's principle is *state is computed from git; only failure is remembered.* That principle was never applied to the clone path — and I think it applies cleanly. The missing piece is a way for a clone on disk to **say which project it is**, and git already has the right place for that:

```
git config portal.projectid <id>      # in .git/config — per-clone, never synced
```

That's exactly what `.git/config` is for: local, per-working-copy, moves with the directory if you move it, invisible to the remote. With it, `clone_path(id)` becomes *scan candidate roots, read each repo's config* — derivable, so the map is a **cache rather than a source of truth**, and losing it is harmless in the same way the sync journal is.

It also means this doesn't need `ProjectMetadata`, which is a small gift to item #5 — one fewer reason for that file to exist.

## The hazard is worse and narrower than I said

I called it "orphaning working copies with unpushed work." That's imprecise. Losing the map doesn't destroy anything — the clone is still on disk, commits intact. What it does is make Portal *act as though it isn't there*, and then **clone the project again**. Now you have two divergent working copies, one of which Portal is actively autosaving and the other invisible to it.

So the failure mode is silent double-cloning, which is the D36 class: not loss, but invisibility. And it has a defense independent of the store — **check for an existing clone before creating one**, which self-identifying clones make cheap.

## Two stores, not one and not three

The split already exists in the architecture and I'd keep it:

| Store | Contents | Owner | Losing it costs |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Core-local** | sync journal · clone-path cache · adopted-location hints | Rust core, path supplied by the shell | a rescan; projects read `Unknown` until the next tick |
| **Shell-local (Mac)** | Space↔project map | Mac shell | Portal re-discovers by cycling Spaces |

Merging them would violate a boundary that's already stated — the Space map is "not a Portal-level noun in the shared sense; it's Mac-shell-local state," and Android has no counterpart at all. But splitting the journal from the clone cache buys nothing: same lifetime, same platform, same harmless-to-lose property.

The pleasing part is that **all four contents become individually reconstructible**, so "losing it must be harmless" (D21) holds for the whole store rather than for the journal alone. That's the property that stops it becoming a second source of truth.

## One inconsistency this surfaces

D35 says "every display and **the clone path** use the name" — but names are freely renameable (that's the whole point of the name/id split). So a rename either has to move the directory or silently break the path. With self-identifying clones the answer is clean: **the directory name is cosmetic, the git config is authoritative.** Rename moves nothing, and a user hand-renaming a directory breaks nothing either. But D35 currently says otherwise and would need amending.

Two things I'd want your call on before I write it: does **git config as the identity marker** sit right with you, or would you rather the id live somewhere more visible? And do you want adopted repos to be *scanned for* at all, or should adoption simply record the path and accept that a lost cache means re-pointing Portal at them by hand?

## #2 from the ground up

**The default is that nothing is local.** Portal's model is that a project *is* a git repo: everything about it lives in the repo and syncs between devices, and even the list of what projects exist is itself a repo in Home. So machine-local, unsynced state is an exception that has to justify itself each time. Three things claim it.

**A. The Space↔project map** — Mac only, settled (D2). Portal's Mac trick is that each project gets a macOS Space: opening a project switches to its Space, and reading which Space is active is how capture knows what you're working on. Spaces are identified by ordinal position *on this Mac*. Space 3 here means nothing on another machine and nothing whatsoever on a phone, so syncing it would be actively wrong rather than merely wasteful. Uncontroversial.

**B. The sync journal** — settled (D21). Portal computes project status from git rather than storing it: clone presence from the filesystem, dirty/ahead/behind by comparing refs `.git` already holds. All derivable. The single exception is *why the last sync failed* — "push rejected, token expired" appears nowhere in git, so without a record a Blocked project would look fine after a restart and only re-reveal itself at the next failure. One small record per project, local because it's about this machine's attempts.

**C. The clone-path map** — undesigned, and this is the actual problem.

### The problem

A project is identified by a `ProjectId`: an 8-character opaque string (D35/D36). The registry — one synced file in Home (D34/D38) — records which projects exist, their human names, and their lifecycle state.

What the registry does not and cannot record is **where the working copy is on this machine.** That's inherently local: `/Users/zunger/src/portal` is meaningless to your phone.

And Portal needs that answer constantly — `ProjectStatus.clone: Present { path }`, `clone_path(project)` on the Host surface, and every integration operating in *engaged* mode, which works with the directory directly and is what the notebook's whole design assumes (D41).

So something must answer: **where is project `k3f9q7m2` on this machine?**

### Why it never got designed

When it was first assumed, the answer looked obvious — clone everything under one root, named by project. Two later decisions broke that, and one gap was never filled:

- **Adoption (D12)** lets you point Portal at a repo that already exists anywhere on disk. Paths are arbitrary, and this is a first-class flow rather than an edge case.
- **Clone reclamation (D41)** acquires clones on engagement and reclaims them when inactive, so the set of local clones changes over time — and on a phone, most projects have no clone at all.
- Nothing ever said where a **newly created** project's clone lands.

### What's at stake

If this is a stored table and it's lost or stale, Portal concludes project X has no clone and clones it fresh. Now two working copies exist: Portal autosaves one, and the other — possibly holding unpushed commits — is invisible to it. Nothing errors. That's the failure class D36 warns about: not destruction, but invisibility.

### So the question is

**Is this a stored table, or is it derivable the way everything else in D21 is?**

My proposal, in one line each: make each clone **self-identifying** via `git config portal.projectid` in `.git/config` — local per working copy, moves with the directory, never syncs — so the map becomes a **cache** that can be rebuilt by scanning, which makes losing it harmless. Keep **two** stores rather than three: core-local (journal + clone cache + adopted-location hints) and Mac-shell-local (the Space map), split along the core/shell line that already exists. And **check for an existing clone before creating one**, which self-identification makes cheap and which defends against the double-clone failure independently of the cache.

Does the problem land now? And does self-identification-plus-cache feel right, or would you rather this be an authoritative table that Portal simply must not lose?

**User:** Got it, and I like this — but I think the problem of stranding clones is likely to be a very rare one and not one we need to actively design around. If I think about normal user flows, what’s going to happen is:

* Existing directories get adopted: We create an associated git repo and push them, and work out of them.
* New projects get created: By default they’ll probably be created in a certain place, but there’s no reason this has to be fixed; we can present the user with a file picker for where to make it.

So this means that clones live in various directories on the local machine. (This is on Mac, btw; on Android the filesystem is basically hidden so this doesn’t show up at all and we can just name the directories after the clones) 

Now let’s say Portal gets blown up and loses all of its state; it needs to find existing clones. If the clones were all in “clean” states — synced — then those directories are uninteresting, in a way, and the worst consequence of losing one is having a stale set of files on local disk. The question is, do we want to build a mechanism by which Portal can automatically rebuild the table of those previous locations, or do we just say “too bad, clone everything afresh as you need it?”

This feels like a nicely deferrable problem: we can start with Portal just keeping track of its own state in the app config (although in a file in /Library, not inside the app itself, so that it doesn’t get lost when the app is updated!) and if the need to rebuild this automatically surfaces, that’s an easy feature to add later.

**Assistant:** Agreed, and your severity analysis is the part that makes it deferrable: **a Synced clone is a cache** — it holds nothing the remote doesn't — so losing track of one costs disk space, not data. The residual risk is narrow and I'd name it precisely rather than dismiss it: losing the table *while a project is non-Synced*. Autosave keeps the steady state Synced, but Blocked can persist quietly for days if a token expires. Rare × rare, so deferring is right.

One thing I'd add that costs a line and keeps the door open: **write `portal.projectid` into each clone's `.git/config` at clone/adopt time**, even though nothing reads it yet. Without it a future rebuild feature has to guess — matching by remote URL fails for local-only projects, breaks when a name changes, and can't distinguish two clones of the same project, which is the exact case you'd be rebuilding *for*. It also earns its keep immediately in one place: adoption can check the marker on a directory and say "this is already project X" without any scan.

Your Android point simplifies things further — app-private storage means the path is pure convention there, so the table is Mac-substantive and Android-trivial.

## #4 — the out-of-process cluster

Laying this one out from the ground up, since it's the largest remaining and its pieces were written across several decisions.

**The setting.** An integration has a Rust core half that Portal calls directly — `merge3`, `diff`, `create`, plus **domain operations**, which are the type-specific verbs a widget uses to manipulate content without knowing the file format: TODO's `set_status` and `list_items`, notebook's `append` and `list_days`. Everything else in the facet surface has a fixed signature shared by all types. Domain operations are the sole exception: **every integration declares its own**, with its own names and parameter types.

That's fine today because everything is in one process and one binary — the shells call domain ops through UniFFI-generated bindings, and UniFFI can express them as long as the types stay records, enums and sequences (D13).

**Where it becomes a problem.** Two things now want to cross a process boundary:

- **A Portal-aware app** — the notebook editor. We settled that holding a `Host` proxy *is* what makes an app Portal-aware (D48), so this is no longer optional; it's the definition. That needs a transport: XPC on macOS, a bound service on Android.
- **Integrations themselves**, eventually. `integration-api.md` §7 names the domain-operation shape as "the concrete obstacle to running out-of-process," and D3 flags integrations as where this design would over-build.

**The asymmetry that makes it hard.** Fixed-signature facets forward generically — a proxy can relay `merge3(base, ours, theirs) -> MergeOutcome` without knowing which type it's for, because the signature is the same for all of them. Domain operations can't be relayed that way: each integration has different verbs with different types, so a proxy either needs **generated per-integration marshalling**, or domain ops must become dynamically typed at the boundary (names and a serialized payload), which throws away the type safety that made them worth having.

**What's actually being decided.** I'd frame it as three questions, in dependency order:

1. **Does the notebook editor need domain operations at all**, or only the narrow things an editor can't do alone — present a merge, resolve a type? If the latter, the transport only ever carries fixed-signature calls and the hard problem doesn't arise for v1.
2. **What's the transport**, and is the proxy granted per-app or per-open?
3. **Does the domain-op shape need solving now**, or is it genuinely v2 — the thing you'd design when an integration first ships independently?

My instinct is that (1) is the crux and may collapse the whole cluster: a notebook editor that *edits files* (D48's rule) needs the filesystem and a merge presenter, not `append()`. But I'd rather hear how you picture that editor working before I argue for it, because if it wants to call `append` — say, to share the capture path with the panel widget — the answer changes completely.

**Assistant:** This deserves to be taken seriously rather than talked out of, so let me test the claim first and then say where I think it's under-counting.

## The 90% claim holds up better than I expected

The stated problem has two halves. The context-switching tax — and a notebook is *where context lives*. "What was I doing, what did I decide, what's next" is the expensive part of rebuilding context; relaunching some apps is the cheap part. If you can open a project's notebook and scroll to where you left off, with its FileSets holding the links and files, you've recovered most of what Portal's Space machinery was reconstructing more elaborately.

The other half is the graveyard problem, and there it's weaker but not absent: a notebook app needs an index view anyway — *which notebook do I open?* — and that index is structurally Portal's dashboard minus the lifecycle metadata. Last-touched dates give you a crude neglect signal for free.

So I'd believe 90% for the first half and maybe 50% for the second. Worth checking against your own sense, because the graveyard half was co-equal in the original framing and it's the half that a notebook doesn't naturally answer.

## It's a shell swap, not a restart

The framing "pause this and start that" undersells how much carries over. Sorting honestly:

**Carries almost entirely** — the notebook design, the FileSet design (and FileSets are explicitly on your list), the format specs, D31's parse-leniently/serialize-precisely/round-trip-losslessly, D51's merge ordering, the merge ladder and D52's picker.

**Carries if storage stays git-shaped** — the git filesystem, sync, autosave, rewind, the state machine, D53's local stores, and T5's validated cross-compilation and UniFFI work, which was expensive and is done.

**Doesn't carry** — `SpaceController`, the Space↔project map, the panel and hotkey, launch manifests, the `open`/`close`/`reopen` verbs, the registry and lifecycle.

And one that surprised me: **the integration architecture survives in a different role.** You want notebook plus embedded images plus side lists (`.todo`, generic text) plus FileSets — that's the v1 roster minus the registry. The notebook app *is* a host for typed content with per-type behaviour, which means D48's three aspects, D49's widget contract and D50's deferred nesting were designed for exactly this. We already called the notebook viewer "the most demanding host we've designed." It turns out to be the *only* host.

## Three things I'd want tested before committing

**Storage is the load-bearing one, and "no specific git requirement" may understate it.** Git wasn't there because it's git — it was meeting four requirements that don't go away: multi-device sync, offline operation, non-lossy conflict resolution, and history you can rewind. Drop it and you still need all four. Cloud file sync gives you the first two and answers the third with "conflicted copy of notebook.md" — precisely the outcome the type-aware merge design exists to prevent. It might genuinely be simpler than I think, since dated-file-per-day already makes most conflicts vanish and you've argued concurrent editing is weird. But that's the question to answer deliberately, not to inherit.

**Scope, honestly.** Portal's notebook integration was small *because* it shipped no editor — "Portal ships no markdown editor and has no opinion about which one you use" was a deliberate cost-avoidance. The app you're describing is an extended-markdown editor with embedded images, infinite scroll, bookmarks, search and side-panel management, on two platforms. That's plausibly bigger than all of Portal's core, and `notes.md` flags platform scaffolding — decades-old macOS experience, little Android — as the project's real risk. It concentrates exactly here. **The question that changes this by an order of magnitude: are you picturing native Mac and Android, or something web-based extending the Clarity Agent UX?** Those are different projects.

**"Extend markdown" versus "generic format so I can leave" are in tension.** Extended markdown is by construction not standard markdown. The resolution is presumably that extensions degrade gracefully — a reader without your tool sees something sensible rather than syntax noise — but that's a real constraint on every extension you design, and it's easier to adopt now than to retrofit.

## What I'd actually do

**Don't decide the pivot. Do the thing that informs it.**

Capturing the notebook requirements in depth is the right next move whether you pivot or not — if you stay with Portal, it's the notebook integration's spec plus the editor app D48 already permits; if you pivot, it's the project definition. It's non-destructive either way, and you can't honestly evaluate the pivot without it, because the scope question above turns entirely on what those requirements say.

So: run problem clarification on the notebook as its own thing. Fresh, in its own terms — what it's for, who it's for, what it must never do — rather than as a subtraction from Portal. Then look at the two side by side and the sequencing question will mostly answer itself.

Want me to start that? I'd set it up in its own protocol directory rather than inside Portal's, so it can stand alone if it turns out to deserve to.

**Assistant:** The subject idea is the load-bearing one, and I think it's bigger than you're giving it credit for.

## Subjects-as-tags deletes the hardest thing in the notebook design

In Portal the project is the container and the notebook lives inside it. You've inverted that: the notebook is the container, and subjects are labels on ranges within it. Three consequences fall out:

**Capture stops having a routing decision.** "Which notebook does this go in?" was real friction — Portal solved it with Space inference, an elaborate mechanism (`SpaceController`, T1–T3, the whole Accessibility-permission surface) whose main job was answering that question. If everything lands in one stream and gets tagged, the question doesn't arise.

**"Move a range between notebooks" evaporates.** That was the most demanding requirement in `integration-notebook.md` — a cross-project write, copy-verify-delete ordering, images that must travel, links that break. If ranges are tagged rather than filed, moving is *retagging*. A whole section of hard design disappears.

**Overlap becomes natural.** A range that's both "house deal" and "legal" is trivial for tags and impossible for containers. Your real work is full of that, and the container model was forcing a false choice.

When a model deletes the hardest requirement rather than solving it, that's usually the model being right.

## And it reprioritizes exactly one thing, hard

If subjects are tags, then **"open a subject" is a filtered view over the stream** — which means finding and filtering *is* the product, not a feature of it. In Portal, search was deferred to v2 (D20) as "a genuinely generic facet with a genuinely generic consumer." In this design it's v1-critical and probably the thing everything else is judged by. A single undifferentiated stream with weak filtering is *worse* than several small notebooks; with good filtering it's much better. That's the whole bet.

That's the reprioritization you were reaching for, I think, and it's a bigger swing than dropping the Space machinery.

Agreed on the rest: TODO-as-a-feature kills the only genuinely hard part of TODO (the cross-project unified view, which is what needed ambient mode and cross-project writes), and with one app over one folder the integration architecture mostly evaporates — which also retroactively confirms the domain-ops instinct from twenty minutes ago.

## On storage, three things worth separating before you commit

**"Strong consistency" and "keep working on the flight" are in direct tension.** If you write offline on the plane and your phone also wrote, you have divergence and you need a reconciliation rule — strong consistency only holds while connected. So the real requirement isn't strongly-consistent storage, it's **local-first with deterministic reconciliation**, and that's a meaningfully different thing to shop for.

**What's actually wrong with git here?** I want to ask rather than defend it, because the answer changes the search. If it's *conceptual weight*, the design already hid it — autosave meant you never typed a git command. If it's *the merge model*, that's fair but type-aware merge is built and validated. If it's *Android*, that's the strongest version: git on Android was the one unresolved hole from T5 (TLS trust), and an HTTP backend sidesteps it entirely. If it's *requiring GitHub*, that's real too.

**Does "web service" mean build one or use one?** Operating a server for a single-user tool is a serious ongoing cost — uptime, backups, auth, certs, deployment — and it converts a thing that works when GitHub is up into a thing that works when *you* are up. Using existing storage (Drive, Dropbox, S3, WebDAV) is a completely different commitment. Worth being explicit about which you meant.

And one tension to hold onto, because it'll shape everything: **plain markdown files and excellent automatic merge pull against each other.** CRDT-based sync (Automerge, Yjs) is purpose-built for exactly your scenario and merges without conflicts by construction — but then the CRDT document is the source of truth and plain files are an export, which cuts against "in case one day I decide this isn't the tool for me." Files-as-truth keeps your exit and makes merge your problem. That's a genuine fork and it deserves to be a decision rather than a default.

## The precedent

You said something I want to pull on hard:

> *I've had methods for doing that which worked in the past, but which don't really align with my work patterns today, but whenever they were present graveyards weren't an issue.*

That's a working solution that broke, which is worth more than any amount of speculation about what might work. **What were those methods, and what changed?**

I'd want both halves specifically. What was the actual practice — a physical notebook, a weekly review, an org-mode setup, index cards, something else? And what about your work patterns today makes it not fit: more concurrent threads? more devices? more mobile? less predictable schedule? work that's less bounded than it used to be?

The reason this matters more than it might seem: if the old method worked, the new tool's job may be to *restore a practice* rather than to invent one — and those are very different design targets. A tool that restores a practice can be much smaller, because the practice is doing the work and the tool just needs to stop getting in the way.

One pass at this, then I'll drop it. "A high-powered notebook app" names an artifact, and the destination might be one level up — something like *"I can hold and re-find my thinking across many concurrent threads, so nothing dies from being forgotten rather than from being decided against."*

Subjects replace projects, but projects held more than notes — drafts, contracts, photos, code, the ISO documents. You said you'll keep managing those in git by hand.

If that's right it's a strong boundary and worth writing down now, because "it should also manage my files" is exactly the pressure that would turn this back into Portal.

Written up as `goal/precedent.md`, and Portal is marked paused. Here's the observation.

## The filling page was a forced review, and nobody designed it

When a spread filled, you transcribed the live items forward. Anything not worth re-copying **died — silently, cheaply, and by a decision rather than by neglect.** The physical constraint of a finite page manufactured the discipline for free.

A digital list never fills. Nothing ever forces the re-read, items accumulate indefinitely, and things die by being forgotten instead of by being dropped. **That is the graveyard problem in miniature** — and it explains your report that graveyards weren't an issue whenever these methods were present. It wasn't the notebook. It was the page running out.

Finding a digital equivalent of the filling page may be the highest-leverage question in this project, and it's the one thing here that speaks directly to what Portal was originally for. It's also the sort of thing that's nearly impossible to retrofit, because it's a rhythm rather than a feature.

A smaller tension in the same family: **era 2 had no due dates and worked excellently.** Due dates and urgency are on the wish list as the thing that "covers the rest," but your best-performing system did without them, using a filling page and periodic transcription instead. Worth knowing which mechanism is actually doing the work before building both.

## And the requirement set the history supports is short

None of the three systems failed for lack of features. Era 3 had strictly more than era 2 and failed anyway. What the record actually says is:

1. **Available wherever thinking happens** — era 2's failure.
2. **A joy to read and write in, on every device** — era 3's failure, and the primary requirement rather than a polish item.
3. **Searchable** — era 1's gap, which grew rather than being wrong from the start, and which the subject model now makes load-bearing.

That's a sharper target than "high-powered notebook app," and it's a *smaller* one.

## For your document

You'll cover typography, layout and typing responsiveness — that's the core and you clearly have the most to say there. Four things you might not think to include, which I can't reconstruct:

- **What you've already tried and rejected** — Obsidian, Notion, Roam, org-mode, Bear, whatever — and specifically *why each one lost you*. Negative space is unusually informative here, and your era-3 diagnosis suggests you'll have precise reasons.
- **The Mac/phone split, concretely.** You said serious work on the Mac, browsing and adding and reviewing on the go. Pinning down what must work on the phone versus what's Mac-only bounds the hardest part of the build enormously.
- **Which era-2 structures you want back literally** versus reimagined. The dot/slash/X/strikethrough vocabulary is a validated design; the two-page spread is a constraint of paper that may or may not deserve a digital analogue.
- **What "a joy" means in failure**, not just success — the specific moments in Docs and M365 where it broke. Latency on keystroke? Reflow while typing? Scroll position lost? Those are testable requirements; "responsive" isn't.

Take your time. When you're back, share the document and I'll work it into `problem.md`, `requirements.md` and `stakeholders.md` properly — the precedent file is already the spine for it.

## Vim is the constraint that reorganizes everything

You buried it in a "note on writing experience," but 40 years of vim and active irritation at deviations is a **hard requirement on the desktop editing surface**, and it pulls directly against inline equations, tables and figures. Modal editing assumes a plain-text buffer; inline-rendered rich content assumes a document model. That's Q1, and I think it's the decision the whole desktop app hangs on.

There are three shapes, and only one satisfies both: plain text with a preview pane (proven, but two panes and a context switch), **plain text with inline widgets decorating a buffer that stays plain text underneath**, or a rich editor with vim keybindings bolted on — historically where this goes badly.

And Q1 drags Q4 with it. The evidence converging toward web technology is stronger than I expected: the vim-plus-inline-widgets shape is exactly what browser-based editor components are built for, TeX rendering is solved there, URL snapshotting is native, **your wanted browser extension is only possible there**, and one codebase covers Mac and Android — which matters a lot given slight Android experience and a single builder working evenings. The counter-argument is your own primary requirement: typing latency and font rendering are precisely where web stacks disappoint. So it's genuinely open, but one prototype answers both questions.

## Three smaller findings

**Selection is the core primitive.** Tag a range, branch a range, print a range — and a bookmark is a range of length zero. Four features, one gesture. That's worth knowing before designing any of them, because it means the selection model is the thing to get right.

**Explicit resync is a big relaxation, and you may not have noticed you granted it.** "Having to pause and sync from that device before I can continue is generally not a problem" means sync doesn't have to be invisible — which means conflicts can be *surfaced* rather than silently merged, and a large amount of merge machinery becomes optional. That materially widens the storage options in Q2.

**The writer and the reader are in direct tension**, and it's the sharpest one in the design. The writer will not pay any up-front cost — tagging, titling, filing — which is exactly why your tagging instinct is retrospective. Every organizational feature has to be *after the fact and cheap*, or it won't get used. I made those separate stakeholders because most design arguments here will be between them.

## One thing your document doesn't address

**Q3: what replaces the filling page?** The TODO section has a `nevermind` status but nothing that forces a review. Era 2's graveyard prevention was a side effect of paper running out — and it's the one requirement inherited from Portal's original purpose. Related: era 2 had *no due dates* and worked excellently, while due dates are now on the list as the thing that "covers the rest." Worth knowing whether the deadline or the forced review was doing the work, before building both.

Do the goal documents read right? If they do, the natural next step is the Q1/Q4 prototype — the smallest thing with a real vim mode, an inline equation, an inline image and a table, typed into at full speed and measured. That single spike would resolve more than any further discussion.

## The two-mode model is more tractable than my three shapes suggested

Raw-with-vim ↔ rendered-visual is a well-trodden shape — Obsidian's source/live-preview modes, Typora, Zettlr — and it rests on one foundation: a plain-text editor with a genuine vim mode that *also* supports inline widget decorations. CodeMirror 6 is precisely that combination, which is why Obsidian can offer both. Mode switching becomes a decoration toggle rather than two editors.

The useful decomposition: **rendered *reading* is nearly free; rendered *editing* is where the cost is**, because markup has to be managed on your behalf — cursor enters a node, markup reveals; cursor leaves, it re-renders. That works well for emphasis, links and headings, and badly for tables and equations, which want dedicated affordances. So it ships incrementally, expensive node types last.

## The Tauri finding cuts one way, and VSCode cuts back

Rendering being acceptable retires my main worry about R1.3. OS integration being painful matters *more here than for most apps*, because OS conversations aren't incidental to these requirements — printing a range, opening fileset documents en masse, pasting images in situ, drag-and-drop into filesets. A weak OS bridge taxes four requirements.

But **you wrote that document in VSCode with vim plugins at 130 WPM and found it acceptable** — and VSCode is Electron. That's an existence proof that typing latency, vim, and rendering quality are all satisfiable in a web-based stack, which means the Tauri result is evidence about *Tauri's OS bridge* specifically, not about web rendering generally.

So Q4 became four shapes rather than two: fully native, **native shell with a web view for the editor surface only** (which targets the Tauri failure directly), full web shell, or an extension to an existing editor. I priced that last one honestly rather than dismissing it — it gives you the editor, vim, performance and printing free, and costs the deepest requirement, "the UX is *mine*," plus all of mobile.

And one structural point that widens things: **desktop and mobile need not share a UI stack at all.** The contract between them is the file format, which R26 already requires be plain and durable. Two purpose-built apps sharing no code is legitimate here, and it lets each optimise for a genuinely different problem.

## Due dates resolved; the review stays open

The era-2 detail settles it — due dates failed **by scale**, not by absence. A drawn box works until there are too many boxes to take in at a glance. So the requirement isn't storing due dates, it's **ranking by them so the eye never has to scan**. R16 now says that, with the failure mode behind it.

And that makes the two mechanisms clearly different jobs rather than competitors: a deadline says *this has a date*, a review asks *is this still alive*. Both needed.

**One cheap thing to do now, since you want to experiment with review rhythms later:** record a **last-touched timestamp on every TODO item from day one**. No staleness mechanism can be built without it, and backfilling makes every existing item look equally fresh — you'd lose the very signal you wanted. It's the same marker-now-mechanism-later move that Portal used for clone identity.
